#                    └── the object BEFORE THE DOT becomes the first argument: self

Try it yourself, because both lines do exactly the same thing:

asha.deposit(10)                 # the normal way
BankAccount.deposit(asha, 10)    # what Python actually does underneath
print(asha.balance)              # increased by 20 in total
The one-line definition self = "the object before the dot".
When you call asha.deposit(50), self is asha. When you call ravi.deposit(50), self is ravi. There's only one deposit function, shared by all accounts. self tells it which account to work on.

self isn't a keyword or magic. It's just the conventional name for the first parameter. (You could call it me and it would still work. Please don't.)

That's why self.balance += amount changes only that account: self.balance means "the balance of this object".

Picture it in memory

   class BankAccount  (ONE copy, shared)
   ┌───────────────────────────────┐
   │ __init__  (function)          │
   │ deposit   (function)          │◄───────────┐ both objects know their class,
   │ withdraw  (function)          │◄──────┐    │ so they can find its methods
   └───────────────────────────────┘       │    │
                                           │    │
   asha ──► ┌────────────────────────┐     │    │
            │ owner   = "Asha"       │─────┘    │
            │ balance = 150          │          │
            └────────────────────────┘          │
   ravi ──► ┌────────────────────────┐          │
            │ owner   = "Ravi"       │──────────┘
            │ balance = 50           │
            └────────────────────────┘
   Data lives in each object. Methods live once, in the class.

Decode the error messages (you will see these)

ErrorWhat it really meansFix
TypeError: deposit() takes 1 positional argument but 2 were givenYou wrote def deposit(amount) and forgot self. Python passed the object plus your amount, which makes two arguments.def deposit(self, amount)
TypeError: deposit() missing 1 required positional argument: 'amount'You called BankAccount.deposit(50) on the class, so 50 became self and amount is missing.Call it on an object: asha.deposit(50)
NameError: name 'balance' is not definedInside a method you wrote balance instead of self.balance.Always use self. to reach the object's data
AttributeError: 'BankAccount' object has no attribute 'balence'A typo, or the attribute was never set in __init__.Check the spelling, and set every attribute in __init__
TypeError: BankAccount.__init__() missing 1 required positional argument: 'balance'You created the object without all of __init__'s arguments.BankAccount("Asha", 100), or give the parameter a default: balance=0

Class attributes vs instance attributes

Some data belongs to every account (the bank's name, the interest rate). Some belongs to one account (the balance). Put shared data on the class:

class BankAccount:
    bank_name = "PyBank"              # CLASS attribute: one copy, shared by all
    interest_rate = 0.04

    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        self.owner = owner            # INSTANCE attributes: one copy per object
        self.balance = balance

    def add_interest(self):
        self.balance += self.balance * self.interest_rate

a = BankAccount("Asha", 100)
b = BankAccount("Ravi")
print(a.bank_name, b.bank_name)       # PyBank PyBank  (both read the shared copy)
BankAccount.interest_rate = 0.05      # change it once → every account sees the new rate
How Python finds a.something 
a.balance    → look in the object a first   → found ✅ (it's a's own data)
a.bank_name  → look in the object a          → not there
             → look in the class BankAccount → found ✅ (shared)
a.deposit    → object: no → class: yes → a method; Python binds self = a
Object first, then the class. That single rule explains every surprise below.
Trap: a shared list 
class Team:
    members = []                      # ❌ class attribute: ONE list shared by every team!
    def add(self, name):
        self.members.append(name)

t1, t2 = Team(), Team()
t1.add("Asha")
print(t2.members)                     # ['Asha']  ← t2 never added anyone!

class Team:
    def __init__(self):
        self.members = []             # ✅ each team gets its own new list
Rule: anything that differs per object (especially lists and dicts) must be created in __init__ with self..
Interview check · 3 questions
Coding Write a Robot class that counts how many robots have been created in total, and gives each robot a serial number.
class Robot:
    count = 0                              # class attribute: shared by all robots
    def __init__(self, name):
        Robot.count += 1                   # update through the CLASS (self.count += 1 would create an instance attr!)
        self.serial = Robot.count          # instance attribute: unique per robot
        self.name = name

a, b = Robot("A"), Robot("B")
assert (a.serial, b.serial, Robot.count) == (1, 2, 2)
The trap: self.count += 1 reads the class value, then creates an instance attribute count on that robot, so the shared counter never changes.
Concept What does this print? class A: x = 1, then a = A(); a.x = 2; A.x = 3; print(a.x, A().x)
2 3. a.x = 2 creates an instance attribute on a, which hides the class one for a only. A.x = 3 changes the class attribute, and a new instance (with no own x) sees 3. Lookup: object first, then class.
Concept Can you add a new attribute to an object after it's created? Is that a good idea?
Yes: acc.nickname = "savings" works, because attributes live in the object's __dict__. Exceptions: classes with __slots__, and built-ins like int or list instances. It's usually not a good idea, because objects of the same class end up with different shapes, which causes surprise AttributeErrors and makes code hard to read. Define every attribute in __init__ (use None if it's unknown yet). Linters and type checkers flag attributes added outside __init__.

Making objects print nicely: __repr__

print(a)          # <__main__.BankAccount object at 0x7f…>  😕 not helpful

class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        self.owner, self.balance = owner, balance
    def __repr__(self):                                   # Python calls this when it needs to display the object
        return f"BankAccount(owner={self.owner!r}, balance={self.balance})"

print(BankAccount("Asha", 100))   # BankAccount(owner='Asha', balance=100)  😀

Always add a __repr__. It makes debugging much easier. (Methods with double underscores are called "dunder" methods. Chapter 10 covers them properly.)

Practice: try first, then open the answer

Write a Rectangle class with width and height, plus methods area() and perimeter().
class Rectangle:
    def __init__(self, width, height):
        self.width = width
        self.height = height
    def area(self):
        return self.width * self.height
    def perimeter(self):
        return 2 * (self.width + self.height)
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Rectangle({self.width}, {self.height})"

r = Rectangle(3, 4)
assert r.area() == 12 and r.perimeter() == 14
Notice: area() needs no arguments besides self, because the rectangle already knows its own width and height.
This code crashes. Find all three bugs.
class Student:
  def __init__(name):
    self.name = name
    grades = []
  def add(self, g): grades.append(g)
  1. __init__(name) is missing self. It should be __init__(self, name).
  2. grades = [] creates a local variable that vanishes when __init__ ends. It should be self.grades = [].
  3. grades.append(g) should be self.grades.append(g).
class Student:
    def __init__(self, name):
        self.name = name
        self.grades = []
    def add(self, g):
        self.grades.append(g)
    def average(self):
        return sum(self.grades) / len(self.grades) if self.grades else 0.0

s = Student("Asha"); s.add(80); s.add(90)
assert s.average() == 85
What does this print, and why? a = BankAccount("A", 10); b = a; b.deposit(5); print(a.balance)
15. b = a does not copy the account. It makes a second name pointing to the same object (see ch.02). Depositing through b changes the one object that both names refer to.
Without running it: what is self during ravi.withdraw(20)?
The object ravi: the object before the dot. Python runs BankAccount.withdraw(ravi, 20).

09 OOP from Zero II: The Four Pillars, Plainly

30 min · encapsulation, abstraction, inheritance, polymorphism, plus composition · each with "why" first
The four pillars in one breath Encapsulation: an object guards its own data. Abstraction: show the buttons, hide the wiring. Inheritance: a new class reuses an existing one and adds to it. Polymorphism: the same command, and each object responds in its own way.
Every interviewer asks about these. Below, each one starts with the problem it solves.

1. Encapsulation: the object guards its own data

Problem: in chapter 08, anyone could still write asha.balance = -1000000. The rules in withdraw are useless if people skip them.

Analogy: an ATM. You can't open the cash box and grab money. You go through the buttons, and the machine checks the rules.

Step 1: mark the data as "internal" with an underscore

class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        self.owner = owner
        self._balance = balance       # _ means "internal, please don't touch from outside"

Python doesn't enforce privacy. The underscore is a strong convention among developers: "this is internal, use the methods". (Two underscores, __balance, mangle the name to _BankAccount__balance, which makes it harder to reach by accident. It's still not truly private.)

Step 2: offer a safe way to read and change it: @property

class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        self.owner = owner
        self._balance = 0
        self.balance = balance        # goes through the setter below, so it's validated even here

    @property
    def balance(self):                # GETTER: runs when someone READS acc.balance
        return self._balance

    @balance.setter
    def balance(self, value):         # SETTER: runs when someone WRITES acc.balance = value
        if value < 0:
            raise ValueError("balance cannot be negative")
        self._balance = value

acc = BankAccount("Asha", 100)
print(acc.balance)        # 100      ← looks like a plain attribute, but the getter ran
acc.balance = 500         # OK       ← the setter ran and validated it
acc.balance = -5          # ValueError ← the rule is enforced no matter who tries
Why @property is brilliant Callers still write acc.balance, exactly as before, but now your code runs on every read and write. You can add validation later without changing any code that uses the class. That's why Python doesn't need Java-style getBalance() / setBalance(). Start with plain attributes and switch to a property when you need rules.
# read-only property: a getter with no setter
class Circle:
    def __init__(self, radius): self.radius = radius
    @property
    def area(self):                   # computed every time it's read
        return 3.14159 * self.radius ** 2

c = Circle(2)
c.area          # 12.56636 (no parentheses: it reads like data)
c.area = 5      # AttributeError: property 'area' has no setter
Interview check · 2 questions
Debug This property crashes with RecursionError. Why?
@property
def name(self): return self.name
Inside the getter, self.name calls the same property again, forever. The setter has the same bug if it does self.name = value. Store the real value under a different name, conventionally with an underscore: 
class User:
    def __init__(self, name): self.name = name     # goes through the setter
    @property
    def name(self): return self._name               # read the underscore attribute
    @name.setter
    def name(self, v):
        if not v: raise ValueError("empty name")
        self._name = v                              # write the underscore attribute

assert User("asha").name == "asha"
This is one of the most common OOP bugs in interviews.
Concept Can a child class access the parent's __secret attribute?
Not by that name. Inside class Parent, self.__secret is mangled to self._Parent__secret. Inside Child, writing self.__secret means self._Child__secret, which is a different name, so you get an AttributeError. That's the purpose of double underscores: preventing accidental name clashes between parent and child. To share with subclasses, use a single underscore _secret (a "protected" convention).

2. Abstraction: show the buttons, hide the wiring

Analogy: to drive a car you use the steering wheel, pedals, and gear lever. You don't need to know how fuel injection works. The car hides the complexity behind a simple interface.

In code, abstraction means callers use a few clear methods and don't care how they work inside. Python's tool for "every subclass must provide these buttons" is the abstract base class:

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod

class Notifier(ABC):                       # an abstract "idea" of a notifier: can't be created directly
    @abstractmethod
    def send(self, to: str, message: str) -> None:
        """Every notifier MUST implement this."""

class EmailNotifier(Notifier):
    def send(self, to, message):
        print(f"Email to {to}: {message}")   # the SMTP details would be hidden here

class SMSNotifier(Notifier):
    def send(self, to, message):
        print(f"SMS to {to}: {message}")

def alert_user(notifier: Notifier, user: str):   # only knows the "send" button
    notifier.send(user, "Your order shipped!")

alert_user(EmailNotifier(), "asha@x.com")
Notifier()          # TypeError: Can't instantiate abstract class Notifier ...

class BrokenNotifier(Notifier): pass
BrokenNotifier()    # TypeError: forgot to implement send → Python catches it immediately
Encapsulation vs abstraction (a favorite interview question) Encapsulation is about protecting data: bundle it with its rules and hide the internals (_balance + property).
Abstraction is about hiding complexity: expose a simple interface and hide how it works (send()).
They work together. Encapsulation is one way to achieve abstraction.

3. Inheritance: build a new class on top of an existing one

Problem: you need a savings account. It's a bank account in every way, plus interest. Copy-pasting BankAccount would duplicate the code and the bugs.

class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = balance
    def deposit(self, amount):
        self.balance += amount
    def describe(self):
        return f"{self.owner}: ₹{self.balance}"

class SavingsAccount(BankAccount):            # (BankAccount) = "inherit everything from BankAccount"
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0, rate=0.04):
        super().__init__(owner, balance)       # let the PARENT set up its part (owner, balance)
        self.rate = rate                       # then add the new part
    def add_interest(self):                    # NEW method only savings accounts have
        self.deposit(self.balance * self.rate) # reuses the inherited deposit()
    def describe(self):                        # OVERRIDE: replace the parent's version…
        return super().describe() + f" (savings @ {self.rate:.0%})"   # …while reusing it

s = SavingsAccount("Asha", 1000)
s.deposit(100)              # inherited: SavingsAccount never defined deposit
s.add_interest()
print(s.describe())         # Asha: ₹1144.0 (savings @ 4%)
isinstance(s, BankAccount)  # True: a savings account IS a bank account
How Python finds a method with inheritance 
s.deposit(100)
  1. look in the object s         → no
  2. look in SavingsAccount       → no
  3. look in BankAccount (parent) → found ✅ → run it with self = s
s.describe()
  1. object → no   2. SavingsAccount → found ✅ (the override wins; the search stops here)
Object, then its class, then the parent, then the grandparent, up to object. The first match wins. That is "inheritance" in one picture.
What super() means, simply super().__init__(owner, balance) means "run my parent's version of this method on me." The parent knows how to set up owner and balance, so let it, then add your own extras. Forgetting super().__init__ is a classic bug: the child object ends up missing the parent's attributes (an AttributeError later).

When NOT to use inheritance

Use it only if "X is a kind of Y" is true in every situation. A savings account is a bank account ✅. A car is not a kind of engine, because a car has an engine. That's the next idea.

Interview check · 2 questions
Coding Build Vehicle → Car → ElectricCar, where each level adds attributes using super().
class Vehicle:
    def __init__(self, brand, wheels):
        self.brand, self.wheels = brand, wheels
    def info(self): return f"{self.brand} ({self.wheels} wheels)"

class Car(Vehicle):
    def __init__(self, brand, seats=5):
        super().__init__(brand, wheels=4)          # Vehicle sets up brand and wheels
        self.seats = seats
    def info(self): return super().info() + f", {self.seats} seats"

class ElectricCar(Car):
    def __init__(self, brand, battery_kwh, seats=5):
        super().__init__(brand, seats)             # Car (and through it, Vehicle) sets up its part
        self.battery_kwh = battery_kwh
    def info(self): return super().info() + f", {self.battery_kwh} kWh"

e = ElectricCar("Tata", 40)
assert e.info() == "Tata (4 wheels), 5 seats, 40 kWh"
assert [c.__name__ for c in ElectricCar.__mro__] == ["ElectricCar", "Car", "Vehicle", "object"]
Concept What is the diamond problem, and how does Python solve it?
      A          D inherits from B and C, which both inherit from A.
     / \         If B and C both override hello(), which one does D get?
    B   C        And is A's __init__ run once or twice?
     \ /
      D
Python computes one linear search order, the MRO (method resolution order), using the C3 algorithm: D → B → C → A → object. A method is looked up in that order and the first match wins. With every class calling super(), each class's method runs exactly once. Check it with D.__mro__.

3b. Composition: "has a" (often better than inheritance)

class Engine:
    def __init__(self, hp): self.hp = hp
    def start(self): return f"{self.hp}hp engine started"

class Car:
    def __init__(self, brand, engine: Engine):
        self.brand = brand
        self.engine = engine                 # Car HAS an Engine (an object inside an object)
    def start(self):
        return f"{self.brand}: " + self.engine.start()   # delegate the work to the part

car = Car("Tata", Engine(110))
car.start()                                  # 'Tata: 110hp engine started'
car.engine = Engine(150)                     # swap the part at runtime, which inheritance can't do
Inheritance ("is a")Composition ("has a")
ExampleSavingsAccount is a BankAccountCar has an Engine
CouplingTight: parent changes ripple into childrenLoose: parts are independent
FlexibilityFixed when the class is writtenParts can be swapped at runtime, and fakes used in tests
Use whenA true subtype that shares real behaviorEverything else (the default choice)

The famous advice: "favor composition over inheritance."

4. Polymorphism: same command, different behavior

Analogy: a teacher says "speak!" to a dog, a cat, and a parrot. Each responds in its own way. The teacher doesn't need a separate instruction for each animal.

class Shape:
    def area(self):
        raise NotImplementedError

class Square(Shape):
    def __init__(self, side): self.side = side
    def area(self): return self.side ** 2

class Circle(Shape):
    def __init__(self, r): self.r = r
    def area(self): return 3.14159 * self.r ** 2

shapes = [Square(2), Circle(1), Square(3)]
total = sum(s.area() for s in shapes)    # ONE line works for every shape: no if/elif on type!
# Adding Triangle later? Write the class. This line never changes.
The anti-polymorphism smell 
if type(shape) == Square: a = shape.side ** 2
elif type(shape) == Circle: a = 3.14 * shape.r ** 2      # ❌ every new shape means editing this
When you see if type(x) == ... chains, the fix is usually polymorphism: move the behavior into each class.

Duck typing: Python's relaxed polymorphism

"If it walks like a duck and quacks like a duck, it's a duck." Python doesn't require a shared parent class. It only checks that the method exists when it is called:

class Duck:
    def speak(self): return "Quack"
class Robot:                       # NOT related to Duck at all
    def speak(self): return "Beep"

for thing in [Duck(), Robot()]:
    print(thing.speak())           # works: Python only cares that .speak() exists

Polymorphism is everywhere already: len() works on strings, lists, and dicts, and + adds numbers, joins strings, and concatenates lists. Each type answers the same command in its own way. Chapter 10 shows how to make your classes work with len(), +, and the rest.

Interview check · 1 question
Concept Duck typing vs inheritance-based polymorphism: pros and cons?
Duck typing (anything with a .read() works) is flexible, needs no shared base class, works with third-party classes, and is very Pythonic. But errors only appear at runtime and the contract isn't written down. Inheritance/ABC makes the contract explicit, Python enforces abstract methods, and you can share code. But it couples classes and requires inheriting. Best of both: a typing.Protocol documents the duck type, and a static type checker verifies it with no inheritance needed.

Practice

Add a @property called age to Person that rejects negative values and values over 150.
class Person:
    def __init__(self, name, age):
        self.name = name
        self.age = age               # validated by the setter
    @property
    def age(self):
        return self._age
    @age.setter
    def age(self, value):
        if not 0 <= value <= 150:
            raise ValueError("age must be between 0 and 150")
        self._age = value

p = Person("Asha", 30)
try:
    p.age = -1; raise AssertionError("should have failed")
except ValueError:
    pass
Create Employee(name, salary) with pay() returning the salary, and Manager(Employee) with an extra bonus that pay() includes. Reuse the parent's code.
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, salary):
        self.name, self.salary = name, salary
    def pay(self):
        return self.salary

class Manager(Employee):
    def __init__(self, name, salary, bonus):
        super().__init__(name, salary)        # parent sets name and salary
        self.bonus = bonus
    def pay(self):
        return super().pay() + self.bonus     # reuse the parent's pay instead of rewriting it

staff = [Employee("A", 100), Manager("B", 200, 50)]
assert [e.pay() for e in staff] == [100, 250]   # polymorphism: same call, different result
Inheritance or composition? (a) Dog / Animal (b) Order / OrderItem (c) Admin / User (d) Computer / CPU
(a) Inheritance: a dog is a kind of animal. (b) Composition: an order has items. (c) It depends. If a user can become an admin later, use composition (user.role = Role.ADMIN), because an object can't change its class. (d) Composition: a computer has a CPU.
Why does this fail? class Child(Parent): def __init__(self): self.y = 2 then Child().x raises AttributeError (Parent's __init__ sets self.x).
Child defined its own __init__, which replaces the parent's, and never called super().__init__(). So the parent's setup (self.x = ...) never ran. Fix: call super().__init__() first, then self.y = 2.

10 OOP from Zero III: Dunder Methods, classmethod & a Mini Project

30 min · make your objects work with Python's syntax · three kinds of methods · dataclasses · build a shopping cart

Dunder methods: teach your object Python's "language"

Why does len("hello") work? Because str has a method called __len__, and len(x) simply calls x.__len__(). Python's syntax is a set of hooks. Define the hook, and your object works with that syntax.

You write…Python calls…Define it to…
print(obj), str(obj)obj.__str__() (falls back to __repr__)Show a friendly text form
the REPL, lists, debuggersobj.__repr__()Show a developer-friendly form
len(obj)obj.__len__()Report the size
obj[key]obj.__getitem__(key)Support indexing
x in objobj.__contains__(x)Support membership
for x in objobj.__iter__()Make it loopable
a == ba.__eq__(b)Compare by value (by default, objects compare by identity)
a < b, sorted(objs)a.__lt__(b)Enable sorting
a + ba.__add__(b)Support addition
if obj:obj.__bool__() (else __len__)Decide truthiness
obj()obj.__call__()Make it callable like a function
with obj:__enter__ / __exit__Automatic setup and cleanup (ch.15)
class Playlist:
    def __init__(self, name):
        self.name = name
        self.songs = []
    def add(self, song): self.songs.append(song)
    def __len__(self): return len(self.songs)              # len(p)
    def __getitem__(self, i): return self.songs[i]          # p[0], p[-1], p[1:3]
    def __contains__(self, song): return song in self.songs # "x" in p
    def __iter__(self): return iter(self.songs)             # for s in p
    def __repr__(self): return f"Playlist({self.name!r}, {len(self)} songs)"

p = Playlist("Focus")
p.add("Lofi 1"); p.add("Rain")
len(p), p[0], "Rain" in p, list(p)    # (2, 'Lofi 1', True, ['Lofi 1', 'Rain'])
print(p)                              # Playlist('Focus', 2 songs)
Equality: __eq__ 
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x, y): self.x, self.y = x, y
    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Point):
            return NotImplemented          # "I don't know how to compare with that"; lets Python try other options
        return self.x == other.x and self.y == other.y
    def __hash__(self):                    # needed if you want Points in sets or as dict keys
        return hash((self.x, self.y))

Point(1, 2) == Point(1, 2)    # True (without __eq__ this would be False: two different objects)
When you define __eq__, also define __hash__ if the objects should go in sets or dicts (ch.02 explains the rule).
Interview check · 4 questions
Coding Make a Deck of 52 cards so that len, indexing, slicing, random.choice, in, and iteration all work, with only two dunder methods.
import random
from collections import namedtuple

Card = namedtuple("Card", ["rank", "suit"])

class Deck:
    ranks = [str(n) for n in range(2, 11)] + list("JQKA")
    suits = ["spades", "diamonds", "clubs", "hearts"]
    def __init__(self):
        self._cards = [Card(r, s) for s in self.suits for r in self.ranks]
    def __len__(self): return len(self._cards)
    def __getitem__(self, i): return self._cards[i]       # ints AND slices pass straight through

d = Deck()
assert len(d) == 52 and d[0] == Card("2", "spades") and d[-1] == Card("A", "hearts")
assert len(d[:3]) == 3 and Card("Q", "hearts") in d    # `in` falls back to __getitem__ iteration
random.choice(d)                                        # works: uses len + indexing
for card in d: pass                                     # iteration works without __iter__!
This classic (from Fluent Python) shows how much of Python's syntax two dunders unlock.
Coding Make Employee objects sortable by salary (high to low), then by name, using @total_ordering.
from functools import total_ordering

@total_ordering
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, salary): self.name, self.salary = name, salary
    def _key(self): return (-self.salary, self.name)
    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Employee): return NotImplemented
        return self._key() == other._key()
    def __lt__(self, other):                       # total_ordering derives <=, >, >= from __lt__ + __eq__
        if not isinstance(other, Employee): return NotImplemented
        return self._key() < other._key()
    def __repr__(self): return self.name

staff = [Employee("bo", 100), Employee("al", 200), Employee("cy", 100)]
assert [e.name for e in sorted(staff)] == ["al", "bo", "cy"] and (Employee("x", 5) >= Employee("y", 5)) is False
# careful: without the parentheses, `a >= b is False` is a CHAINED comparison: (a >= b) and (b is False)
If you only need one sort order, sorted(staff, key=lambda e: (-e.salary, e.name)) is simpler. Define the dunders when the ordering is natural to the type.
Concept A class defines both __str__ and __repr__. What does print([obj]) use?
__repr__. print(obj) uses __str__, but containers always display their items with repr. That's why a list of objects without a __repr__ prints as [<…object at 0x…>] even when __str__ exists. f-strings use __format__, which falls back to __str__, and f"{obj!r}" forces repr.
Coding Use __call__ to build a function-like object that remembers how many times it was called.
class CallCounter:
    def __init__(self, func):
        self.func, self.calls = func, 0
    def __call__(self, *args, **kwargs):        # makes instances callable: obj(...)
        self.calls += 1
        return self.func(*args, **kwargs)

square = CallCounter(lambda x: x * x)
square(3); square(4)
assert square(5) == 25 and square.calls == 3 and callable(square)
Callable objects are useful when a "function" needs state or configuration: model objects in ML libraries, strategies, and decorators implemented as classes.

The three kinds of methods

Think of a pizza shop:

class Pizza:
    def __init__(self, toppings):
        self.toppings = toppings

    def describe(self):                         # INSTANCE method: needs a specific pizza (self)
        return "Pizza with " + ", ".join(self.toppings)

    @classmethod
    def margherita(cls):                        # CLASS method: gets the class (cls) and builds pizzas
        return cls(["tomato", "mozzarella"])    # an "alternative constructor"

    @staticmethod
    def is_valid_topping(t):                    # STATIC method: needs neither; a helper that lives here
        return t not in {"pineapple"}           # (controversial 🍍)

p = Pizza.margherita()          # create without listing the toppings yourself
p.describe()                    # 'Pizza with tomato, mozzarella'
Pizza.is_valid_topping("olive") # True
KindFirst parameterCan useTypical use
Instance methodself (the object)The object's data plus the class99% of methods
@classmethodcls (the class)The class onlyAlternative constructors: from_json, from_string, margherita
@staticmethodnothingOnly its argumentsA utility that logically belongs to the class

Why cls(...) instead of Pizza(...)? If someone writes class VeganPizza(Pizza), then VeganPizza.margherita() builds a VeganPizza, because cls is whichever class the method was called on.

@dataclass: skip the boring code

Many classes just hold data, and writing __init__, __repr__, and __eq__ by hand is tedious. @dataclass writes them for you from the field list:

# Without dataclass: 9 lines of boilerplate
class Product:
    def __init__(self, name, price, qty=1):
        self.name, self.price, self.qty = name, price, qty
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Product(name={self.name!r}, price={self.price!r}, qty={self.qty!r})"
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return isinstance(other, Product) and (self.name, self.price, self.qty) == (other.name, other.price, other.qty)

# With dataclass: same behavior, 4 lines
from dataclasses import dataclass, field

@dataclass
class Product:
    name: str
    price: float
    qty: int = 1                         # a default value
    tags: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)   # a mutable default needs default_factory (see the trap in ch.08)

Product("Pen", 10.0) == Product("Pen", 10.0)    # True: __eq__ was generated
print(Product("Pen", 10.0))                     # Product(name='Pen', price=10.0, qty=1, tags=[])

@dataclass(frozen=True)                  # frozen = immutable (and hashable): great for values like coordinates
class Point:
    x: int
    y: int

You can still add methods, and properties too, to a dataclass. It's a normal class with the boilerplate pre-written.

Interview check · 1 question
Debug @dataclass raises TypeError: non-default argument 'b' follows default argument. Why, and what's the fix?
The generated __init__ lists the fields in order, and Python forbids a required parameter after an optional one: def __init__(self, a=1, b) is invalid. This often happens with inheritance, when a parent has a default and the child adds a required field. Fixes: reorder the fields, give b a default, or use @dataclass(kw_only=True) (3.10+) or a _: KW_ONLY marker, so the fields become keyword-only and order no longer matters.

Mini project: a shopping cart that uses everything

Read it slowly. Every comment points back to an idea from these three chapters.

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
from dataclasses import dataclass

# --- a data class (ch.10) ---
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Product:
    name: str
    price: float

# --- abstraction + polymorphism (ch.09) ---
class Discount(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def apply(self, total: float) -> float: ...

class NoDiscount(Discount):
    def apply(self, total): return total

class PercentOff(Discount):
    def __init__(self, percent: float):
        if not 0 <= percent <= 100:                 # validate in __init__ (encapsulation)
            raise ValueError("percent must be 0..100")
        self.percent = percent
    def apply(self, total): return round(total * (1 - self.percent / 100), 2)

class FlatOff(Discount):
    def __init__(self, amount: float): self.amount = amount
    def apply(self, total): return max(0.0, total - self.amount)

# --- the main class: encapsulation + dunders (ch.08–10) ---
class Cart:
    def __init__(self, owner: str):
        self.owner = owner
        self._items: dict[Product, int] = {}        # internal: product → quantity (frozen Product is hashable)
        self._discount: Discount = NoDiscount()     # composition: a cart HAS a discount

    def add(self, product: Product, qty: int = 1) -> "Cart":
        if qty <= 0:
            raise ValueError("qty must be positive")
        self._items[product] = self._items.get(product, 0) + qty
        return self                                  # returning self allows chaining: cart.add(a).add(b)

    def remove(self, product: Product) -> None:
        self._items.pop(product, None)

    @property
    def discount(self) -> Discount:
        return self._discount

    @discount.setter
    def discount(self, d: Discount) -> None:
        if not isinstance(d, Discount):
            raise TypeError("must be a Discount")
        self._discount = d

    @property
    def subtotal(self) -> float:                     # computed, read-only
        return sum(p.price * q for p, q in self._items.items())

    @property
    def total(self) -> float:
        return self._discount.apply(self.subtotal)  # polymorphism: works for ANY Discount

    # dunders: make the cart feel like a built-in container
    def __len__(self): return sum(self._items.values())          # total item count
    def __contains__(self, product): return product in self._items
    def __iter__(self): return iter(self._items.items())
    def __bool__(self): return bool(self._items)
    def __repr__(self): return f"Cart({self.owner!r}, {len(self)} items, total={self.total})"

# --- try it ---
pen, book = Product("Pen", 10.0), Product("Book", 250.0)
cart = Cart("Asha").add(pen, 3).add(book)
print(len(cart), pen in cart, cart.subtotal)   # 4 True 280.0
cart.discount = PercentOff(10)
print(cart.total)                              # 252.0
cart.discount = FlatOff(300)
print(cart.total)                              # 0.0
for product, qty in cart:
    print(f"{qty} × {product.name}")
print(cart)                                    # Cart('Asha', 4 items, total=0.0)
Where each idea appears Class/object/self: everywhere. Encapsulation: _items, the discount setter checks the type, add rejects bad quantities. Abstraction: Discount ABC. Polymorphism: total works for any discount. Composition: the cart has a discount and has products. Dunders: len, in, for, if cart, print. Dataclass: Product.

One-page OOP cheat sheet

class Name(Parent):                 # inherit (optional)
    shared = 0                      # class attribute: one copy for all
    def __init__(self, a):          # fill in a new object
        super().__init__()          # let the parent set up its part (when inheriting)
        self.a = a                  # instance attribute: per object
        self._secret = 0            # internal by convention
    def method(self, x):            # self = the object before the dot
        return self.a + x
    @property                       # read like data, runs code
    def value(self): return self._secret
    @value.setter                   # validate on write
    def value(self, v): self._secret = v
    @classmethod                    # cls = the class → alternative constructors
    def create(cls): return cls(1)
    @staticmethod                   # no self/cls → helper
    def helper(x): return x * 2
    def __repr__(self): return f"Name({self.a!r})"   # print-friendly

Lookup order:  object → its class → parent → … → object.  First match wins.
is-a → inheritance.   has-a → composition (the default).   Same call, different behavior → polymorphism.

Final self-test

Is __init__ a constructor?
Informally people call it that, but strictly no. __new__ creates (constructs) the object, and __init__ initializes the already-created object. You almost never write __new__. The details are in ch.11.
What's the difference between a.x when x is an instance attribute and when it's a class attribute?
Python checks the object first, then the class. An instance attribute belongs to that one object. A class attribute is shared. Assigning a.x = 5 always creates or changes an instance attribute on a, which then hides the class one for a only.
Why does print(obj) show <__main__.X object at 0x…>, and how do you fix it?
That's the default __repr__ inherited from object. Define __repr__ (and optionally __str__) in your class, or use @dataclass, which generates one.
Make Stack work with len(), if stack:, and print(), with push, pop, and peek.
class Stack:
    def __init__(self): self._items = []
    def push(self, x): self._items.append(x)
    def pop(self):
        if not self._items: raise IndexError("pop from empty stack")
        return self._items.pop()
    def peek(self):
        if not self._items: raise IndexError("peek at empty stack")
        return self._items[-1]
    def __len__(self): return len(self._items)
    def __bool__(self): return bool(self._items)
    def __repr__(self): return f"Stack({self._items})"

s = Stack(); s.push(1); s.push(2)
assert len(s) == 2 and s.peek() == 2 and s.pop() == 2 and s
s.pop(); assert not s
When would you pick @classmethod over @staticmethod?
When the method needs the class itself, usually to create objects (cls(...)) in a way that also works correctly for subclasses, or to read or modify class-level data. A staticmethod is for helpers that need neither the object nor the class.

Where to go next

  1. Rebuild the shopping cart from memory (30 min). If you can, you understand OOP.
  2. Read ch.11 OOP & the Data Model. It now reads as "how all of this works inside".
  3. Then LLD from Zero (ch.24–26), which is OOP applied to design problems.

More interview questions

Concept What is self? Why is it explicit in Python?
self is the instance the method was called on. obj.m(x) becomes type(obj).m(obj, x). It's explicit because methods are ordinary functions stored on the class, and the binding happens through the descriptor protocol (ch.12). Being explicit also makes the difference between local variables and attributes obvious (x vs self.x) and lets you call methods as plain functions (Class.m(obj, x)), which helps with map and callbacks. "Explicit is better than implicit."
Concept Does Python support private members? How do _x and __x differ?
There is no enforced privacy. _x is a convention meaning "internal; not part of the public API". __x inside a class triggers name mangling to _ClassName__x, which prevents accidental clashes with subclasses' attributes. It's still reachable. The Python philosophy: "we're all consenting adults". Use properties for controlled access.
Coding Design a class hierarchy for shapes (Circle, Rectangle, Square) with area and perimeter. Should Square inherit from Rectangle?
from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
import math

class Shape(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def area(self) -> float: ...
    @abstractmethod
    def perimeter(self) -> float: ...
    def __repr__(self): return f"{type(self).__name__}(area={self.area():.2f})"

class Circle(Shape):
    def __init__(self, r): self.r = r
    def area(self): return math.pi * self.r ** 2
    def perimeter(self): return 2 * math.pi * self.r

class Rectangle(Shape):
    def __init__(self, w, h): self.w, self.h = w, h
    def area(self): return self.w * self.h
    def perimeter(self): return 2 * (self.w + self.h)

class Square(Shape):                       # deliberately NOT a Rectangle subclass
    def __init__(self, side): self.side = side
    def area(self): return self.side ** 2
    def perimeter(self): return 4 * self.side

assert round(sum(s.area() for s in [Circle(1), Rectangle(2, 3), Square(2)]), 2) == 13.14
The trick part: if Rectangle is mutable (you can set w and h separately), Square-as-Rectangle breaks the Liskov Substitution Principle. Code that sets w = 5 and expects h to stay unchanged fails for a square. Make shapes immutable, or keep them as siblings.
Concept What's the difference between isinstance(x, C) and type(x) == C?
isinstance respects inheritance: a SavingsAccount is a BankAccount, so it returns True. It also supports ABCs, virtual subclasses, and tuples of types. type(x) == C is an exact match only, so it breaks for subclasses and violates polymorphism. Use isinstance, or better, call the method and rely on polymorphism instead of checking the type.
Coding Implement a Temperature class storing Celsius, with a fahrenheit property you can both read and set.
class Temperature:
    def __init__(self, celsius=0.0):
        self.celsius = celsius
    @property
    def celsius(self): return self._c
    @celsius.setter
    def celsius(self, v):
        if v < -273.15: raise ValueError("below absolute zero")
        self._c = v
    @property
    def fahrenheit(self): return self._c * 9 / 5 + 32
    @fahrenheit.setter
    def fahrenheit(self, f): self.celsius = (f - 32) * 5 / 9   # reuses the celsius validation

t = Temperature(100); assert t.fahrenheit == 212
t.fahrenheit = 32;    assert t.celsius == 0
This shows that a property can be computed (no stored _f) and settable, while all validation stays in one place.

11 OOP & the Data Model

25 min · classes, instance creation, dunders, operator dispatch, MRO, super, ABCs, dataclasses

Classes are objects too

class Dog:
    species = "canis"                 # class attribute (shared by all instances)
    def __init__(self, name):
        self.name = name              # instance attribute (stored in self.__dict__)
    def bark(self): return f"{self.name}: woof"
    @classmethod
    def from_str(cls, s): return cls(s.strip())   # alternative constructor; works for subclasses
    @staticmethod
    def valid(name): return name.isalpha()        # plain function in the class namespace

Dog.bark(d) == d.bark()   # methods are functions; d.bark is a *bound method* that holds (func, self)
Trap Mutable class attributes are shared. class A: items = [], then a1.items.append(1) shows up on every instance. Assigning a1.items = [...] creates an instance attribute that shadows the class one.
Interview check · 1 question
classmethod vs staticmethod vs instance method?
An instance method receives self. A classmethod receives cls, which gives polymorphic alternative constructors and class-level operations. A staticmethod receives nothing implicitly; it's a namespaced utility. All three are implemented as descriptors (ch.12).

Object creation: __new__ then __init__

Under the hood

Dog("rex") calls type(Dog).__call__(Dog, "rex"), i.e. type.__call__, which does:

obj = Dog.__new__(Dog, "rex")      # allocate (a static method; returns the instance)
if isinstance(obj, Dog):
    type(obj).__init__(obj, "rex") # initialize in place; must return None
return obj

Override __new__ to subclass immutable types (int, str, tuple), to implement singletons or caching, or to return a different type. Override __init__ for ordinary setup.

class Celsius(float):
    def __new__(cls, value):
        if value < -273.15: raise ValueError("below absolute zero")
        return super().__new__(cls, value)    # the value must be set during __new__ since float is immutable
Interview check · 1 question
__new__ vs __init__?
__new__ is a static method that creates and returns the instance. __init__ initializes an instance that already exists. type.__call__ calls __new__, then calls __init__ only if the result is an instance of the class. Use __new__ for immutable subclasses, singletons or caching, and metaclasses.

The data model: dunder methods

ProtocolMethods
Representation__repr__ (unambiguous, for developers, used in containers), __str__ (for users; falls back to repr), __format__
Comparison__eq__ __ne__ __lt__ __le__ __gt__ __ge__. @total_ordering fills in the rest from __eq__ + one ordering method
Hashing / truth__hash__, __bool__
Container__len__ __getitem__ __setitem__ __delitem__ __contains__ __iter__ __reversed__ __missing__
Numeric__add__ __radd__ __iadd__ __mul__ __matmul__ __truediv__ __floordiv__ __neg__ __abs__ __index__ …
Callable / context / async__call__, __enter__ __exit__, __aenter__ __aexit__ __aiter__ __anext__ __await__
Attribute__getattr__ __getattribute__ __setattr__ __delattr__ __dir__ (ch.12)
Lifecycle / class__new__ __init__ __del__ __init_subclass__ __set_name__ __class_getitem__
Under the hood: implicit lookups skip the instance len(x) and x + y look up dunders on the type, not the instance (they go through C slots). Setting obj.__len__ = lambda: 5 on an instance does nothing for len(obj). This is faster and keeps metaclass behavior consistent.
Interview check · 4 questions
__str__ vs __repr__?
__repr__ is an unambiguous representation for developers, ideally valid code that reconstructs the object. It is used by the REPL, by containers, and by !r. __str__ is the readable form for print and str(), and it falls back to __repr__. Always define __repr__.
Coding Build a Matrix class supporting m[i, j], +, @, ==, and iterating over rows.
class Matrix:
    def __init__(self, rows):
        self.rows = [list(r) for r in rows]
        self.n, self.m = len(self.rows), len(self.rows[0]) if rows else 0
    def __getitem__(self, ij):                 # m[i, j] passes the tuple (i, j)
        i, j = ij; return self.rows[i][j]
    def __setitem__(self, ij, v):
        i, j = ij; self.rows[i][j] = v
    def __iter__(self): return iter(self.rows)
    def __eq__(self, o): return isinstance(o, Matrix) and self.rows == o.rows
    def __add__(self, o):
        if not isinstance(o, Matrix): return NotImplemented
        return Matrix([[a + b for a, b in zip(r1, r2)] for r1, r2 in zip(self.rows, o.rows)])
    def __matmul__(self, o):
        cols = list(zip(*o.rows))
        return Matrix([[sum(a * b for a, b in zip(r, c)) for c in cols] for r in self.rows])
    def __repr__(self): return f"Matrix({self.rows})"

a = Matrix([[1, 2], [3, 4]])
assert (a @ a).rows == [[7, 10], [15, 22]] and a[1, 0] == 3
Coding Make __getitem__ support slices that return the same type, like list does.
class Series:
    def __init__(self, data): self._data = list(data)
    def __len__(self): return len(self._data)
    def __getitem__(self, index):
        if isinstance(index, slice):
            return type(self)(self._data[index])    # type(self): subclasses get their own type back
        return self._data[index]
    def __repr__(self): return f"{type(self).__name__}({self._data})"

s = Series(range(10))
assert isinstance(s[2:5], Series) and s[2:5]._data == [2, 3, 4] and s[-1] == 9
# a slice object carries .start, .stop, .step; slice.indices(len) normalizes negatives and None
Concept What do object's default __eq__ and __hash__ do?
By default, a == b is identity (a is b), and hash(a) is derived from id(a). So every instance is unique and hashable, and it's safe in sets because identity never changes. Once you override __eq__ to compare by value, you must define a matching __hash__ (or leave it as None, meaning unhashable). Otherwise equal objects could have different hashes.

Operator dispatch and NotImplemented

a + b:
 1. if type(b) is a proper subclass of type(a) and overrides __radd__ → try b.__radd__(a) first
 2. r = a.__add__(b);  if r is not NotImplemented → return r
 3. r = b.__radd__(a); if r is not NotImplemented → return r
 4. raise TypeError
a += b: try a.__iadd__(b) (mutate in place), else fall back to a = a + b
class Vec:
    __slots__ = ("x", "y")
    def __init__(self, x, y): self.x, self.y = x, y
    def __repr__(self): return f"Vec({self.x!r}, {self.y!r})"
    def __eq__(self, o):
        if not isinstance(o, Vec): return NotImplemented   # NOT False; lets the other side try
        return (self.x, self.y) == (o.x, o.y)
    def __hash__(self): return hash((self.x, self.y))
    def __add__(self, o):
        return Vec(self.x + o.x, self.y + o.y) if isinstance(o, Vec) else NotImplemented
    def __mul__(self, k): return Vec(self.x * k, self.y * k)
    __rmul__ = __mul__                                       # 3 * v works
    def __abs__(self): return (self.x**2 + self.y**2) ** 0.5
    def __bool__(self): return bool(self.x or self.y)
Interview check · 4 questions
Why return NotImplemented instead of raising or returning False?
It tells the interpreter to try the reflected operation on the other operand (__radd__, or the swapped __eq__). Only when both sides return it does Python raise TypeError (for == it falls back to identity). Returning False would wrongly decide the comparison.
Coding Implement Money so that sum([Money(...), ...]) works and adding different currencies fails.
from decimal import Decimal
from functools import total_ordering

@total_ordering
class Money:
    __slots__ = ("amount", "currency")
    def __init__(self, amount, currency="USD"):
        self.amount, self.currency = Decimal(str(amount)), currency
    def _check(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Money): return False
        if other.currency != self.currency: raise ValueError("currency mismatch")
        return True
    def __add__(self, other):
        if not self._check(other): return NotImplemented
        return Money(self.amount + other.amount, self.currency)
    def __radd__(self, other):                 # sum() starts with 0 → 0 + Money calls Money.__radd__
        return self if other == 0 else self.__add__(other)
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return isinstance(other, Money) and (self.amount, self.currency) == (other.amount, other.currency)
    def __lt__(self, other):
        if not self._check(other): return NotImplemented
        return self.amount < other.amount
    def __hash__(self): return hash((self.amount, self.currency))
    def __repr__(self): return f"Money('{self.amount}', {self.currency!r})"

assert sum([Money("1.10"), Money("2.20")]) == Money("3.30")
Coding Show the difference between __add__ and __iadd__. Why does it matter for identity?
class Bag:
    def __init__(self, items=()): self.items = list(items)
    def __add__(self, other):                 # a + b → a NEW object
        return Bag(self.items + other.items)
    def __iadd__(self, other):                # a += b → mutate IN PLACE, return self
        self.items.extend(other.items)
        return self                           # must return self (or the result is rebound to None!)

a = Bag([1]); alias = a
a = a + Bag([2]);  assert alias.items == [1]      # rebinding: the alias is unchanged
a = alias; a += Bag([3]); assert alias.items == [1, 3] and a is alias   # in place: the alias sees it
If __iadd__ isn't defined, a += b falls back to a = a + b. Forgetting return self in __iadd__ sets a to None, which is a classic bug.
Deep-dive Base() == Child(): whose __eq__ runs first?
Normally the left operand's method runs first. Exception: if the right operand's type is a subclass of the left's type and overrides the reflected method, Python tries the right side first. For ==, the reflection of __eq__ is __eq__, so Child.__eq__ runs first. This lets subclasses that know more about the comparison take control. The same rule applies to + (__radd__) and the other operators.

Inheritance, MRO, and super()

Under the hood: C3 linearization

The MRO is the order Python searches classes. C3 guarantees that (1) a child comes before its parents, (2) the order of bases in the class statement is kept, and (3) the result is consistent across the hierarchy (monotonic). If no consistent order exists, you get TypeError: Cannot create a consistent method resolution order.

class A: ...
class B(A): ...
class C(A): ...
class D(B, C): ...
D.__mro__    # (D, B, C, A, object)   — the diamond: A appears once, after both B and C

L[D] = D + merge(L[B], L[C], [B, C]). Repeatedly take the first head that doesn't appear in the tail of any other list.

super() means "next in the MRO of type(self)", not "my parent". With cooperative multiple inheritance, every class calls super().__init__(**kwargs) so the whole chain runs exactly once.

class Base:
    def __init__(self, **kw): super().__init__(**kw)   # passes leftover kwargs along to object
class Logged(Base):
    def __init__(self, *, log=True, **kw): self.log = log; super().__init__(**kw)
class Cached(Base):
    def __init__(self, *, size=10, **kw): self.size = size; super().__init__(**kw)
class Service(Logged, Cached): pass
Service(log=False, size=5)   # MRO: Service → Logged → Cached → Base → object

Zero-argument super() works because the compiler gives the method a hidden __class__ cell (the class it was defined in) and reads the first argument (self/cls). It equals super(__class__, self).

Mixins are small classes that add one capability (JSONMixin, ReprMixin), have no state of their own, and are listed before the main base. Prefer composition over deep inheritance.

Interview check · 2 questions
Explain the MRO and how super() works with diamond inheritance.
C3 linearization produces a single order: children before parents, local base order preserved, and consistent across the hierarchy. super() follows the MRO of the actual instance's type starting after the current class, so in a diamond each class's method runs once, as long as every class cooperates by calling super and accepting **kwargs.
Debug In a diamond hierarchy, the base __init__ runs twice and opens two DB connections. Why?
The subclasses call their parents explicitly (Base.__init__(self)) instead of super().__init__(). Explicit calls ignore the MRO, so each path reaches Base. Fix: every class in the hierarchy calls super().__init__(**kwargs) (cooperative inheritance). Then the MRO runs each __init__ exactly once. Check the order with Cls.__mro__.

Abstract classes and protocols

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
class Repo(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def get(self, id): ...
Repo()   # TypeError: Can't instantiate abstract class  (checked in object.__new__)

from typing import Protocol, runtime_checkable
@runtime_checkable
class SupportsClose(Protocol):          # structural ("static duck") typing: no inheritance needed
    def close(self) -> None: ...
isinstance(open("f"), SupportsClose)    # True: only checks that the method exists

collections.abc (Iterable, Mapping, MutableSequence…) gives you mixin methods for free. Implement __getitem__ and __len__, and Sequence provides __contains__, index, count, and __reversed__. ABCs also support virtual subclasses through register() and __subclasshook__.

Interview check · 1 question
ABC vs Protocol?
An ABC is nominal: you must inherit from it or register with it, it can carry implemented mixin methods, and it enforces abstract methods at instantiation. A Protocol is structural: any class with matching methods satisfies it. It is mainly checked by static type checkers, and with @runtime_checkable, isinstance checks whether the methods exist (not their signatures).

dataclasses

from dataclasses import dataclass, field, KW_ONLY

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True, order=True)   # slots= since 3.10
class Point:
    x: float
    y: float = 0.0
    tags: list[str] = field(default_factory=list, compare=False)  # mutable defaults need a factory

    def __post_init__(self): ...        # runs after the generated __init__ (validation)
# Generated: __init__, __repr__, __eq__; with order=True __lt__ etc.; frozen → __setattr__ raises
#   and __hash__ is generated. Other dataclass tools: replace(), asdict(), astuple(), fields().

How it works: the decorator reads __annotations__, generates source code for __init__ and the other methods, and execs it. No metaclass is involved. Alternatives: attrs (more features), pydantic (runtime validation and parsing), NamedTuple (immutable and tuple-compatible).

Interview check · 1 question
Follow-up dataclass vs NamedTuple vs attrs vs pydantic: which do you pick, and when?
dataclass: an internal domain model, stdlib only, with no validation. NamedTuple: immutable lightweight records that need tuple behavior (unpacking, use as dict keys). attrs: a richer dataclass (validators, converters, slots by default). pydantic: data at the edges (HTTP, config, JSON), where it validates and parses untrusted input and generates schemas. A common architecture: pydantic at the boundary, dataclasses in the core.

Encapsulation

Python has no access modifiers. _name means "internal, by convention". __name triggers name mangling to _ClassName__name, which prevents subclasses from accidentally clobbering it. It is not a security feature. Use @property to add validation later without changing the API.

More interview questions

Deep-dive bool is a subclass of int. What problems does that cause, and why can't you subclass bool?
Problems: isinstance(True, int) is True, so validation code accepts True as a number, JSON or DB layers can coerce it, True == 1 collides as a dict key, and sum(flags) counts Trues (sometimes handy). Bool is final (its type lacks the Py_TPFLAGS_BASETYPE flag) because there must be exactly two instances (singletons). To exclude bools, check with type(x) is int, or test bool before int.

12 Attribute Access, Descriptors & __slots__

20 min · the lookup algorithm, data vs non-data descriptors, property, bound methods, slots

Descriptors are the mechanism behind methods, property, classmethod, staticmethod, __slots__, and ORM fields. Understanding them explains most of the OOP "magic" in Python.

The descriptor protocol

A descriptor is an object stored as a class attribute that defines one or more of these:

__get__(self, instance, owner)   # instance is None when accessed on the class
__set__(self, instance, value)
__delete__(self, instance)
__set_name__(self, owner, name)  # 3.6: called when the class is created; tells the descriptor its attribute name
  • Data descriptor: defines __set__ or __delete__. It wins over the instance __dict__. Examples: property, slot members.
  • Non-data descriptor: defines only __get__. The instance __dict__ wins over it. Examples: functions, classmethod, staticmethod, cached_property.
Interview check · 1 question
What is a descriptor? Give real examples.
An object, set as a class attribute, that implements __get__, __set__, or __delete__ to customize attribute access. Examples: functions (which produce bound methods), property, classmethod, staticmethod, slots, cached_property, and ORM fields.

The lookup algorithm: obj.attr

Under the hood: object.__getattribute__ (C: PyObject_GenericGetAttr) 
1. cls_attr = search type(obj).__mro__ for "attr"          (cached in the per-type method cache)
2. if cls_attr is a DATA descriptor   → return cls_attr.__get__(obj, type(obj))
3. if "attr" in obj.__dict__           → return obj.__dict__["attr"]
4. if cls_attr is a NON-DATA descriptor→ return cls_attr.__get__(obj, type(obj))
5. if cls_attr found (plain value)     → return cls_attr
6. raise AttributeError → then Python calls type(obj).__getattr__(obj, "attr") if defined

Setting (obj.attr = v): if the type has a data descriptor for that name, call its __set__. Otherwise write obj.__dict__["attr"]. Class-level lookup (Cls.attr) goes through type.__getattribute__, which also checks the metaclass.

  • __getattribute__ runs for every attribute access. Overriding it is powerful and dangerous: call super().__getattribute__ to avoid infinite recursion.
  • __getattr__ runs only when normal lookup fails. Use it for proxies, lazy attributes, and delegation.
  • __setattr__ runs for every assignment. Inside it, use super().__setattr__(name, value).
Interview check · 4 questions
Data vs non-data descriptor precedence?
The order is: data descriptor on the type, then the instance __dict__, then a non-data descriptor or plain class attribute, then __getattr__. That's why you can shadow a method by assigning to the instance, but cannot shadow a property.
__getattr__ vs __getattribute__?
__getattribute__ intercepts every access, and object's default implementation runs the descriptor algorithm. __getattr__ is the fallback that runs only after normal lookup raises AttributeError. Prefer __getattr__, which is safer and cheaper.
Coding Write a proxy that logs every method call on a wrapped object. Why does len(proxy) fail?
class LoggingProxy:
    def __init__(self, target): object.__setattr__(self, "_t", target)
    def __getattr__(self, name):                   # only called for attributes NOT found normally
        attr = getattr(self._t, name)
        if callable(attr):
            def wrapped(*a, **kw):
                print(f"call {name}{a}"); return attr(*a, **kw)
            return wrapped
        return attr

p = LoggingProxy([3, 1, 2]); p.sort()
len(p) raises TypeError because implicit special-method lookup goes through the type, not the instance, and __getattr__ is never consulted for dunders. You must define __len__, __iter__, and so on explicitly on the proxy class (libraries like wrapt do this).
Concept You can replace a method on one instance (obj.save = fake), but obj.total = 5 fails for a property. Why the difference?
Functions are non-data descriptors, so an instance __dict__ entry shadows them, and the assignment just stores a new attribute. property is a data descriptor (it defines __set__), so assignment goes through it. Without a setter it raises AttributeError, and reads always go to the property even if __dict__ has the key. That's also why mocking a property needs patch.object(Class, "prop", new_callable=PropertyMock) on the class.

How methods are bound

class A:
    def f(self): ...
a = A()
A.__dict__["f"]                # <function A.f>          — a plain function
A.__dict__["f"].__get__(a, A)  # <bound method A.f of a>  — a function is a non-data descriptor
a.f is a.f                     # False: a new bound-method object is created on every access
# 3.12+: the "a.f()" pattern compiles to LOAD_ATTR (method flag) + CALL, which skips creating the bound object

Rough pure-Python equivalents of the built-ins:

class Function:
    def __get__(self, obj, objtype=None):
        if obj is None: return self
        return MethodType(self, obj)

class StaticMethod:
    def __init__(self, f): self.f = f
    def __get__(self, obj, objtype=None): return self.f

class ClassMethod:
    def __init__(self, f): self.f = f
    def __get__(self, obj, objtype=None): return MethodType(self.f, objtype or type(obj))

class Property:
    def __init__(self, fget=None, fset=None): self.fget, self.fset = fget, fset
    def __get__(self, obj, objtype=None):
        return self if obj is None else self.fget(obj)
    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if self.fset is None: raise AttributeError("can't set attribute")
        self.fset(obj, value)
    def setter(self, fset): return type(self)(self.fget, fset)

A reusable validated field

class Positive:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.private = "_" + name
    def __get__(self, obj, objtype=None):
        return self if obj is None else getattr(obj, self.private)
    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if value <= 0: raise ValueError(f"{self.private[1:]} must be > 0")
        setattr(obj, self.private, value)

class Order:
    qty = Positive()           # one descriptor instance shared by the class; state lives on obj
    price = Positive()
    def __init__(self, qty, price): self.qty, self.price = qty, price

This is how Django model fields and SQLAlchemy columns work. Store per-instance state on the instance, not on the descriptor, which is shared by every instance.

functools.cached_property is a non-data descriptor. On first access it computes the value and writes it into obj.__dict__, so later reads skip the descriptor entirely (step 3 beats step 4). It requires the instance to have a __dict__.

Interview check · 5 questions
Coding Write a Typed descriptor that enforces a type on assignment, and use it in a class.
class Typed:
    def __init__(self, kind): self.kind = kind
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name): self.name = name
    def __get__(self, obj, objtype=None):
        if obj is None: return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]               # data descriptor wins, so reading the same key is safe
    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not isinstance(value, self.kind):
            raise TypeError(f"{self.name} must be {self.kind.__name__}")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value

class User:
    age = Typed(int)
    name = Typed(str)
    def __init__(self, name, age): self.name, self.age = name, age

u = User("a", 3)
try: u.age = "x"; raise AssertionError
except TypeError: pass
Storing the value under the same name in obj.__dict__ works because data descriptors take precedence over the instance dict.
Deep-dive How does an ORM make User.email return a column object but user.email return a string?
The field is a descriptor on the class. __get__(self, instance, owner) receives instance=None for class access, so it returns itself (the column) to build queries like User.email == "x", often by overloading __eq__ to produce an SQL expression. For instance access it returns the loaded value from the instance's state. __set__ marks the field dirty for the next flush.
Follow-up Is functools.cached_property thread-safe?
Not since 3.12. The per-property lock was removed because it serialized every instance's access. Two threads may both compute the value, and the last write wins. If the computation must run exactly once, add your own lock (per instance, or with double-checked locking) or compute the value eagerly in __init__.
Coding Write a lazy descriptor that computes an attribute on first access and never again, and explain why it never runs again.
class lazy:
    def __init__(self, func): self.func = func
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name): self.name = name
    def __get__(self, obj, objtype=None):
        if obj is None: return self
        value = self.func(obj)
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value       # cache it in the instance dict under the SAME name
        return value

class Report:
    calls = 0
    @lazy
    def data(self):
        Report.calls += 1
        return [1, 2, 3]

r = Report(); r.data; r.data
assert Report.calls == 1
Why: lazy has only __get__, so it's a non-data descriptor. After the first access, obj.__dict__["data"] exists, and the instance dict beats non-data descriptors in the lookup order. The descriptor is never consulted again. This is exactly how functools.cached_property works.
Deep-dive When exactly is __set_name__ called? What if you attach a descriptor after the class exists?
type.__new__ calls it once per attribute while the class is being created, passing (owner_class, attribute_name). If you later do MyClass.field = Typed(int), it is not called automatically, so the descriptor doesn't know its name and breaks. Call descriptor.__set_name__(MyClass, "field") yourself. Copying a descriptor to two names gives one instance two names. Create a separate instance per attribute.

__slots__

class P:
    __slots__ = ("x", "y")     # no per-instance __dict__ or __weakref__ (unless listed)
    def __init__(self, x, y): self.x, self.y = x, y
P(1, 2).z = 3                  # AttributeError
P.x                            # <member 'x' of 'P' objects>  — a data descriptor at a fixed offset
Under the hood Each slot becomes a member_descriptor that reads and writes a fixed-offset pointer inside the C struct of the instance. There is no dict, so each instance saves roughly 50–100+ bytes and attribute access is slightly faster. This matters when you create millions of objects.
  • Every class in the hierarchy needs __slots__, or a __dict__ comes back.
  • Add "__weakref__" to the slots if you need weak references. Slots cannot coexist with a class attribute of the same name.
  • Using @dataclass(slots=True) creates a new class with the slots.
Interview check · 1 question
When would you use __slots__, and what are the downsides?
Use it when you have many small instances (memory savings, slightly faster access) or want to forbid stray attributes. Downsides: no dynamic attributes, no weakrefs or cached_property unless you add them, every class in the hierarchy must declare slots, and multiple inheritance with non-empty slots in more than one base fails with a layout conflict.

13 Metaclasses & Class Creation

15 min · type, the class-creation pipeline, __init_subclass__, when to use what

A class is an instance of its metaclass. By default that's type. type(type) is type, and type is itself a subclass of object.

Dog = type("Dog", (object,), {"bark": lambda self: "woof"})   # same as writing a class statement
type(Dog)        # <class 'type'>
isinstance(Dog, type), isinstance(type, object), isinstance(object, type)   # all True

What a class statement does

Under the hood: the class-creation pipeline 
class Child(Base, metaclass=Meta, flag=1):  body

1. Resolve bases     → call __mro_entries__ on non-class bases (e.g. Generic[T])
2. Pick metaclass    → explicit, or the most derived metaclass among the bases (conflict → TypeError)
3. Prepare namespace → ns = Meta.__prepare__("Child", bases, flag=1)   (default: a plain dict)
4. Execute body      → exec(body, globals, ns); __module__, __qualname__ are set, and
                       __classcell__ is added if any method uses super()/__class__
5. Create class      → cls = Meta("Child", bases, ns, flag=1)
                         → Meta.__new__ → type.__new__:
                              • builds the type object and computes the MRO
                              • calls __set_name__(cls, name) on every attribute that has it
                              • calls super(cls).__init_subclass__(flag=1) on the parent
                         → Meta.__init__
6. Apply class decorators (bottom-up), then bind the name "Child"
Interview check · 6 questions
What is a metaclass?
The class of a class: it is what creates class objects and defines how they behave. type is the default. A custom metaclass (a subclass of type) can hook into __prepare__, __new__, __init__, and __call__ to customize class creation and instantiation. Real uses: ABCMeta, EnumMeta, Django ModelBase.
Walk through what happens when Python executes a class statement.
It resolves the bases, picks the metaclass, calls __prepare__ to get the namespace, and executes the body in that namespace. Then it calls metaclass(name, bases, ns). Inside, type.__new__ builds the type, computes the MRO, calls __set_name__ on attributes, and calls the parent's __init_subclass__. Finally, class decorators are applied and the name is bound.
Debug class C(ModelBase, ABC) raises "metaclass conflict". Explain and fix.
The bases have unrelated metaclasses (say ModelMeta and ABCMeta). Python requires the derived class's metaclass to be a subclass of all of its bases' metaclasses. Fix: class ModelABCMeta(ModelMeta, ABCMeta): pass and declare class C(ModelBase, ABC, metaclass=ModelABCMeta). Or avoid ABC and use a Protocol. This is one reason to keep metaclasses rare.
Follow-up How does ABCMeta block instantiating abstract classes?
At class creation, ABCMeta.__new__ computes __abstractmethods__: a frozenset of names whose values have __isabstractmethod__ = True, including inherited names not yet overridden. The type gets a flag, and object.__new__ raises TypeError if that set is non-empty. Abstract-ness is therefore checked at instantiation, not at subclass definition.
Coding Create a class dynamically with type(), including a base class, a method, and a class attribute.
class Animal:
    def speak(self): return "..."

def bark(self): return f"{self.name} says woof"
def init(self, name): self.name = name

Dog = type("Dog", (Animal,), {"__init__": init, "speak": bark, "legs": 4})
d = Dog("rex")
assert d.speak() == "rex says woof" and Dog.legs == 4 and isinstance(d, Animal)
type(name, bases, namespace) is exactly what a class statement does at the end. Used for generated classes: ORMs, serializers, test factories, namedtuple (historically), and dataclasses.make_dataclass.
Coding Use __prepare__ to make defining the same method twice in a class body an error.
class NoDupNamespace(dict):
    def __setitem__(self, key, value):
        if key in self and not key.startswith("__"):
            raise TypeError(f"duplicate definition of {key!r}")
        super().__setitem__(key, value)

class NoDupMeta(type):
    @classmethod
    def __prepare__(mcls, name, bases, **kw):
        return NoDupNamespace()                 # the class body executes into THIS mapping
    def __new__(mcls, name, bases, ns, **kw):
        return super().__new__(mcls, name, bases, dict(ns))

try:
    class Service(metaclass=NoDupMeta):
        def run(self): ...
        def run(self): ...                      # accidental copy-paste
    raise AssertionError
except TypeError as e:
    assert "run" in str(e)
Real use: Enum uses a custom namespace to prevent duplicate member names.

A metaclass example

class PluginMeta(type):
    registry = {}
    def __new__(mcls, name, bases, ns, **kw):
        cls = super().__new__(mcls, name, bases, ns)
        if bases:                                 # skip the abstract base itself
            mcls.registry[name.lower()] = cls
        return cls
    def __call__(cls, *args, **kw):               # intercepts instantiation of every class using this metaclass
        inst = super().__call__(*args, **kw)
        return inst

class Plugin(metaclass=PluginMeta): pass
class CSVExporter(Plugin): pass
PluginMeta.registry      # {'csvexporter': <class CSVExporter>}

Singleton via a metaclass (a classic interview question)

class Singleton(type):
    _instances = {}
    def __call__(cls, *a, **kw):
        if cls not in cls._instances:
            cls._instances[cls] = super().__call__(*a, **kw)
        return cls._instances[cls]
class Config(metaclass=Singleton): ...
# More Pythonic: a module-level instance (modules are singletons), or @functools.cache on a factory.
# Thread safety: wrap the creation in a lock if construction can race.
Interview check · 2 questions
Coding Enforce at class-definition time that every public method of every subclass has a docstring.
class Documented:
    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kw):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kw)
        for name, val in vars(cls).items():
            if callable(val) and not name.startswith("_") and not val.__doc__:
                raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__}.{name} needs a docstring")

class Ok(Documented):
    def run(self):
        """Runs."""
try:
    class Bad(Documented):
        def run(self): pass
    raise AssertionError
except TypeError: pass
A metaclass works too, but __init_subclass__ is simpler and can't cause metaclass conflicts. Interviewers like it when you choose the lighter tool.
Deep-dive How does Enum stop you from doing Color.RED = 5 or creating Color(...) members dynamically?
Enum classes are built by the EnumType metaclass. Its __prepare__ returns a special _EnumDict that records member definitions in order and rejects duplicates. EnumType.__new__ turns each value into a singleton member instance. EnumType.__setattr__ blocks reassigning members, and EnumType.__call__ makes Color(1) a lookup, not a construction. Iteration and len(Color) work through the metaclass's __iter__ and __len__.

Lighter alternatives (prefer these)

class Plugin:
    registry = {}
    def __init_subclass__(cls, /, key=None, **kw):   # 3.6, PEP 487; implicitly a classmethod
        super().__init_subclass__(**kw)
        Plugin.registry[key or cls.__name__] = cls

class CSV(Plugin, key="csv"): pass
NeedTool
Register or validate subclasses__init_subclass__
Let an attribute know its own name__set_name__
Modify one classclass decorator
Make Cls[int] work__class_getitem__
Change how the class itself behaves (Cls[...], len(Cls), iterating a class, a custom namespace, behavior that must be inherited)metaclass. Examples: Enum, ABCMeta, ORMs

"Metaclasses are deeper magic than 99% of users should ever worry about" (Tim Peters). Know how they work, but reach for them last.

Interview check · 2 questions
Metaclass vs class decorator vs __init_subclass__?
A decorator affects only the class it's applied to and isn't inherited. __init_subclass__ runs for every subclass and covers most registration and validation needs simply. A metaclass is needed only when you must customize the class object's own behavior, its namespace, or instantiation. Metaclasses also complicate multiple inheritance (metaclass conflicts).
Coding Register every subclass automatically and build objects by name, using a factory.
class Shape:
    _registry: dict[str, type["Shape"]] = {}
    def __init_subclass__(cls, /, kind: str, **kw):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kw)
        Shape._registry[kind] = cls
    @classmethod
    def create(cls, kind: str, *args):
        try: return cls._registry[kind](*args)
        except KeyError: raise ValueError(f"unknown shape {kind!r}") from None

class Circle(Shape, kind="circle"):
    def __init__(self, r): self.r = r
class Square(Shape, kind="square"):
    def __init__(self, s): self.s = s

assert isinstance(Shape.create("circle", 2), Circle) and set(Shape._registry) == {"circle", "square"}
This is used for parsing config or JSON ("type": "circle") into objects. New shapes register themselves, so the factory never changes (open/closed).

14 Memory Management & Garbage Collection

20 min · refcounting, cyclic GC, pymalloc, weakref, leaks, profiling memory

Layer 1: reference counting

Every object has ob_refcnt. It goes up when a reference is created (binding a name, putting the object in a container, passing it as an argument) and down when one goes away. When it hits zero, the object is deallocated immediately, which is deterministic. That's why open(...).read() closes the file right away in CPython.

import sys
x = []
sys.getrefcount(x)   # 2: x plus the temporary argument reference

Strengths: immediate, predictable freeing with good cache behavior. Weaknesses: it cannot free cycles, every increment and decrement costs time, and the counts aren't thread-safe without a lock (the main reason the GIL exists).

Interview check · 2 questions
How does Python manage memory?
A private heap managed by CPython. Reference counting frees most objects immediately. A generational cyclic GC (incremental in 3.14) handles reference cycles among container objects. pymalloc serves small allocations (512 bytes or less) from arenas, pools, and blocks, and type free lists recycle common objects. Developers never call free directly.
Concept What does del x actually do?
