It removes the name x from the current namespace and decrements the object's refcount. The object is freed only if that was the last reference. del lst[0] and del d[k] call __delitem__, and del obj.attr calls __delattr__. del never directly destroys an object, and it doesn't call __del__ unless the refcount reaches zero.

Layer 2: the cyclic garbage collector

a = []; a.append(a)     # self-reference: refcount never reaches 0
del a                   # unreachable now, but only the cyclic GC can free it
Under the hood: how the gc module finds cycles
  • Only container objects that can hold references (lists, dicts, class instances…) are tracked. They're kept in a doubly linked list via a PyGC_Head placed before the object. Atomic objects (ints, strings) are not tracked.
  • The algorithm: for each tracked object, copy its refcount to gc_ref. For each reference between tracked objects, decrement the target's gc_ref. Objects still above zero are referenced from outside the group, so they are reachable roots. Everything reachable from a root survives, and the rest is garbage.
  • Generations (through 3.13): 0, 1, 2 with thresholds (700, 10, 10). A collection runs when allocations minus deallocations exceed 700. This follows the weak generational hypothesis: most objects die young. Survivors are promoted.
  • 3.14: an incremental collector with a young and an old generation. Each pass scans the young generation plus a slice of the old one, so long-lived heaps no longer cause long pauses.
  • Finalizers: since 3.4 (PEP 442), cycles containing objects with __del__ can be collected. gc.garbage is now rarely populated.
import gc
gc.collect()           # force a full collection; returns the number of unreachable objects found
gc.get_threshold(); gc.set_threshold(...)
gc.disable()           # some services (e.g. Instagram) disabled it or used gc.freeze() after fork,
gc.freeze()            # so copy-on-write pages aren't dirtied by GC writes to object headers
gc.get_referrers(obj)  # debugging: who references this object?
Interview check · 4 questions
Why can't reference counting alone handle all garbage?
In a cycle (A → B → A), each object holds a reference to the other, so neither count reaches zero even when nothing outside points to them. The cyclic GC detects groups whose references come only from inside the group.
Deep-dive Why do pre-fork servers call gc.freeze(), and why does refcounting hurt copy-on-write?
After fork, the child shares the parent's memory pages until either side writes to them. Simply reading a Python object increments its refcount, which is a write, and GC passes write to object headers. So most shared pages get copied, and memory multiplies across workers. gc.freeze() before forking moves all existing objects into a permanent generation the GC never scans. Immortal objects (3.12) avoid refcount writes for the core singletons.
Follow-up Which objects does the cycle GC track? Is {"a": 1} tracked?
Only containers that can form cycles. Ints, strings, and floats are never tracked. CPython also untracks some containers as an optimization: a dict holding only atomic keys and values may be untracked, and tuples of atomic items get untracked during collection. Check with gc.is_tracked(obj). Fewer tracked objects means cheaper GC passes.
Coding Prove that the cycle GC frees a reference cycle that refcounting cannot.
import gc, weakref

class Node:
    def __init__(self): self.other = None

freed = []
a, b = Node(), Node()
a.other, b.other = b, a                     # a cycle: a → b → a
r = weakref.ref(a, lambda _: freed.append("a"))   # the callback fires when a is really freed
gc.disable()
del a, b                                    # the refcounts stay at 1 (each references the other)
assert r() is not None and freed == []      # still alive: refcounting alone can't free a cycle
gc.collect()                                # the cycle detector finds the unreachable pair
assert r() is None and "a" in freed
gc.enable()

Layer 3: the allocator (pymalloc)

Under the hood 
Requests > 512 bytes ─────────────► system malloc
Requests ≤ 512 bytes ─► pymalloc:
   Arena (1 MiB, 3.10+, 64-bit; 256 KiB before)  ← obtained with mmap
     └─ Pools (16 KiB; 4 KiB before)            ← each pool serves ONE size class
          └─ Blocks (multiples of 16 bytes)     ← one object per block

This avoids malloc's overhead for the many small, short-lived objects Python creates. Type-specific free lists sit on top (floats, tuples, frames, dicts) to recycle objects. An arena is returned to the OS only when it is completely empty, so a long-running process's RSS often doesn't shrink after you free data. That is fragmentation, not a leak. The free-threaded build uses mimalloc.

Interview check · 1 question
Why doesn't the memory usage of my process go down after deleting a big list?
The objects were freed into pymalloc pools and free lists, but an arena returns to the OS only when every pool in it is empty, and fragmentation keeps arenas partly used. glibc malloc may also hold freed memory. The memory is reused by later Python allocations. For truly temporary big work, use a subprocess.

__del__ and weak references

  • __del__ is a finalizer, not a destructor. It runs when the refcount reaches 0 or when the GC collects the object, and at interpreter exit it might not run at all. Exceptions inside it are printed and ignored. Prefer context managers or weakref.finalize.
  • A weakref refers to an object without keeping it alive. Use it for caches, observers, and parent back-pointers in trees.
import weakref
cache = weakref.WeakValueDictionary()   # an entry disappears when its value has no strong refs left
r = weakref.ref(obj, callback)          # r() returns the object, or None once it's dead
weakref.finalize(obj, cleanup, arg)     # reliable cleanup hook; runs at most once
Interview check · 1 question
Coding Build a cache of parsed objects that doesn't keep them alive once nobody else uses them.
import weakref
class Parsed:                      # weakref needs a normal class (not int/str/tuple), or __weakref__ in __slots__
    def __init__(self, src): self.src = src

_cache: "weakref.WeakValueDictionary[str, Parsed]" = weakref.WeakValueDictionary()
def parse(src: str) -> Parsed:
    obj = _cache.get(src)
    if obj is None:
        obj = Parsed(src)
        _cache[src] = obj          # the entry vanishes when the last strong reference dies
    return obj

a = parse("x"); assert parse("x") is a
del a                              # in CPython the entry disappears immediately (refcount hits 0)

Common memory leaks in Python

  • Unbounded caches: module-level dicts, lru_cache(maxsize=None), memoizing on self.
  • Growing globals or class-level lists. Listeners or callbacks that are never unregistered.
  • Reference cycles involving large objects, which wait for a GC pass. Keeping exception tracebacks alive (the traceback references frames, which reference all their locals).
  • C extensions that leak, and thread-locals in thread pools that are never cleared.
import tracemalloc
tracemalloc.start()
... # workload
snap = tracemalloc.take_snapshot()
for stat in snap.statistics("lineno")[:10]: print(stat)   # top allocation sites
# Also: objgraph (back-reference graphs), memray / scalene (native + Python heap), sys.getsizeof (shallow size only)
Trap sys.getsizeof is shallow: getsizeof([big_obj]) counts only the list's pointer array, not big_obj. For a deep size, walk the references (e.g. pympler.asizeof).
Interview check · 3 questions
Can Python have memory leaks? How do you find them?
Yes, logical leaks: references that stay reachable unintentionally (caches, globals, listeners, cycles with long-lived roots, C extensions). Find them with tracemalloc snapshot diffs, gc.get_referrers/objgraph, and memray, and by watching RSS over time under load.
Debug A Celery or Gunicorn worker's memory climbs 50 MB per hour until it's killed. Walk through your investigation.
  1. Confirm the trend in metrics (RSS over time vs requests served). Is it a leak or just fragmentation or a warming cache?
  2. Reproduce under load. Take tracemalloc snapshots at T1 and T2 and diff them with snap2.compare_to(snap1, "lineno") to find the top growing allocation sites.
  3. Inspect growing types with objgraph.show_growth(), then find what's holding them with gc.get_referrers or objgraph.show_backrefs.
  4. Suspects: module-level caches or dicts, unbounded lru_cache, listeners never removed, stored exceptions or tracebacks, a C-extension leak (use memray with native traces).
  5. Mitigate right away with max_requests or max_tasks_per_child worker recycling, then fix the root cause.
Debug In Jupyter, del big_df doesn't free memory. Why?
IPython keeps hidden references: the output cache (Out[12], _, __, ___) holds anything you displayed, and the last exception's traceback holds frames whose locals reference the data. Fixes: %reset out / %xdel big_df, avoid displaying huge objects, then gc.collect(). Even then, the allocator might not return the memory to the OS (ch.14).

More interview questions

Deep-dive How would you measure how much __slots__ saves for 1M objects?
import tracemalloc
class P:  __slots__ = ("x", "y")
class D:
    def __init__(self): pass
def measure(cls):
    tracemalloc.start()
    objs = [cls() for _ in range(100_000)]
    for o in objs: o.x = o.y = 1
    cur, _ = tracemalloc.get_traced_memory(); tracemalloc.stop()
    return cur / len(objs)
print(f"slots ≈ {measure(P):.0f} B/obj, dict ≈ {measure(D):.0f} B/obj")
Typical results on 3.12: about 56–64 bytes per object with slots vs about 100–150 with a dict (3.11+ shrank instance dicts considerably). Measure on your version, because the gap has narrowed over the years.

15 Exceptions & Context Managers

15 min · hierarchy, chaining, ExceptionGroup, the with protocol, contextlib

Hierarchy (the important part)

BaseException
 ├── SystemExit, KeyboardInterrupt, GeneratorExit   ← don't catch these by accident
 ├── BaseExceptionGroup → ExceptionGroup (3.11)
 └── Exception
      ├── ArithmeticError → ZeroDivisionError, OverflowError
      ├── LookupError → KeyError, IndexError
      ├── OSError → FileNotFoundError, PermissionError, ConnectionError, TimeoutError…
      ├── ValueError → UnicodeError…    TypeError   AttributeError   NameError → UnboundLocalError
      ├── RuntimeError → RecursionError, NotImplementedError
      └── StopIteration, StopAsyncIteration, ImportError → ModuleNotFoundError …

Bare except: and except BaseException also catch Ctrl-C and sys.exit. Catch the narrowest exception you can, and at minimum use except Exception.

Interview check · 2 questions
Follow-up How do you design the exception hierarchy for a library?
One root exception (class AcmeError(Exception)) so users can catch everything from the library. Subclasses by how the caller should react, not by where the error was raised: NotFound, Conflict, RetryableError, AuthError. Include structured attributes (status code, retry_after). Wrap low-level errors with raise AcmeError(...) from e so they don't leak implementation details. Optionally multi-inherit from builtins (class NotFound(AcmeError, KeyError)) for compatibility.
Concept Which exceptions should you almost never catch, and why?
KeyboardInterrupt and SystemExit (the user or program wants to stop), GeneratorExit (it would break generator cleanup), and asyncio.CancelledError (it breaks cancellation; it's a BaseException since 3.8). These are why the hierarchy splits BaseException from Exception: except Exception lets them through. MemoryError and RecursionError are Exceptions, but recovering from them is rarely safe. Log and re-raise.

Full syntax and control flow

try:
    r = risky()
except (KeyError, ValueError) as e:      # `e` is deleted at the end of the block (avoids a frame cycle)
    log.warning("bad: %s", e)
    raise                                 # re-raise, keeping the original traceback
except Exception as e:
    raise AppError("failed") from e       # explicit chaining: sets __cause__
else:
    commit(r)                             # runs only if no exception; keep the try block minimal
finally:
    cleanup()                             # always runs: on return, break, or exception
  • Chaining: an exception raised while handling another automatically records the original in __context__ ("During handling … another exception occurred"). raise X from e sets __cause__ ("The above exception was the direct cause"). raise X from None hides the context.
  • e.add_note("context") (3.11) attaches extra lines to the traceback.
  • EAFP vs LBYL: Pythonic code tries the operation and handles the failure ("easier to ask forgiveness than permission"). This avoids race conditions (TOCTOU: checking that a file exists and then opening it). Thanks to zero-cost try (3.11), the happy path is free.
Trap: return inside finally 
def f():
    try:
        return 1
    finally:
        return 2      # wins, and also silently swallows any in-flight exception
f()   # 2  — 3.14 emits a SyntaxWarning for return/break/continue in finally (PEP 765)
Interview check · 5 questions
What's the difference between else and finally on a try?
else runs only when the try block raised no exception. Put success-path code there so it isn't accidentally covered by the excepts. finally always runs, even on return, break, or an unhandled exception.
raise vs raise e vs raise New from e?
A bare raise re-raises the active exception with its original traceback. raise e re-raises the same object but adds the current line to the traceback. raise New from e wraps it: a new exception type with __cause__ set, which is good for crossing abstraction layers.
Debug Errors in a ThreadPoolExecutor job vanish silently. Where did they go?
An exception inside a submitted task is stored on the Future. It resurfaces only when you call future.result() (or inspect .exception()). Fire-and-forget submits lose it. Fix: iterate with as_completed and call result(), or add a done_callback that logs the exception. For plain threading.Thread, set threading.excepthook. In asyncio, look for "Task exception was never retrieved" and keep task references.
Deep-dive If try is "zero-cost" since 3.11, is raising exceptions cheap?
No. Zero-cost means entering a try block costs nothing on the happy path, because handlers are found through the exception table. Raising still allocates an exception object, builds traceback entries as the stack unwinds, and runs handler matching. That costs microseconds per raise. EAFP is fine for genuinely exceptional cases (a missing key), but don't use exceptions as normal control flow in a hot loop where the "failure" happens most of the time.
Deep-dive If code in finally (or __exit__) raises while an exception is already propagating, what happens to the original?
The new exception replaces the original as the one that propagates, but the original isn't lost: it's attached as the new exception's __context__, and the traceback shows "During handling of the above exception, another exception occurred". Cleanup code should therefore be defensive (wrap risky cleanup in its own try) so it doesn't mask the root cause.

ExceptionGroup and except* (3.11)

try:
    async with asyncio.TaskGroup() as tg:      # concurrent failures are raised together
        tg.create_task(a()); tg.create_task(b())
except* ValueError as eg:                       # handles every ValueError in the group
    for e in eg.exceptions: ...
except* OSError: ...                            # several except* clauses can each run
Interview check · 1 question
Coding Run several validators, collect all failures, and let callers handle them by type with except*.
def validate(user: dict) -> None:
    errors = []
    if "@" not in user.get("email", ""): errors.append(ValueError("bad email"))
    if user.get("age", 0) < 0:            errors.append(ValueError("negative age"))
    if "name" not in user:                 errors.append(KeyError("name"))
    if errors:
        raise ExceptionGroup("invalid user", errors)

caught = {}
try:
    validate({"email": "x", "age": -1})
except* ValueError as eg:
    caught["value"] = len(eg.exceptions)       # the 2 ValueErrors
except* KeyError as eg:
    caught["key"] = len(eg.exceptions)         # the 1 KeyError; both clauses run
assert caught == {"value": 2, "key": 1}
Form validation and batch jobs can report every error at once instead of failing on the first.

Custom exceptions

class AppError(Exception):
    """Root exception for the library. Callers can catch everything with one except."""
class NotFound(AppError):
    def __init__(self, key):
        super().__init__(f"not found: {key}")   # passing the message keeps str(e) and pickling working
        self.key = key

Context managers: the with protocol

with EXPR as VAR:
    BODY
# is roughly:
mgr = EXPR
enter, exit = type(mgr).__enter__, type(mgr).__exit__   # looked up on the type
VAR = enter(mgr)
try:
    BODY
except BaseException as e:
    if not exit(mgr, type(e), e, e.__traceback__):   # a truthy return SUPPRESSES the exception
        raise
else:
    exit(mgr, None, None, None)
from contextlib import contextmanager, suppress, ExitStack, closing, nullcontext, redirect_stdout, chdir

@contextmanager
def timer(label):
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    try:
        yield                          # the with-body runs here; an exception in it is re-raised at this yield
    finally:                           # without try/finally the cleanup is skipped when the body raises!
        print(label, time.perf_counter() - t0)

with suppress(FileNotFoundError): os.remove(p)
with ExitStack() as stack:             # a dynamic number of context managers
    files = [stack.enter_context(open(f)) for f in paths]
with open("a") as src, open("b", "w") as dst: ...   # parenthesized multi-line form allowed in 3.10+

Async versions: async with calls __aenter__/__aexit__, and contextlib.asynccontextmanager builds them from async generators. Context managers are used for locks, transactions, temporary state, and resource cleanup.

Interview check · 4 questions
How does a context manager suppress an exception?
If __exit__ returns a truthy value, the exception is swallowed. With @contextmanager, catch the exception around the yield and don't re-raise it.
Write a context manager that temporarily changes an environment variable.
@contextmanager
def env(key, value):
    old = os.environ.get(key)
    os.environ[key] = value
    try:
        yield
    finally:
        if old is None: os.environ.pop(key, None)
        else: os.environ[key] = old
Coding Write a Timer that works both as with Timer(): and as @Timer().
import time
from contextlib import ContextDecorator

class Timer(ContextDecorator):            # ContextDecorator adds __call__ that wraps a function in `with self`
    def __init__(self, label="block"): self.label = label
    def __enter__(self):
        self.t0 = time.perf_counter(); return self
    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc, tb):
        self.elapsed = time.perf_counter() - self.t0
        print(f"{self.label}: {self.elapsed:.4f}s")
        return False                      # don't suppress exceptions

with Timer("io"): sum(range(10**5))
@Timer("fn")
def work(): return 42
assert work() == 42
Coding Write a context manager that swallows the specified exceptions, logs them, and counts them.
import logging
log = logging.getLogger(__name__)

class Tolerate:
    def __init__(self, *exc_types):
        self.exc_types, self.count = exc_types, 0
    def __enter__(self): return self
    def __exit__(self, et, ev, tb):
        if et is not None and issubclass(et, self.exc_types):
            self.count += 1
            log.warning("tolerated %s: %s", et.__name__, ev)
            return True                    # suppress
        return False                       # anything else propagates

t = Tolerate(ValueError, KeyError)
for raw in ["1", "x", "3"]:
    with t: int(raw)
assert t.count == 1

16 Modules, Imports & Packaging

15 min · the import system, sys.modules, circular imports, packages, venvs, pyproject

What import x does

Under the hood: importlib 
import pkg.mod
1. Is "pkg.mod" in sys.modules? → return it (the cache; a module runs only ONCE per process)
2. Import the parent "pkg" first (runs pkg/__init__.py)
3. For each finder in sys.meta_path:  BuiltinImporter → FrozenImporter → PathFinder
       spec = finder.find_spec("pkg.mod", pkg.__path__)
   PathFinder walks sys.path / __path__ using path-entry finders (FileFinder: .py, .pyc, .so/.pyd)
4. module = loader.create_module(spec) or a new module object
5. sys.modules["pkg.mod"] = module          ← inserted BEFORE executing (this enables circular imports)
6. loader.exec_module(module)               ← run the code in module.__dict__ (using a cached .pyc if valid)
7. Bind the name "pkg" in the importer's namespace (import pkg.mod binds pkg; from-import binds the attribute)
  • sys.path: the script's directory (or cwd for -m/REPL), then PYTHONPATH, then the stdlib, then site-packages. Third-party code lands in site-packages.
  • if __name__ == "__main__":: a module run as a script has __name__ == "__main__". When it's imported, __name__ is its dotted name.
  • python -m pkg.mod runs a module with correct package context, so relative imports work. A package runs its __main__.py.
  • __all__ controls what from m import * exports. A leading underscore marks a name as private.
  • A package is a module with __path__. A regular package has __init__.py. A namespace package (PEP 420) has no __init__.py and can span several directories.
  • Relative imports: from . import sibling, from ..core import x. They work only inside packages.
  • importlib.import_module(name) imports dynamically. importlib.reload(m) re-executes the module in place, but old objects (and anything that did from m import f) keep references to the old code.
Interview check · 9 questions
What happens when you import a module twice?
The second import is a sys.modules dictionary lookup. The code doesn't run again. Module-level code therefore runs once per process, which is why modules work as natural singletons.
import x vs from x import y?
Both execute and cache all of x. import x binds the module, and x.y is looked up at use time, so you see later rebinding and monkeypatches. from x import y copies the current binding of y into your namespace, so later reassignment of x.y isn't seen. That matters for mocking: patch where the name is used.
What's in sys.path, and how does Python find a module?
The script directory, PYTHONPATH, the stdlib, and site-packages (from site.py and .pth files). The finders in sys.meta_path are asked in order. PathFinder scans the sys.path entries for a matching package directory, .py file, or extension module and returns a ModuleSpec with a loader.
Debug "It works in PyCharm but python src/app/main.py gives ModuleNotFoundError: app." Why?
Running a file puts its own directory (src/app) on sys.path[0], not the project root, so import app fails. The IDE adds the source roots to the path for you. Fixes: run it as a module from the right directory (python -m app.main with src on the path), or better, install the project in editable mode (pip install -e . or uv pip install -e .) with a src layout. Avoid sys.path hacks in code.
Coding Auto-discover and load every plugin module inside a package plugins/.
import importlib, pkgutil

def load_plugins(package_name: str) -> dict[str, object]:
    pkg = importlib.import_module(package_name)
    found = {}
    for info in pkgutil.iter_modules(pkg.__path__, prefix=package_name + "."):
        found[info.name] = importlib.import_module(info.name)   # runs the module, which can self-register
    return found
For installed third-party plugins, use entry points: importlib.metadata.entry_points(group="acme.plugins"), declared in pyproject.toml under [project.entry-points."acme.plugins"].
Deep-dive Two threads import the same module at the same time. Can it run twice or see a half-initialized module?
It won't run twice. importlib holds a global import lock briefly, plus a per-module lock (_ModuleLock) while the module executes. The second thread blocks until the module finishes, then gets the cached entry from sys.modules. Circular imports across threads could deadlock, so importlib detects that and lets one thread see a partially initialized module. Lesson: avoid side effects at import time, and in particular don't start threads that import during module load.
Concept Is __init__.py still required? What goes in it?
It isn't required: without it a directory becomes a namespace package (PEP 420), which can be split across several locations. But include it for normal packages. It marks a regular package, avoids slower namespace scanning, and gives you a place to define the public API (from .client import Client, __all__, __version__). Keep it light: heavy imports there slow every import pkg.anything.
Concept What does from module import * import, and why avoid it?
If the module defines __all__, exactly those names. Otherwise, every global not starting with an underscore. Avoid it because it pollutes your namespace, can silently shadow your own names or builtins, makes it unclear where a name came from (for readers and tools), and breaks static analysis. It's acceptable in a package's __init__.py to re-export a curated __all__, or in an interactive session.
Coding Make a heavy submodule load only when it's first accessed, using a module-level __getattr__.
# acme/__init__.py
import importlib

_LAZY = {"pandas_tools": ".pandas_tools", "ml": ".ml"}     # attribute → submodule

def __getattr__(name):                     # PEP 562: called only if normal module lookup fails
    if name in _LAZY:
        module = importlib.import_module(_LAZY[name], __name__)
        globals()[name] = module           # cache it: later accesses skip __getattr__
        return module
    raise AttributeError(f"module {__name__!r} has no attribute {name!r}")

def __dir__():
    return sorted(list(globals()) + list(_LAZY))

# user code:  import acme; acme.ml.train()   → acme.ml is imported only at this moment
SciPy and other big libraries use this to cut import time. In 3.15, lazy import syntax (PEP 810) covers the common case.

Circular imports

# a.py                        # b.py
import b                      from a import A    # ImportError: cannot import name 'A' from
class A: ...                  class B: ...       #   partially initialized module 'a'

Why: a is already in sys.modules but hasn't finished executing, so A doesn't exist yet. Fixes: restructure (move shared code into a third module), use import a and refer to a.A at call time, import inside a function, or for type hints only use if TYPE_CHECKING: imports.

3.15 (PEP 810) adds explicit lazy import x. The import resolves on first use, which improves startup time and helps with some cycles.

Interview check · 1 question
How do you resolve a circular import?
Restructure dependencies (extract the shared part into a lower-level module, or use dependency inversion). Or refer through the module at call time (import a; a.A), defer the import into a function, or for annotation-only cycles use TYPE_CHECKING together with string or deferred annotations.

Environments and packaging

  • venv: python -m venv .venv creates an isolated site-packages plus a pyvenv.cfg. sys.prefix points into the venv, and the stdlib is shared with the base interpreter.
  • pyproject.toml (PEP 517/518/621) is the standard place for project metadata plus a build-backend (hatchling, setuptools, flit, poetry-core, uv_build). It replaces setup.py.
  • Wheels (.whl) are prebuilt zip archives that install fast with no compilation. An sdist is a source archive. Platform tags look like cp313-cp313-manylinux_2_28_x86_64.
  • Tools: pip plus pip-tools, Poetry, or uv (Rust, very fast, manages Python versions, lockfiles, and venvs). Always pin applications with a lockfile. Libraries declare ranges.
  • src/ layout: keeps tests from importing the un-installed source by accident.
[project]
name = "acme"
version = "1.2.0"
requires-python = ">=3.11"
dependencies = ["httpx>=0.27", "pydantic>=2"]
[project.optional-dependencies]
dev = ["pytest", "mypy", "ruff"]
[project.scripts]
acme = "acme.cli:main"
[build-system]
requires = ["hatchling"]
build-backend = "hatchling.build"
Interview check · 1 question
Follow-up Why does pip install sometimes compile C code for minutes, and how do you avoid it in CI?
No matching wheel existed for your platform tag (OS, architecture, Python version, ABI), so pip fell back to the sdist and built it, which needs compilers and headers. Avoid it by pinning versions that ship wheels (manylinux, musllinux, macOS, Windows), using a supported Python version, caching built wheels (pip wheel into a private index), or building multi-stage Docker images. The --only-binary=:all: flag makes this fail fast instead.

17 Concurrency: GIL, Threads & Processes

25 min · what the GIL is and isn't, free-threading, threading, multiprocessing, futures, subinterpreters

The GIL: what it is

The Global Interpreter Lock is a mutex per interpreter. Only the thread holding it can execute Python bytecode. It exists because CPython's internals, especially reference counts, aren't thread-safe. One big lock made the interpreter simple, made single-threaded code fast, and made C extensions easy to write.

Under the hood: when the GIL is released
  • Blocking I/O and sleep: C code wraps blocking calls in Py_BEGIN_ALLOW_THREADS / Py_END_ALLOW_THREADS. Sockets, file reads, time.sleep, select all release the lock.
  • Forced switching: since 3.2, a thread waiting for the GIL sets a "drop request" after the switch interval (sys.getswitchinterval() = 5 ms). The running thread checks for it at "eval breaker" points (backward jumps, function calls) and hands the lock over.
  • C extensions such as numpy, hashlib on large data, zlib, and many DB drivers release the GIL during heavy computation, so those threads run truly in parallel.
WorkloadThreads (with GIL)Best tool
I/O-bound (HTTP, DB, files)Works well: the GIL is released while waitingasyncio (thousands of connections) or a ThreadPoolExecutor
CPU-bound pure PythonNo speedup, sometimes slower because of contentionmultiprocessing / ProcessPoolExecutor, C extensions, free-threaded build
CPU-bound in numpy etc.Often parallel (the GIL is released)Threads are fine
Interview check · 2 questions
What is the GIL, why does it exist, and how does it affect performance?
It's a per-interpreter mutex that allows only one thread to execute bytecode at a time. It protects the non-atomic reference counts and interpreter state, keeps single-threaded code fast, and simplifies C extensions. CPU-bound threads can't run in parallel. I/O-bound threads work fine because blocking calls release the GIL. Workarounds are multiprocessing, C extensions that release the GIL, asyncio for I/O, and the free-threaded build (3.13+, officially supported in 3.14).
Threads vs processes vs asyncio: how do you choose?
Use asyncio for many concurrent I/O operations with async libraries: cooperative scheduling with very cheap tasks. Use threads for I/O with blocking libraries or a moderate amount of concurrency, and for C code that releases the GIL. Use processes for CPU-bound pure Python. They pay for memory and pickling but scale across cores. Combine them as needed: an asyncio service can push CPU work to a process pool with run_in_executor.

The GIL doesn't make your code thread-safe

Trap: a race condition while holding the GIL 
counter = 0
def work():
    global counter
    for _ in range(1_000_000):
        counter += 1     # LOAD_GLOBAL, LOAD_CONST, BINARY_OP, STORE_GLOBAL: a thread switch can happen in between
# Four threads → total often less than 4,000,000 (lost updates)

lock = threading.Lock()
def work():
    global counter
    for _ in range(1_000_000):
        with lock:
            counter += 1
The GIL makes single bytecodes atomic, along with some C-level operations like list.append and dict[k] = v. Compound operations (read-modify-write, check-then-act) are not atomic. Always synchronize shared mutable state.
Interview check · 1 question
If we have the GIL, why do we need locks?
The GIL only guarantees that individual bytecodes (and some C-level operations) are atomic. A thread can be switched out between the read and the write of x += 1, or between checking a condition and acting on it. Locks protect invariants that span several operations. And without the GIL (free-threaded builds), correct locking is mandatory.

Free-threaded Python (no GIL)

  • 3.13 (PEP 703): an experimental build, python3.13t, without the GIL. 3.14 (PEP 779): officially supported but still optional, not the default. Check with sys._is_gil_enabled().
  • How it works: biased reference counting (the owning thread uses fast non-atomic increments, other threads use atomics), immortal objects, deferred refcounting for some objects, per-object locks on built-in containers (each list or dict has a lightweight mutex), mimalloc, and GC pauses that stop the world.
  • Costs: slower single-threaded performance (about 5–10% in 3.14), and C extensions must declare themselves safe, or the GIL is turned back on at import.

Subinterpreters: 3.12 (PEP 684) gave each interpreter its own GIL. 3.14 (PEP 734) adds concurrent.interpreters and InterpreterPoolExecutor: parallelism inside one process with isolated state, a middle ground between threads and processes.

Interview check · 1 question
Follow-up Your team wants to move to free-threaded Python 3.14t. What risks do you check?
(1) C extensions: are they marked free-threading compatible? Otherwise the GIL is re-enabled at import. (2) Hidden reliance on GIL atomicity, such as unsynchronized check-then-set, lazy-initialized globals, and non-thread-safe iterators shared between threads. Built-in containers stay internally consistent, but your invariants need locks. (3) Single-threaded slowdown (about 5–10%). (4) Memory overhead and mimalloc behavior. Run the test suite with thread stress tests and benchmark representative workloads before switching.

threading toolkit

import threading, queue
t = threading.Thread(target=fn, args=(1,), daemon=True)   # daemon threads are killed abruptly at exit
t.start(); t.join(timeout=5)

threading.Lock()        # not reentrant: the same thread acquiring it twice deadlocks
threading.RLock()       # reentrant (it tracks the owner and a count)
threading.Semaphore(5)  # limit concurrency
threading.Event()       # a one-shot flag: set() / wait()
threading.Condition()   # wait/notify around a lock
threading.local()       # per-thread storage
q = queue.Queue(maxsize=100)  # thread-safe FIFO; the best way to hand data between threads (producer/consumer)

Deadlock prevention: acquire locks in a consistent global order, hold them briefly, use timeouts, prefer queues over shared state, and never call unknown code while holding a lock.

Interview check · 5 questions
Coding Implement a thread-safe bounded blocking queue without the queue module.
import threading
from collections import deque

class BoundedQueue:
    def __init__(self, capacity: int):
        self._items = deque()
        self._cap = capacity
        lock = threading.Lock()
        self._not_full = threading.Condition(lock)     # two conditions sharing ONE lock
        self._not_empty = threading.Condition(lock)

    def put(self, item) -> None:
        with self._not_full:
            while len(self._items) >= self._cap:       # while, not if: spurious wakeups / competing producers
                self._not_full.wait()
            self._items.append(item)
            self._not_empty.notify()

    def get(self):
        with self._not_empty:
            while not self._items:
                self._not_empty.wait()
            item = self._items.popleft()
            self._not_full.notify()
            return item

q = BoundedQueue(2)
t = threading.Thread(target=lambda: [q.put(i) for i in range(100)]); t.start()
assert [q.get() for _ in range(100)] == list(range(100)); t.join()
Talking points: why while loops instead of if, why the two conditions share one lock, and how you'd add timeouts (wait(timeout)) and shutdown.
Coding Producer/consumer with N worker threads and graceful shutdown.
import queue, threading
STOP = object()                                   # sentinel ("poison pill")

def worker(q: queue.Queue, results: list, lock: threading.Lock):
    while (item := q.get()) is not STOP:
        try:
            with lock: results.append(item * item)
        finally:
            q.task_done()
    q.task_done()                                 # account for the STOP item too

q, results, lock = queue.Queue(maxsize=100), [], threading.Lock()
threads = [threading.Thread(target=worker, args=(q, results, lock)) for _ in range(4)]
for t in threads: t.start()
for i in range(1000): q.put(i)                    # blocks when full → backpressure
for _ in threads: q.put(STOP)                     # one pill per worker
for t in threads: t.join()
assert len(results) == 1000
Debug A production Python process hangs at 0% CPU. How do you find out where it's stuck?
Run py-spy dump --pid PID, which shows every thread's Python stack without stopping the process. Look for threads waiting on acquire (a deadlock: two threads holding locks in opposite order), on a socket read with no timeout, or on a full queue. Preventive measures: faulthandler.register(signal.SIGUSR1) to dump tracebacks on a signal, faulthandler.dump_traceback_later(timeout), and timeouts on every network call and lock acquire.
Coding Two threads print 1 to 10 in order: one prints the odd numbers, the other the even ones.
import threading

def run(n=10):
    out = []
    odd_turn, even_turn = threading.Semaphore(1), threading.Semaphore(0)   # odd goes first
    def odd():
        for i in range(1, n + 1, 2):
            odd_turn.acquire(); out.append(i); even_turn.release()          # hand the turn over
    def even():
        for i in range(2, n + 1, 2):
            even_turn.acquire(); out.append(i); odd_turn.release()
    t1, t2 = threading.Thread(target=odd), threading.Thread(target=even)
    t1.start(); t2.start(); t1.join(); t2.join()
    return out

assert run() == list(range(1, 11))
Two semaphores act as a baton. Alternatives: a Condition with a shared turn variable, or two Events. The point is showing you can order threads explicitly.
Concept Daemon vs non-daemon threads?
The interpreter waits for every non-daemon thread to finish before exiting. Daemon threads (daemon=True) are killed abruptly at exit, without running finally blocks, so files may not be flushed and transactions not committed. Use daemon threads only for disposable background work like heartbeats or cache warmers. For anything that must finish cleanly, use non-daemon threads with an explicit shutdown signal (an Event) and join().

multiprocessing

  • Separate processes, each with its own interpreter and GIL, give true CPU parallelism.
  • Start methods: fork (copy-on-write; fast, but unsafe with threads, since locks held by other threads are copied in a locked state), spawn (a fresh interpreter that re-imports the main module, so you need the if __name__ == "__main__": guard; the default on Windows and macOS), and forkserver (the default on Linux since 3.14).
  • Arguments and results are pickled across a pipe. That overhead means each task should be coarse-grained. Lambdas and local functions can't be pickled.
  • Sharing: Queue, Pipe, Value/Array (shared ctypes), shared_memory.SharedMemory (zero-copy buffers, works well with numpy), and Manager (proxy objects; slow).
Interview check · 2 questions
Why is fork dangerous in a multithreaded program?
Only the thread that called fork exists in the child, but the child gets a copy of all memory, including locks held by other threads (the logging lock, malloc arenas). Those are never released, so the child can deadlock. Use spawn or forkserver. 3.12+ warns when you fork with threads running.
Deep-dive You have a 64-core machine and a CPU-heavy pure-Python transform over 100M records. Design the parallelization.
Use a ProcessPoolExecutor (or multiprocessing Pool) with about one worker per core. Send chunks (say 10k records), not single items, to amortize pickling and IPC. Better still, send references (file paths and byte ranges) and let workers read the data themselves, so results come back small. Use shared_memory or memory-mapped numpy for large read-only arrays. Use fork or forkserver to share warm state. Measure serialization overhead. First, try vectorizing (numpy/polars), which can beat 64 processes. Also consider the free-threaded build when the dependencies support it.

concurrent.futures: the high-level API

from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor, ProcessPoolExecutor, as_completed

with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=16) as pool:          # I/O
    futures = {pool.submit(fetch, url): url for url in urls}
    for fut in as_completed(futures):                      # results in completion order
        try: data = fut.result()
        except Exception as e: log.error("%s failed: %s", futures[fut], e)

with ProcessPoolExecutor() as pool:                        # CPU; default worker count = os.process_cpu_count()
    results = list(pool.map(crunch, chunks, chunksize=64)) # map keeps input order; chunksize cuts IPC overhead
Interview check · 1 question
Concept How do you choose the worker count for thread pools and process pools?
CPU-bound (processes): about the number of cores (os.process_cpu_count(); more only adds context switching). I/O-bound (threads): many more than the cores. Estimate with workers ≈ cores × (1 + wait_time / compute_time), then cap by downstream limits (DB pool size, API rate limits, file descriptors). ThreadPoolExecutor defaults to min(32, cpu + 4). Always load-test, because the right number depends on the system.

18 asyncio Deep Dive

25 min · coroutines, the event loop, Tasks, how await works, structured concurrency, pitfalls

asyncio gives you single-threaded cooperative multitasking. Tasks voluntarily yield at await, and one event loop multiplexes I/O with the OS selector: epoll on Linux, kqueue on macOS, IOCP on Windows.

The building blocks

  • Coroutine function: async def f(). Calling it returns a coroutine object and runs nothing.
  • Awaitable: a coroutine, a Task, a Future, or anything with __await__.
  • Future: a placeholder for a result that will be set later. It holds callbacks that run when it is done.
  • Task: a Future subclass that drives a coroutine on the loop. Concurrency only happens through Tasks.
  • Event loop: runs callbacks, polls the selector for I/O readiness, and manages timers.
Under the hood: how await actually works

Coroutines are built on the same machinery as generators: a suspendable frame. await x is roughly yield from x.__await__().

Task.__step():
    result = coro.send(None)        # run the coroutine until it suspends
      ↳ deep inside, some `await future` reaches Future.__await__, which does `yield self`
    the yielded Future bubbles up to the Task
    Task: future.add_done_callback(self.__wakeup)   → the task is now parked (no CPU used)

Event loop iteration (_run_once):
    1. compute a timeout from the nearest scheduled timer
    2. events = selector.select(timeout)       ← the only place the loop blocks (waiting on the OS)
    3. for each ready socket: run its callback → e.g. future.set_result(data)
    4. set_result schedules the done callbacks (call_soon) → Task.__wakeup → __step → coro.send(...)
    5. run all ready callbacks (FIFO)

So the "magic" is generators, a queue of callbacks, and an OS readiness API. Nothing runs in parallel. A coroutine runs uninterrupted until its next await that actually suspends.

Interview check · 2 questions
How does asyncio achieve concurrency in a single thread?
Coroutines suspend at await points, yielding Futures back to the Task that drives them. The event loop blocks only in selector.select() waiting for I/O readiness or timers, then resumes the tasks whose Futures completed. While one task waits on the network, others run. It is concurrency through interleaving, not parallelism.
Coroutine vs Task vs Future?
A coroutine is a suspendable function execution and runs only when awaited or wrapped. A Future is a low-level result placeholder with callbacks. A Task is a Future that schedules and steps a coroutine on the loop. create_task starts it running concurrently right away.

Essential patterns

import asyncio

async def fetch(client, url):
    r = await client.get(url)          # suspends here; other tasks run in the meantime
    return r.text

async def main():
    async with httpx.AsyncClient() as client:
        # 1) gather: run concurrently, results in input order
        pages = await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(client, u) for u in urls), return_exceptions=True)

        # 2) TaskGroup (3.11): structured concurrency. If one task fails, the others are cancelled
        #    and the failures are raised as an ExceptionGroup
        async with asyncio.TaskGroup() as tg:
            t1 = tg.create_task(fetch(client, a))
            t2 = tg.create_task(fetch(client, b))
        print(t1.result(), t2.result())

        # 3) limit concurrency
        sem = asyncio.Semaphore(10)
        async def bounded(u):
            async with sem:
                return await fetch(client, u)

        # 4) timeouts (3.11)
        async with asyncio.timeout(5):
            await fetch(client, slow)

        # 5) process results as they complete
        for coro in asyncio.as_completed(tasks): print(await coro)

asyncio.run(main())    # creates the loop, runs main, cancels leftover tasks, closes the loop
Interview check · 5 questions
gather vs TaskGroup vs wait?
gather returns results in order. By default the first exception propagates while the other tasks keep running; with return_exceptions=True errors come back as values. A TaskGroup gives structured concurrency: on failure it cancels its siblings, raises an ExceptionGroup, and guarantees no orphan tasks. wait is low-level: it returns (done, pending) sets, with modes FIRST_COMPLETED, FIRST_EXCEPTION, and ALL_COMPLETED.
Coding Fetch 1,000 URLs with at most 50 in flight, a per-request timeout, and retries. Return results and errors separately.
import asyncio

async def fetch_all(urls, fetch, limit=50, timeout=5.0, retries=2):
    sem = asyncio.Semaphore(limit)
    results, errors = {}, {}

    async def one(url):
        async with sem:                                   # at most `limit` concurrent requests
            for attempt in range(retries + 1):
                try:
                    async with asyncio.timeout(timeout):  # 3.11+
                        results[url] = await fetch(url)
                        return
                except (TimeoutError, ConnectionError) as e:
                    if attempt == retries:
                        errors[url] = repr(e)
                    else:
                        await asyncio.sleep(0.1 * 2 ** attempt)

    async with asyncio.TaskGroup() as tg:                 # one() never raises, so siblings aren't cancelled
        for u in urls:
            tg.create_task(one(u))
    return results, errors

async def fake_fetch(url):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.001)
    if url.endswith("7"): raise ConnectionError("boom")
    return len(url)

res, err = asyncio.run(fetch_all([f"u{i}" for i in range(100)], fake_fetch))
assert len(res) == 90 and len(err) == 10
Better at large scale: a fixed pool of 50 worker tasks reading from an asyncio.Queue. Creating 1M tasks up front costs memory even when a semaphore limits them.
Coding Write an async generator that streams every item from a paginated API.
async def paginate(get_page, page_size=100):
    cursor = None
    while True:
        page = await get_page(cursor=cursor, limit=page_size)   # get_page returns {"items": [...], "next": ...}
        for item in page["items"]:
            yield item                                            # the caller can stop early: no wasted pages
        cursor = page.get("next")
        if not cursor:
            return

# usage:  async for item in paginate(client.list_users): ...
Follow-ups: prefetch the next page concurrently while yielding the current one (a task plus a buffer), and respect rate limits.
Deep-dive Why does asyncio.run() fail in Jupyter with "cannot be called from a running event loop"?
Jupyter (IPython's kernel) already runs an event loop on that thread, and asyncio allows only one running loop per thread. In notebooks, use top-level await main(). In library code, never call asyncio.run from code that might itself be called from async code. Expose an async def API and let the application own the loop. The nest_asyncio patch exists, but it's a hack.
Coding Write a "single-flight" helper: if 100 coroutines request the same key at once, the expensive fetch runs only once.
import asyncio

class SingleFlight:
    def __init__(self): self._inflight: dict[str, asyncio.Task] = {}
    async def do(self, key, fetch):
        task = self._inflight.get(key)
        if task is None:
            task = asyncio.create_task(fetch(key))
            self._inflight[key] = task
            task.add_done_callback(lambda _: self._inflight.pop(key, None))   # allow refetch next time
        return await asyncio.shield(task)    # one caller being cancelled doesn't cancel the shared fetch

calls = 0
async def fetch(key):
    global calls; calls += 1
    await asyncio.sleep(0.01); return key.upper()

async def main():
    sf = SingleFlight()
    results = await asyncio.gather(*(sf.do("a", fetch) for _ in range(100)))
    assert results == ["A"] * 100 and calls == 1
asyncio.run(main())
This protects databases and caches from the "thundering herd" when a hot cache key expires. Go's singleflight package works the same way.

Mixing in blocking code

data = await asyncio.to_thread(blocking_io, path)        # runs in the default thread pool (3.9)
loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
result = await loop.run_in_executor(process_pool, cpu_heavy, x)
asyncio.run_coroutine_threadsafe(coro, loop)              # submit to the loop from another thread

Cancellation

task.cancel() throws CancelledError into the coroutine at its current await. It is a subclass of BaseException since 3.8, so except Exception doesn't swallow it. Always clean up in finally, and re-raise CancelledError if you catch it. Use asyncio.shield() to protect an inner operation from an outer cancellation.

Interview check · 1 question
Follow-up How do you implement graceful shutdown for an asyncio worker service on SIGTERM?
async def main():
    stop = asyncio.Event()
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    loop.add_signal_handler(signal.SIGTERM, stop.set)     # POSIX; on Windows rely on KeyboardInterrupt
    workers = [asyncio.create_task(worker(q)) for _ in range(10)]
    await stop.wait()                 # 1. stop accepting new work (close the server / stop consuming)
    await asyncio.wait_for(q.join(), timeout=25)   # 2. drain in-flight work within the grace period
    for w in workers: w.cancel()      # 3. cancel the workers
    await asyncio.gather(*workers, return_exceptions=True)
    await client.aclose()             # 4. close connections and flush telemetry
Keep the timeout below the orchestrator's grace period (Kubernetes defaults to 30 s).

Pitfalls

Trap: the classic asyncio mistakes
  • Blocking the loop: time.sleep, requests.get, heavy CPU work, or synchronous DB drivers inside async def freeze every task. Use async libraries, to_thread, or an executor. asyncio.run(main(), debug=True) logs callbacks slower than 100 ms.
  • Forgetting await: fetch() without await creates a coroutine that never runs ("coroutine was never awaited" warning).
  • Fire-and-forget tasks get garbage-collected: the loop holds only a weak reference to tasks. Keep a strong reference (a set plus add_done_callback(set.discard)) or use a TaskGroup.
  • Sequential awaits (await a(); await b()) run one after another. Use gather or a TaskGroup for concurrency.
  • Unbounded concurrency: 10k simultaneous requests leads to rate limits or running out of file descriptors. Use a Semaphore or a bounded worker queue.
  • asyncio primitives (asyncio.Lock, Queue) are not thread-safe. They're only for coordination inside one loop.
# Producer/consumer workers with asyncio.Queue
async def worker(q):
    while True:
        item = await q.get()
        try: await process(item)
        finally: q.task_done()

q = asyncio.Queue(maxsize=100)                 # bounded → backpressure
workers = [asyncio.create_task(worker(q)) for _ in range(10)]
for it in items: await q.put(it)
await q.join()                                 # wait until every item is processed
for w in workers: w.cancel()

Async iteration: async for uses __aiter__/__anext__, and async generators are async def functions containing yield. uvloop is a faster drop-in event loop built on libuv. Alternatives: trio and anyio, the libraries that introduced structured concurrency.

Interview check · 4 questions
What happens if you call a blocking function inside a coroutine?
The whole event loop stalls: no other task progresses, timers fire late, and connections may time out. Fix it with an async equivalent, await asyncio.to_thread(fn), or run_in_executor with a process pool for CPU work.
Is asyncio faster than threads?
Not per operation. Its advantage is scale and control: tasks cost about 1–2 KB versus megabytes of stack per thread, there's no preemption so there are fewer races, and you can have tens of thousands of concurrent connections. For CPU work it's no better than threads, and "async all the way down" is required.
Debug An asyncio API's p99 latency jumps from 20 ms to 2 s under load, while CPU stays low. Hypotheses?
  1. A blocking call in the loop (sync DB driver, requests, file I/O, heavy JSON parsing). Detect it with loop.set_debug(True) plus slow_callback_duration, or py-spy. Fix with an async driver or to_thread.
  2. Connection pool exhausted: requests queue up waiting for a DB or HTTP connection. Check the pool metrics.
  3. Unbounded concurrency hitting a downstream rate limit, so retries pile up.
  4. A single slow downstream with no timeouts.
  5. GC pauses with large heaps (use gc.callbacks to time them).
Coding Show with timing that awaiting coroutines one by one is sequential, while tasks are concurrent.
import asyncio, time

async def work(): await asyncio.sleep(0.1)

async def sequential():
    await work(); await work(); await work()           # about 0.3 s: each await finishes before the next starts

async def concurrent():
    tasks = [asyncio.create_task(work()) for _ in range(3)]   # all start NOW
    for t in tasks: await t                             # about 0.1 s total

for fn in (sequential, concurrent):
    t0 = time.perf_counter(); asyncio.run(fn())
    print(fn.__name__, round(time.perf_counter() - t0, 2))
await coro() runs the coroutine to completion before the next line. create_task schedules it on the loop immediately. That's the difference between async-looking code and actually concurrent code.

More interview questions

Concept How do you carry a request ID through all the logs of one request in an asyncio server?
Use contextvars.ContextVar. Each asyncio Task runs in a copy of the context taken when the task was created, so values set in one request don't leak into others, and child tasks inherit them. Thread-locals don't work, because many requests share one thread. Set the variable in middleware (request_id.set(uuid4())) and read it in a logging.Filter that adds it to every record.

19 Type Hints & Static Typing

15 min · annotations at runtime, generics, variance, Protocol, TypedDict, overload, ParamSpec, PEP 695

Type hints are not enforced at runtime. They're metadata for tools (mypy, pyright, IDEs) and for runtime libraries that choose to read them (pydantic, FastAPI, dataclasses).

The essentials

from typing import Any, Optional, Union, Callable, Iterable, Iterator, Literal, Final, TypeAlias, Self, overload
from collections.abc import Sequence, Mapping

def greet(name: str, times: int = 1) -> str: ...
def find(id: int) -> User | None: ...                # 3.10: X | Y replaces Optional/Union
def apply(f: Callable[[int, str], bool]) -> None: ...
def total(xs: Iterable[float]) -> float: ...         # accept abstract types, return concrete ones
items: list[dict[str, int]] = []                     # 3.9: built-in generics
MODE: Final = "prod"
def open_(mode: Literal["r", "w"]) -> None: ...
class Builder:
    def set(self, k: str) -> Self: ...               # 3.11: return type for fluent/chaining methods
Interview check · 2 questions
Concept Does Optional[int] mean "an optional argument"?
No, and this is a common confusion. Optional[int] means only "int or None" (the same as int | None). Whether an argument is optional to pass depends on having a default value. def f(x: int | None) must still be passed (it can be None), and def f(x: int = 0) is optional but never None. The usual combination is def f(x: int | None = None). Prefer X | None, which is clearer.
Concept Why use -> Self instead of -> "Builder" for a fluent method?
With -> "Builder", a subclass's chained call is typed as returning the base class, so SubBuilder().set(x).sub_only() fails type checking. typing.Self (3.11) means "the type of the actual instance", so subclasses keep their own type through the chain. Use it for fluent setters, __enter__, copy(), and alternative constructors that return cls(...).

Generics

# 3.12+ syntax (PEP 695)
def first[T](xs: Sequence[T]) -> T: return xs[0]
class Stack[T]:
    def __init__(self) -> None: self.items: list[T] = []
    def push(self, x: T) -> None: self.items.append(x)
    def pop(self) -> T: return self.items.pop()
type JSON = dict[str, "JSON"] | list["JSON"] | str | int | float | bool | None   # lazily evaluated alias

def biggest[T: (int, float)](a: T, b: T) -> T: ...        # constrained to int or float
def longest[S: Sized](a: S, b: S) -> S: ...                # bounded by Sized

# Pre-3.12 equivalent
from typing import TypeVar, Generic
T = TypeVar("T")
class Box(Generic[T]): ...
Variance
  • Covariant (read-only producers): Sequence[Dog] is a Sequence[Animal]. Also tuple, frozenset, and the return types of Callable.
  • Invariant (mutable containers): list[Dog] is not a list[Animal], or you could append a Cat to it. Also dict and set.
  • Contravariant (consumers): a Callable[[Animal], None] is acceptable where a Callable[[Dog], None] is expected. Parameter types are contravariant.
PEP 695 type parameters infer variance automatically.
Interview check · 3 questions
Why is list[Dog] not a subtype of list[Animal]?
Lists are mutable, so they're invariant. If it were allowed, a function taking list[Animal] could append a Cat to your Dog list. Read-only Sequence[Dog] is covariant and is a valid Sequence[Animal].
Coding Type a generic in-memory Repository[T] that works for any entity with an id: int.
from typing import Protocol
from dataclasses import dataclass

class HasId(Protocol):
    @property
    def id(self) -> int: ...

class Repository[T: HasId]:                    # 3.12 syntax: T is bounded by HasId
    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._items: dict[int, T] = {}
    def add(self, item: T) -> None:
        self._items[item.id] = item
    def get(self, id: int) -> T | None:
        return self._items.get(id)
    def all(self) -> list[T]:
        return list(self._items.values())

@dataclass
class User:
    id: int
    name: str

repo = Repository[User]()
repo.add(User(1, "a"))
u = repo.get(1)                                # the checker infers User | None
Deep-dive TypeVar bound vs constraints: what's the difference?
T: Number (bound) means T can be any subtype of Number, and the precise type is preserved, so f(bool_value) returns bool. T: (int, str) (constraints) means T must be exactly one of those types, and a subtype is resolved to the listed type. Use bound for "anything with this capability" and constraints for "this function supports these specific types", like AnyStr.

Structural types and dict shapes

from typing import Protocol, TypedDict, NotRequired, NamedTuple

class Readable(Protocol):
    def read(self, n: int = -1) -> bytes: ...
def load(src: Readable) -> bytes: ...      # any object with a matching read() satisfies it

class Movie(TypedDict):                    # the shape of a dict (JSON payloads); still a plain dict at runtime
    title: str
    year: NotRequired[int]
Interview check · 1 question
Protocol vs ABC for an interface?
A Protocol is structural, which suits third-party types you can't change, and keeps coupling loose. An ABC is nominal, which suits shared implementation (mixin methods) and enforcing abstract methods when an object is created.

Advanced tools

@overload
def get(key: str) -> str: ...
@overload
def get(key: str, default: int) -> str | int: ...
def get(key, default=None): ...            # the single real implementation

# Decorators that keep the wrapped function's signature: ParamSpec
from typing import ParamSpec, Concatenate
def logged[**P, R](f: Callable[P, R]) -> Callable[P, R]:
    def inner(*args: P.args, **kwargs: P.kwargs) -> R: return f(*args, **kwargs)
    return inner

from typing import TypeGuard, TypeIs, cast, assert_never, override, NewType
UserId = NewType("UserId", int)            # a distinct type to the checker; plain int at runtime (zero cost)
def is_str_list(v: list[object]) -> TypeIs[list[str]]: ...   # 3.13: narrowing in both branches
match status:
    case "ok": ...
    case _: assert_never(status)           # exhaustiveness check
if TYPE_CHECKING: from heavy import Model  # import only for the type checker
Interview check · 1 question
Concept NewType("UserId", int) vs a type alias UserId = int?
An alias is just another name. The checker treats UserId and int as identical, so passing an OrderId where a UserId is expected isn't caught. NewType creates a distinct type for the checker: you must wrap explicitly (UserId(5)), and mixing IDs becomes an error. At runtime, NewType is an identity function with zero cost, and the value is a plain int.

Annotations at runtime

  • Annotations are stored in __annotations__. Read them with typing.get_type_hints(), or annotationlib.get_annotations() in 3.14.
  • 3.14 (PEP 649/749): annotations are evaluated lazily, on first access. Forward references work without quotes, and from __future__ import annotations (which turns all annotations into strings) is no longer needed.
  • Gradual typing: Any turns checking off. Prefer object ("anything, but you must narrow it before use"). Run mypy/pyright with --strict in CI.
Interview check · 4 questions
Does Python enforce type hints?
No. The interpreter stores them and otherwise ignores them. Static checkers verify them before runtime, and libraries such as pydantic or beartype can validate at runtime on purpose.
Any vs object?
Any is compatible with everything in both directions, so it disables checking. object accepts any value but allows only the operations every object has, which forces you to narrow with isinstance. Prefer object for "accepts anything".
Deep-dive How do FastAPI and pydantic turn type hints into validation at runtime?
When the class or route is created, they read the annotations (typing.get_type_hints, or the 3.14 annotationlib) and walk the types (get_origin and get_args for list[int], unions, Annotated[...] metadata). From that they compile a validation schema once. pydantic v2 compiles it into a Rust core (pydantic-core) for speed. On each request, the payload is validated and coerced against the compiled schema. FastAPI also reads the signature to decide what comes from the path, query, body, or a dependency.
Follow-up How do you introduce static typing to a 500k-line untyped codebase?
Add mypy or pyright to CI in non-strict mode first, with a baseline so existing errors don't block anyone. Configure it per module: strict for new or critical packages, lenient for legacy ones ([[tool.mypy.overrides]]). Annotate public interfaces and hot spots first (service boundaries, models). Install third-party stubs (types-*). Use MonkeyType or pyannotate to generate annotations from runtime traces. Ratchet: the error count may only go down. Ban new Any in reviews.

20 Standard Library Power Tools

15 min · collections, heapq, bisect, enum, pathlib, logging, datetime, and more

collections

from collections import Counter, defaultdict, deque, OrderedDict, ChainMap, namedtuple

Counter("mississippi").most_common(2)     # [('i', 4), ('s', 4)]; supports + - & |
graph = defaultdict(list); graph[u].append(v)   # missing keys call the factory (via __missing__)
dq = deque(maxlen=3); dq.append(1); dq.appendleft(0); dq.rotate(1)   # O(1) at both ends
lru = OrderedDict(); lru.move_to_end(k); lru.popitem(last=False)     # LRU-cache building blocks
cfg = ChainMap(cli_args, env, defaults)  # layered lookup with no copying
Point = namedtuple("Point", "x y")
Under the hood deque is a doubly linked list of blocks of 64 pointers. That gives O(1) appends and pops at both ends with good cache locality, but indexing into the middle is O(n). OrderedDict keeps an extra doubly linked list for O(1) reordering. defaultdict overrides __missing__, which dict.__getitem__ calls on a miss (and .get() does not).
Interview check · 4 questions
Implement an LRU cache.
class LRU:
    def __init__(self, cap): self.cap, self.d = cap, OrderedDict()
    def get(self, k):
        if k not in self.d: return None
        self.d.move_to_end(k); return self.d[k]
    def put(self, k, v):
        self.d[k] = v; self.d.move_to_end(k)
        if len(self.d) > self.cap: self.d.popitem(last=False)
All operations are O(1). Without OrderedDict, use a dict plus a hand-written doubly linked list. A plain dict also works, because it keeps insertion order: delete the key and reinsert it on access, and evict next(iter(d)).
When would you use deque instead of list?
For queues and sliding windows: O(1) append and pop at both ends, versus O(n) pop(0) on a list. maxlen gives a bounded buffer. Use a list when you need random access by index.
Coding Sliding-window maximum in O(n).
from collections import deque

def window_max(nums: list[int], k: int) -> list[int]:
    dq: deque[int] = deque()            # holds indices; their values are decreasing from front to back
    out = []
    for i, x in enumerate(nums):
        while dq and nums[dq[-1]] <= x: dq.pop()      # smaller values can never be the max again
        dq.append(i)
        if dq[0] <= i - k: dq.popleft()               # the front fell out of the window
        if i >= k - 1: out.append(nums[dq[0]])
    return out

assert window_max([1,3,-1,-3,5,3,6,7], 3) == [3,3,5,5,6,7]
Coding Course schedule: given prerequisites, return a valid order or detect a cycle.
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def course_order(n: int, prereqs: list[tuple[int, int]]) -> list[int] | None:
    graph, indeg = defaultdict(list), [0] * n
    for course, pre in prereqs:
        graph[pre].append(course); indeg[course] += 1
    q = deque(i for i in range(n) if indeg[i] == 0)     # Kahn's algorithm: start with courses that have no prerequisites
    order = []
    while q:
        u = q.popleft(); order.append(u)
        for v in graph[u]:
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0: q.append(v)
    return order if len(order) == n else None           # leftovers mean a cycle

assert course_order(4, [(1, 0), (2, 0), (3, 1), (3, 2)]) in ([0, 1, 2, 3], [0, 2, 1, 3])
assert course_order(2, [(0, 1), (1, 0)]) is None
# stdlib: graphlib.TopologicalSorter(graph).static_order() raises CycleError on cycles

heapq and bisect

import heapq, bisect
h = []
heapq.heappush(h, (priority, count, task))   # a min-heap stored in a plain list; h[0] is the smallest
heapq.heappop(h)                              # O(log n). A tie-breaker count avoids comparing tasks
heapq.nlargest(3, data, key=f)                # top-k in O(n log k)
heapq.merge(*sorted_iters)                    # lazy k-way merge
# max-heap: push negated keys (3.14 adds heapq.heappush_max and friends)

i = bisect.bisect_left(sorted_list, x)        # binary search, O(log n)
bisect.insort(sorted_list, x)                 # O(n) insert (the memmove dominates)
Interview check · 4 questions
Coding Top-k frequent words. Ties are broken alphabetically. Target O(n log k).
from collections import Counter
import heapq

def top_k(words: list[str], k: int) -> list[str]:
    counts = Counter(words)
    return heapq.nsmallest(k, counts, key=lambda w: (-counts[w], w))   # high count first, then alphabetical

assert top_k(["i","love","code","i","love","py"], 2) == ["i", "love"]
nsmallest keeps a heap of size k, giving O(n log k). sorted(...)[:k] is O(n log n), which is fine for small n.
Coding Minimum meeting rooms needed for a list of intervals.
import heapq
def min_rooms(intervals: list[tuple[int, int]]) -> int:
    ends: list[int] = []                    # min-heap of end times of the rooms in use
    for start, end in sorted(intervals):
        if ends and ends[0] <= start:
            heapq.heapreplace(ends, end)    # reuse the room that frees up earliest
        else:
            heapq.heappush(ends, end)
    return len(ends)
assert min_rooms([(0, 30), (5, 10), (15, 20)]) == 2
Coding A time-based key-value store: set(key, value, t) and get(key, t), which returns the latest value at or before t.
import bisect
from collections import defaultdict

class TimeMap:
    def __init__(self):
        self.times = defaultdict(list)     # timestamps arrive in increasing order per key
        self.vals = defaultdict(list)
    def set(self, key, value, t):
        self.times[key].append(t); self.vals[key].append(value)
    def get(self, key, t):
        i = bisect.bisect_right(self.times[key], t)   # O(log n)
        return self.vals[key][i - 1] if i else ""

tm = TimeMap(); tm.set("a", "x", 1); tm.set("a", "y", 4)
assert (tm.get("a", 3), tm.get("a", 5), tm.get("a", 0)) == ("x", "y", "")
Coding Shortest path in a weighted graph (Dijkstra) with heapq.
import heapq
def dijkstra(graph: dict[str, list[tuple[str, int]]], src: str) -> dict[str, int]:
    dist = {src: 0}
    pq = [(0, src)]
    while pq:
        d, u = heapq.heappop(pq)
        if d > dist.get(u, float("inf")): continue        # stale entry (we found a shorter path already)
        for v, w in graph.get(u, []):
            nd = d + w
            if nd < dist.get(v, float("inf")):
                dist[v] = nd
                heapq.heappush(pq, (nd, v))                  # "lazy decrease-key": push duplicates, skip stale ones
    return dist

g = {"A": [("B", 4), ("C", 1)], "C": [("B", 2), ("D", 5)], "B": [("D", 1)]}
assert dijkstra(g, "A") == {"A": 0, "C": 1, "B": 3, "D": 4}
O((V + E) log V). heapq has no decrease-key, so you push duplicates and skip stale entries.

Perm.R | Perm.W           # bit flags

import logging

Interview check · 3 questions
Why is log.info("x %s", v) better than an f-string?
Formatting is deferred until the record is actually emitted, so disabled levels cost almost nothing. Aggregators can also group messages by their constant template, and the args are available to handlers and filters.
Follow-up How do you log safely from a multiprocessing application?
Multiple processes writing to the same file handler interleave and corrupt lines, and rotation breaks. Use logging.handlers.QueueHandler in every worker, sending to a multiprocessing.Queue, with one QueueListener in the main process that owns the real handlers. Alternatively, log to stdout as structured JSON and let the platform (Docker, Kubernetes, a log shipper) aggregate it.
Coding Parse web-server log lines and report the count per status code plus the top 2 slowest paths.
import re
from collections import Counter, defaultdict

LINE = re.compile(r'"(?P<method>\w+) (?P<path>\S+) [^"]*" (?P<status>\d{3}) (?P<ms>\d+)ms')
logs = [
    '1.2.3.4 - - [x] "GET /api/users HTTP/1.1" 200 120ms',
    '1.2.3.4 - - [x] "POST /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 500 950ms',
    '1.2.3.4 - - [x] "GET /api/users HTTP/1.1" 200 80ms',
    'garbage line',
]
status, worst = Counter(), defaultdict(int)
for line in logs:
    if m := LINE.search(line):                        # skip malformed lines safely
        status[m["status"]] += 1
        worst[m["path"]] = max(worst[m["path"]], int(m["ms"]))
assert status == {"200": 2, "500": 1}
assert sorted(worst, key=worst.get, reverse=True)[:2] == ["/api/orders", "/api/users"]
Named groups keep the regex readable. Compile once outside the loop. For huge files, stream the lines (ch.07).

21 Performance & Profiling

15 min · measure first, profilers, micro-optimizations that matter, native speedups

Rule 1: measure before you optimize

python -m timeit -s "xs=list(range(1000))" "sum(xs)"       # micro-benchmarks (takes the best of several runs)
python -m cProfile -s cumtime app.py                        # deterministic function-level profiler
python -m cProfile -o out.prof app.py; snakeviz out.prof    # visualize the profile
py-spy top --pid 1234      # sampling profiler: attaches to production processes, low overhead
py-spy record -o flame.svg -- python app.py                 # flame graph
# 3.15: `python -m profiling.sampling` is a built-in sampling profiler (PEP 799)
# line_profiler (@profile, per-line timing), scalene (CPU + memory + GPU), memray (memory)
import cProfile, pstats
with cProfile.Profile() as pr:
    work()
pstats.Stats(pr).sort_stats("cumulative").print_stats(15)
# tottime = time spent in the function itself; cumtime = including the functions it calls
Interview check · 3 questions
How would you find why a Python service is slow in production?
Start with metrics and traces to see whether the time is spent in I/O, CPU, or waiting on locks. Attach py-spy (sampling, safe in production) for flame graphs. Check the DB (slow-query logs, N+1 queries) and external calls. Reproduce with cProfile or line_profiler locally. For memory, use tracemalloc or memray. Then fix the biggest bottleneck first and measure again.
Coding This function takes 40 s on 100k orders. Optimize it.
def matched(orders, blocked): return [o for o in orders if o.user_id not in [b.user_id for b in blocked]]
The inner list is rebuilt for every order, and in on a list is O(m), so the total is O(n·m). 
def matched(orders, blocked):
    blocked_ids = {b.user_id for b in blocked}          # build once: O(m)
    return [o for o in orders if o.user_id not in blocked_ids]   # O(1) lookup → O(n + m) total
This kind of change gives a 1000× win. Always look for the algorithmic fix before micro-optimizing.
Debug One API endpoint pins a CPU at 100% for some inputs. py-spy shows time inside re. What's going on?
Probably catastrophic backtracking (ReDoS). A pattern with nested quantifiers such as (a+)+$ or (\w+\s?)*$ takes exponential time on inputs that almost match. Fixes: rewrite the pattern without ambiguous nested repetition, anchor it, limit the input length, use possessive quantifiers or atomic groups (supported in re since 3.11: (?>...), a++), or use a linear-time engine (google-re2). It's also a security issue if users control the input.

Algorithmic wins first

  • Use the right data structure: a set/dict for membership, deque for queues, heapq for top-k, bisect for sorted lookups.
  • Avoid accidental O(n²): x in list inside a loop, list.pop(0), string +=, repeated sorted() calls.
  • Cache: functools.cache, memoization, precomputation. Batch I/O: bulk DB queries, connection pooling, avoiding N+1 queries.
Interview check · 1 question
Coding Climbing stairs (1 or 2 steps): ways(n). Show the memoized and bottom-up versions. Which is better in Python?
from functools import cache
@cache
def ways_memo(n: int) -> int:
    return 1 if n <= 1 else ways_memo(n - 1) + ways_memo(n - 2)

def ways(n: int) -> int:                 # bottom-up, O(1) memory, no recursion limit
    a, b = 1, 1
    for _ in range(n - 1): a, b = b, a + b
    return b if n >= 1 else 1

assert ways(10) == ways_memo(10) == 89 and ways(5000) > 0   # ways_memo(5000) would hit RecursionError
In Python, prefer bottom-up for deep problems: there's no recursion limit, and lower call overhead. Memoization is quicker to write and fine for shallow recursion.

CPython-level speedups (why they work)

TechniqueWhy it's faster
Built-ins like sum, map, sorted, str.join, anyThe loop runs in C, with no bytecode dispatch per element
Comprehensions instead of a loop with appendThe LIST_APPEND opcode avoids an attribute lookup and a method call
Local variables in hot loopsLOAD_FAST (an array index) instead of dict lookups
Avoid attribute chains in tight loopsEach . is a lookup. Hoist obj.method outside the loop
__slots__Smaller objects, fixed-offset access
Generators and iteratorsLess memory, so fewer cache misses and less GC pressure
Upgrade Python3.11 was about 25% faster than 3.10 thanks to the specializing interpreter, and 3.12–3.14 keep adding gains
Interview check · 4 questions
Why is sum(xs) faster than a for loop?
The for loop runs several bytecodes per element through the eval loop: FOR_ITER, STORE, LOAD, BINARY_OP. sum iterates in C, with a fast path for exact ints and floats that skips creating objects.
Deep-dive A pure-Python function is called 10M times in a hot loop. List the optimizations in the order you'd try them.
  1. Can the work be avoided altogether (caching, batching, computing incrementally)?
  2. Better algorithm or data structure.
  3. Push the loop into C: built-ins, comprehensions, map, itertools, str.join, operator.itemgetter.
  4. Vectorize with numpy or polars.
  5. Reduce interpreter overhead: bind globals and methods to locals, inline tiny functions, avoid attribute chains, use __slots__.
  6. Compile: Cython, mypyc, Numba, or Rust via PyO3.
  7. Parallelize with processes.
  8. Upgrade Python, or try PyPy.
Profile before and after each step.
Debug A benchmark shows version B is 15% faster, but production shows no difference. What might be wrong with the benchmark?
No warm-up (the specializing interpreter and caches weren't warm), a single run with noise (turbo boost, other processes), measuring with time.time() instead of perf_counter, an input unrepresentative of production (tiny data fits in cache), dead-code effects, or the benchmarked function isn't the real bottleneck (production is I/O-bound). Use timeit with repeat and take the min, or pyperf (calibrated, with statistics), use realistic data, and confirm against a production profile.
Follow-up When is NumPy slower than plain Python?
On tiny arrays (a few elements), where the per-call overhead (dispatch, creating array objects) dominates. Also with element-by-element Python loops over a NumPy array (each access boxes a scalar), operations that can't be vectorized and need Python callbacks (np.vectorize is just a loop), and repeated np.append (a copy every time). Use NumPy for bulk operations on large arrays.

When pure Python is too slow

  • Vectorize with NumPy, pandas, or polars. The loop moves into C/SIMD, and the GIL is released.
  • Compile: Cython, mypyc, Numba (a JIT for numeric code), or Rust via PyO3/maturin.
  • Run elsewhere: PyPy for long-running pure-Python code, multiprocessing for CPU-bound work, asyncio for I/O-bound work.
Practice Know where your latency budget goes. In typical web services, 90% or more is I/O (DB, network). Profile end-to-end with tracing (OpenTelemetry) before micro-tuning Python code.
Interview check · 1 question
Concept PyPy vs Cython vs a Rust extension (PyO3). How do you choose?
PyPy: zero code changes and great for long-running pure-Python code, but C-extension support is weaker and the community is smaller.
