Cython: add types to hot Python-like code and get C speed, a mature tool used in scientific stacks, but it adds a build step. Rust/PyO3 (maturin): best for new performance-critical components (memory safety, real threads without the GIL), as done by pydantic-core, polars, and ruff, but you need Rust skills. Also consider NumPy/polars vectorization and Numba for numeric loops. Choose based on the profile, the team's skills, and how you'll distribute it.

22 Testing

12 min · pytest, fixtures, parametrize, mocking correctly, test strategy

pytest essentials

# test_cart.py
import pytest

@pytest.fixture
def cart():                       # dependency injection by argument name
    c = Cart()
    yield c                       # code after yield is teardown
    c.clear()

@pytest.fixture(scope="session")  # function (default) | class | module | package | session
def db(): ...

def test_add(cart):
    cart.add("apple", 2)
    assert cart.total() == 2      # assertion rewriting gives detailed diffs on failure

@pytest.mark.parametrize("a,b,expected", [(1, 2, 3), (0, 0, 0), (-1, 1, 0)])
def test_sum(a, b, expected):
    assert add(a, b) == expected

def test_raises():
    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="negative"):
        withdraw(-5)

Put shared fixtures in conftest.py (discovered automatically). Built-in fixtures: tmp_path, monkeypatch, capsys, caplog. Markers: skip, xfail, and custom ones selected with -m. Plugins: pytest-cov, pytest-xdist (-n auto), pytest-asyncio, hypothesis (property-based testing).

Interview check · 5 questions
Fixture scopes, and why they matter?
Scope decides how often setup runs: per test, class, module, or session. Wider scopes speed up suites that need expensive resources (a DB, a container) but risk state leaking between tests. Use transactions with rollback or cleanup to keep tests isolated.
Deep-dive How does pytest produce detailed failure messages from a plain assert?
pytest installs an import hook (a meta-path finder) for test modules and conftest files. It parses the source into an AST and rewrites each assert into code that saves intermediate subexpression values and builds a detailed message on failure. Rewritten bytecode is cached in __pycache__ with a pytest-specific tag. That's why helper modules outside tests need pytest.register_assert_rewrite.
Debug A test passes alone but fails when the whole suite runs. Common causes?
Shared state leaking between tests: module-level globals or caches (lru_cache not cleared), singletons, environment variables, monkeypatches not undone (use the monkeypatch fixture, which auto-reverts), a DB not rolled back, the order of a session-scoped fixture, leftover temp files, or time and randomness. Find it with pytest -p no:randomly versus pytest-randomly, bisecting with --lf, or pytest -x on different orders.
Concept What is property-based testing? Give an example.
Instead of hand-picking examples, you state a property that must always hold, and the tool (Hypothesis) generates hundreds of inputs, including nasty edge cases, then shrinks any failure to a minimal example. 
from hypothesis import given, strategies as st

@given(st.text())
def test_rle_roundtrip(s):
    assert decode(encode(s)) == s          # finds the bug: digits in the input break the RLE format!
Great properties: round-trips (encode/decode, serialize/parse), invariants (sorting keeps the length and order), comparing against a slow reference implementation, and idempotence.
Coding Assert that a function logs a warning, with pytest's caplog.
import logging
log = logging.getLogger("billing")

def charge(amount):
    if amount > 10_000:
        log.warning("large charge: %s", amount)
    return amount

def test_large_charge_warns(caplog):
    with caplog.at_level(logging.WARNING, logger="billing"):
        charge(50_000)
    assert "large charge: 50000" in caplog.text
    assert caplog.records[0].levelname == "WARNING"
Testing logs is valid for audit or alerting behavior, but don't over-test log wording, which makes tests brittle.

Mocking: patch where the name is looked up

# app/service.py
from app.client import fetch      # binds the name `fetch` inside app.service
def run(): return fetch()

# test
from unittest.mock import patch, MagicMock, AsyncMock
@patch("app.service.fetch")       # ✅ patch the name where it is used
# @patch("app.client.fetch")      # ❌ app.service already holds its own reference to the original
def test_run(mock_fetch):
    mock_fetch.return_value = {"ok": True}
    assert run() == {"ok": True}
    mock_fetch.assert_called_once_with()

m = MagicMock(spec=RealClass)     # spec / autospec: calling a method that doesn't exist raises; catches API drift
m.side_effect = [1, 2, ValueError("boom")]   # successive return values, then an exception
Practice Follow the test pyramid: many fast unit tests, fewer integration tests (real DB in a container), and a few end-to-end tests. Mock only at boundaries (network, clock, randomness), not your own internals. Over-mocking tests the implementation instead of the behavior. Use dependency injection to make code testable without patching.
Interview check · 4 questions
Why does my @patch not take effect?
You patched the module where the name is defined, but the code under test imported the name with from x import y, so it holds its own reference. Patch "module_under_test.y". Patch replaces a name in a namespace, not the object itself.
Coding Make this testable and test it: def is_expired(token): return token.expires_at < datetime.now(timezone.utc)
from datetime import datetime, timedelta, timezone
from typing import Callable

def is_expired(token, now: Callable[[], datetime] = lambda: datetime.now(timezone.utc)) -> bool:
    return token.expires_at < now()          # inject the clock

class T:
    def __init__(self, exp): self.expires_at = exp

FIXED = datetime(2026, 1, 1, tzinfo=timezone.utc)
def test_expired():
    assert is_expired(T(FIXED - timedelta(seconds=1)), now=lambda: FIXED)
def test_not_expired():
    assert not is_expired(T(FIXED + timedelta(seconds=1)), now=lambda: FIXED)
test_expired(); test_not_expired()
Alternatives: freezegun or time-machine to patch time globally. For HTTP, use respx (httpx) or responses (requests), or inject a client interface. Injecting dependencies beats patching.
Follow-up How do you test code for race conditions?
Make the races likely: N threads hammering a barrier-synchronized critical section (threading.Barrier releases them together), and assert invariants afterwards (no double allocation, the counter equals N). Lower sys.setswitchinterval to force frequent switches. Inject delays at suspected points. For asyncio, interleave with await asyncio.sleep(0). Run the test repeatedly (pytest-repeat). Prefer designs that are easy to test: queues and immutable data.
Coding Test an async function that calls an async HTTP client, using AsyncMock.
import asyncio
from unittest.mock import AsyncMock

async def get_username(client, uid: int) -> str:
    resp = await client.get(f"/users/{uid}")
    return resp["name"].title()

def test_get_username():
    client = AsyncMock()
    client.get.return_value = {"name": "asha"}       # what `await client.get(...)` returns
    assert asyncio.run(get_username(client, 7)) == "Asha"
    client.get.assert_awaited_once_with("/users/7")

test_get_username()
With pytest-asyncio you write async def test_... with @pytest.mark.asyncio instead of asyncio.run.

23 Pythonic Design & Patterns

15 min · idioms, SOLID the Python way, which GoF patterns disappear, architecture

Pythonic idioms

  • Duck typing and EAFP: depend on behavior, not type. try the operation instead of checking first.
  • Protocols over inheritance: implement __iter__, __len__, or __enter__ and your object works with the whole language.
  • Use with for every resource. Use enumerate and zip instead of index loops. Unpack (a, *rest = ...). Use dict.get/setdefault, any/all.
  • "Flat is better than nested". Use guard clauses with early returns. Use @property instead of getters and setters.
  • Keep functions small and pure where you can. Push I/O to the edges ("functional core, imperative shell").
Interview check · 2 questions
Follow-up Duck typing is flexible but risky in big codebases. How do you get the safety back?
Protocols to state the expected structure, plus static type checking in CI. Validate at the boundaries (pydantic). Contract or interface tests that every implementation must pass. Good error messages with early checks. Code review conventions. You keep the flexibility while the tools catch mismatches before runtime.
Concept What does "functional core, imperative shell" mean? Why do seniors like it?
Put the business logic in pure functions or objects (data in, data out, no I/O, no clock, no randomness): the core. Keep a thin outer layer that does the I/O (read from the DB, call the core, write the results): the shell. Benefits: the core is trivially unit-testable without mocks, easy to reason about, and reusable. Fewer integration tests are needed, for the shell only. In Python, the core is often dataclasses plus functions, and the shell is FastAPI handlers, workers, and repositories.

How design patterns translate

PatternPython way
SingletonA module-level instance. Modules are imported once
Strategy / CommandPass a function. Functions are first-class
Factory@classmethod alternative constructors, or a dict mapping names to classes
Decorator (GoF)@decorator functions, or wrapper classes with __getattr__ delegation
IteratorGenerators
ObserverA callback list, with weakref.WeakSet to avoid leaks, or blinker signals
Template methodAn ABC with abstract hooks, or passing functions in
Registry / Plugin__init_subclass__, decorators, entry points
Dependency injectionConstructor arguments and default parameters. Frameworks are rarely needed
Builder / Value objectKeyword arguments with defaults, frozen dataclasses
Interview check · 2 questions
How do you implement a singleton in Python?
Preferred: a module-level instance, since modules are cached in sys.modules. Alternatives: @functools.cache on a get_instance() function, overriding __new__, or a metaclass whose __call__ caches instances. Mention thread safety (a lock around creation) and that singletons make testing harder. Dependency injection is often the better answer.
Design Design a plugin system for a CLI tool so that third parties can add commands.
  • Contract: a Command Protocol (or ABC) with name, help, configure(parser), and run(args) -> int.
  • Discovery: importlib.metadata.entry_points(group="mytool.commands"). Third-party packages declare entry points in their pyproject, so installing a package is all it takes. Built-in commands can register the same way.
  • Loading: load lazily (only the command being run) for fast startup. Isolate failures, so a broken plugin logs a warning instead of crashing the tool.
  • Versioning: an API version check (plugin.api_version), and a small stable SDK package that plugins depend on.
  • Security: plugins run arbitrary code, so document trust and consider an allowlist.

SOLID, briefly

  • Single responsibility: a module or class should have one reason to change.
  • Open/closed: extend through new classes, functions, or plugins instead of editing if type == chains. singledispatch and polymorphism help.
  • Liskov substitution: a subclass must honor the base contract. For example, a Square(Rectangle) whose setters break the base invariants violates it.
  • Interface segregation: small Protocols instead of fat base classes.
  • Dependency inversion: depend on abstractions (Protocols) and inject concrete implementations (a repository, a client).

Code examples for each principle, every major design pattern, and full LLD case studies are in chapters 24–31.

Interview check · 1 question
Composition vs inheritance?
Inheritance models an "is-a" relationship and couples tightly: subclasses depend on base internals (the fragile base class problem). Composition models "has-a": inject collaborators and delegate to them. It's more flexible and easier to test. Use inheritance for true subtypes and framework hooks, and use mixins sparingly.

Architecture topics seniors get asked about

  • Project layout: src/ layout, domain logic separated from frameworks (hexagonal / ports and adapters), configuration from the environment (pydantic-settings), a shared exception hierarchy.
  • Quality tooling: ruff (lint and format), mypy or pyright, pytest with coverage, pre-commit hooks, CI, dependency pinning, vulnerability scanning (pip-audit).
  • Web stack: WSGI (sync, used by Django/Flask through gunicorn) vs ASGI (async, used by FastAPI/Starlette through uvicorn). Know worker models: gunicorn prefork workers with sync or uvicorn workers.
  • Observability: structured logging, metrics, tracing, correlation IDs carried through contextvars.
  • Security: avoid SQL injection (use parameterized queries), use secrets, never unpickle or yaml.load untrusted data (use safe_load), avoid eval, keep dependencies updated.
Interview check · 4 questions
Design How would you structure the codebase of a large Python backend service?
src/acme/
  domain/        entities, value objects, domain services. Pure Python, no framework imports
  application/   use cases (orchestration) and interfaces/ports (repository protocols)
  infrastructure/ DB repositories, HTTP clients, queues. Implement the ports
  api/           FastAPI/Django views, request/response schemas (pydantic)
  config.py      settings from the environment
  main.py        composition root: wire the concrete implementations together
tests/ unit/ integration/ e2e/
Dependencies point inward: the api layer depends on application, which depends on domain, and infrastructure implements interfaces defined in application. This lets you test the domain without a DB and swap infrastructure without touching business rules.
Design You maintain a library used by 200 internal teams. How do you evolve its API without breaking them?
Semantic versioning. Keep the public surface small (__all__, underscore-prefixed internals). Use keyword-only parameters so new parameters with defaults are safe to add. Never change the meaning of an existing parameter. Deprecate before removing: emit warnings.warn(..., DeprecationWarning, stacklevel=2) (or @warnings.deprecated in 3.13) for at least one minor version. Write a changelog and migration guide. Keep compatibility shims. Type the public API. Run contract tests, or the main consumers' test suites, in CI.
Design How do you manage configuration and secrets across dev, staging, and prod?
Follow the 12-factor approach: config comes from the environment, not from code. Use one typed settings object (pydantic-settings) that validates at startup, so a bad config fails fast. Layer the sources: defaults, then .env (dev only), then environment variables, then a secret manager (Vault, AWS Secrets Manager). Never commit secrets (use pre-commit scanning). Inject the settings object instead of importing a global everywhere, so tests can override it.
Coding Implement a circuit breaker: after N failures, fail fast for T seconds, then allow one trial call.
import time, threading

class CircuitOpen(Exception): ...

class CircuitBreaker:
    def __init__(self, threshold=3, reset_after=10.0, clock=time.monotonic):
        self.threshold, self.reset_after, self.clock = threshold, reset_after, clock
        self.failures, self.opened_at, self.state = 0, 0.0, "CLOSED"
        self._lock = threading.Lock()

    def call(self, fn, *args, **kwargs):
        with self._lock:
            if self.state == "OPEN":
                if self.clock() - self.opened_at < self.reset_after:
                    raise CircuitOpen("failing fast")
                self.state = "HALF_OPEN"                  # let one trial through
        try:
            result = fn(*args, **kwargs)
        except Exception:
            with self._lock:
                self.failures += 1
                if self.state == "HALF_OPEN" or self.failures >= self.threshold:
                    self.state, self.opened_at = "OPEN", self.clock()
            raise
        with self._lock:
            self.failures, self.state = 0, "CLOSED"       # success closes the circuit again
        return result

t = [0.0]; cb = CircuitBreaker(threshold=2, reset_after=5, clock=lambda: t[0])
def boom(): raise ConnectionError()
for _ in range(2):
    try: cb.call(boom)
    except ConnectionError: pass
try: cb.call(lambda: 1); raise AssertionError
except CircuitOpen: pass
t[0] = 6; assert cb.call(lambda: "ok") == "ok" and cb.state == "CLOSED"
It's a state machine (ch.29) protecting the caller from a failing dependency. Pair it with timeouts and retries with backoff. Libraries: pybreaker, tenacity (for retries).

24 LLD from Zero I: The Mental Model

15 min · start here if LLD has never "clicked" · no jargon until you need it
Read this first If you've tried to learn LLD several times and it didn't stick, that's not a sign you're bad at it. Most courses teach it in the wrong order: they start with 23 design patterns, UML diagrams, and SOLID acronyms. That's like learning chess openings before you know how the pieces move. This chapter teaches the one basic move. Everything else in LLD is that move repeated.

What LLD actually is, in one sentence

LLD means deciding which "things" your program has, what each thing knows, what each thing can do, and who talks to whom. Then you write it as classes.

That's all. The rest (patterns, SOLID, diagrams) is advice for doing that sentence well.

Think of a restaurant

Picture a small restaurant. Nobody does everything. Every person has one job:

PersonWhat they knowWhat they doWho they talk to
WaiterTable number, the orderTakes orders, serves foodCustomer, Chef
ChefRecipesCooks dishesWaiter
CashierPrices, the billTakes paymentCustomer
Menu (a thing, not a person)Dishes and pricesAnswers "how much is X?"Anyone who asks

Why does a restaurant work this way? If the chef also took orders, cooked, and handled cash, then every time the payment system changed (say, UPI is added) you'd have to retrain the chef. Separate jobs mean a change in one place doesn't break everything else.

The basic move Each row of that table becomes a class:
  • "What they know" becomes the class's attributes (data).
  • "What they do" becomes the class's methods (functions).
  • "Who they talk to" becomes the relationships (one object holding or calling another).
That's LLD. You just did it for a restaurant without writing any code.

A class is a "thing" with knowledge and abilities

class Menu:
    def __init__(self):
        self.prices = {"chai": 20, "coffee": 30}   # what it KNOWS

    def price_of(self, item: str) -> int:          # what it can DO
        return self.prices[item]

menu = Menu()               # an actual menu card (an "object" made from the Menu blueprint)
menu.price_of("chai")       # 20

A class is a blueprint ("what every menu looks like"). An object is a real one built from it ("this menu card on table 4"). That's the only OOP you need to begin.

The three ways things connect

There are only three relationships. Learn them in plain words:

Plain wordsExampleIn codeFancy name
"has a"An order has items. A car has an engine.self.items = [] (one object kept inside another)composition / aggregation
"is a kind of"A student discount is a kind of discount.class StudentDiscount(Discount)inheritance
"uses for a moment"A cashier uses a card machine during payment.passed as a function argumentdependency
The beginner mistake Using "is a kind of" (inheritance) everywhere. Ask yourself: "Is X really a kind of Y, in every situation?" If you're unsure, use "has a" instead. In interviews, "has a" is the safe default.
Interview check · 1 question
Concept Class diagram vs sequence diagram: when do you draw each in an interview?
A class diagram is the static structure: classes, attributes, methods, and relationships (has-a, is-a). Draw it first in every LLD round. A sequence diagram shows the dynamic flow of one use case over time: who calls whom, and in what order (User → ParkingLot.park → Allocator.allocate → Floor.take → Ticket). Draw it when the interaction is the tricky part (payment flows, retries, concurrency). A quick text version is fine: "1. A calls B.x(); 2. B calls C.y()...".

The 3 rules that do 80% of the work

Rule 1: One job per class

Describe the class in one sentence without the word "and".
✅ "Menu knows the prices."
❌ "Order stores items and calculates tax and sends SMS." That's three classes hiding in one.

Rule 2: Whoever has the data does the work

Who should calculate an order's total? The thing that has the items: Order. Who knows a dish's price? Menu.
When you're stuck on "where does this method go?", ask "who has the information?"

Rule 3: Put the changing part behind a "socket"

A wall socket doesn't care if you plug in a phone charger or a lamp. Find what the client will change (payment types, discounts, pricing) and make a "socket" (an interface) so new versions can plug in without touching old code.

…and that's it

Every design pattern in chapters 28–29 is one of these three rules applied to a common situation, with a name attached. Patterns are named solutions, not new ideas.

What's an "interface"? (the socket)

An interface is a promise: "anything plugged in here will have these methods." The code that uses it doesn't care which thing it got.

from typing import Protocol

class PaymentMethod(Protocol):        # the SOCKET: "anything that can pay(amount)"
    def pay(self, amount: int) -> None: ...

class Cash:                           # plug 1
    def pay(self, amount): print(f"Collected ₹{amount} in cash")

class UPI:                            # plug 2
    def pay(self, amount): print(f"Sent UPI request for ₹{amount}")

def checkout(amount: int, method: PaymentMethod):   # doesn't know or care which plug it got
    method.pay(amount)

checkout(50, Cash())
checkout(50, UPI())      # adding Card later = write one new class. checkout() never changes.

Read that again slowly. This one idea (a socket for the part that changes) is half of all LLD interviews.

Interview check · 1 question
Concept Abstract class vs interface. How do you express each in Python?
An abstract class can hold shared code and state plus abstract methods that subclasses must implement. In Python: ABC with @abstractmethod. An interface is a pure contract with no implementation. In Python: an ABC containing only abstract methods (nominal: subclasses must inherit) or a Protocol (structural: anything with matching methods fits). A class can satisfy many interfaces but should usually extend only one concrete base.

Self-check (cover the answers)

In a library, which class should have the method is_overdue(): Member, Book, or Loan?
Loan. Rule 2: who has the information? The loan knows its due date and return date. A book doesn't know who borrowed it or when, and a member can have many loans.
"The Order class stores items, calculates the total, and prints a receipt." What's wrong?
It has three jobs (the sentence contains "and"). Keep items and total in Order, because the total comes from its own data. Move printing to a ReceiptPrinter. Then changing the receipt format can't break the order math.
The client says "we'll add more delivery partners later." What do you do?
Rule 3. Create a socket (interface) DeliveryPartner with a method like assign(order), and write one class per partner. New partners become new classes, and existing code stays untouched.
Is a Manager "a kind of" Employee, or does an Employee "have a" role?
Either can work, but think about promotions. A person can go from engineer to manager, and an object can't change its class. So Employee has a role is more flexible. Whenever something can change over time, prefer "has a".

More interview questions

Concept Explain the four pillars of OOP with tiny Python examples.
  • Encapsulation: keep data and the rules for changing it together, and hide the internals. account.withdraw(100) checks the balance itself. Use _balance plus a @property.
  • Abstraction: expose what, hide how. PaymentMethod.pay(amount): the caller doesn't know whether it's UPI or a card.
  • Inheritance: reuse and specialize. class SavingsAccount(Account) adds interest.
  • Polymorphism: the same call behaves differently depending on the object. for shape in shapes: shape.area(). In Python this also works through duck typing, without a shared base class.
Concept Method overloading vs overriding. Does Python support overloading?
Overriding: a subclass redefines a parent's method with the same name. Fully supported, and you can call the parent with super(). Overloading (several methods with the same name and different parameters): not natively. A later def simply replaces the earlier one. Python alternatives: default and keyword arguments, *args, functools.singledispatch / singledispatchmethod (dispatch on the type of the first argument), and typing.overload for type checkers only.
Design (Warm-up round) Design the classes for a to-do app with tasks, due dates, priorities, and lists.
  • Priority(Enum): LOW, MEDIUM, HIGH. Status(Enum): TODO, DONE.
  • Task: id, title, due: date | None, priority, status. Methods complete() and is_overdue(today) (Rule 2: the task has the dates).
  • TaskList: name, tasks. Methods add, remove, pending(), and sorted_by(strategy).
  • Socket: SortStrategy (by due date, by priority), since sort orders are something users customize.
  • Coordinator: TodoService with a TaskRepository protocol (in-memory now, a DB later).
Signals the interviewer looks for: Enums instead of strings, behavior on the class that owns the data, one extension point, and no god class.
Coding Design a bank with accounts and thread-safe transfers that can't deadlock.
import threading, itertools

class InsufficientFunds(Exception): ...

class Account:
    _ids = itertools.count(1)
    def __init__(self, owner: str, balance: int = 0):
        self.id, self.owner, self.balance = next(Account._ids), owner, balance
        self.lock = threading.Lock()

class Bank:
    def transfer(self, src: Account, dst: Account, amount: int) -> None:
        if amount <= 0 or src is dst: raise ValueError("invalid transfer")
        first, second = sorted((src, dst), key=lambda a: a.id)   # ALWAYS lock in id order → no deadlock
        with first.lock, second.lock:
            if src.balance < amount: raise InsufficientFunds(src.owner)
            src.balance -= amount
            dst.balance += amount

a, b, bank = Account("A", 1000), Account("B", 1000), Bank()
ts = [threading.Thread(target=bank.transfer, args=(a, b, 1) if i % 2 else (b, a, 1)) for i in range(200)]
for t in ts: t.start()
for t in ts: t.join()
assert a.balance + b.balance == 2000           # money is conserved; no deadlock occurred
The key insight: if thread 1 locks A then B while thread 2 locks B then A, they deadlock. A global lock order prevents it. Add a Transaction record (an immutable ledger) for auditing.
Design LLD of a URL shortener: classes only, no infrastructure.
  • ShortLink (code, long_url, owner, created_at, expires_at, a clicks count).
  • CodeGenerator socket: Base62Counter (an incrementing ID encoded in base62: short, no collisions), RandomCode (random 7 characters, retried on collision), CustomAlias (validated against a blocklist).
  • LinkRepository protocol (an in-memory dict now, Redis or SQL later).
  • Shortener service: shorten(url, alias=None, ttl=None) validates the URL and generates and stores the code. resolve(code) checks expiry and publishes a LinkClicked event.
  • ClickListener observers: analytics, a rate-abuse detector.
  • Edge cases: duplicate long URLs (return the existing code?), expired links (404 vs 410), custom alias conflicts, and malicious URLs.

25 LLD from Zero II: Build a Chai Shop, One Requirement at a Time

30 min · we start with bad code on purpose, feel the pain, then fix it. That's how design really happens.
How this chapter works A chai shop owner is our client. Every "day" they ask for one new feature, just like a real job (and like an interviewer's follow-up questions). We write the simplest code first, notice what hurts when the next request arrives, and fix only that. By the end you'll have used three famous design patterns without trying to "learn patterns".

Day 1: "Customers order items. Show the bill."

Write the most obvious code first. No classes.

MENU = {"chai": 20, "coffee": 30, "samosa": 15}

def bill(items):                         # items = [("chai", 2), ("samosa", 1)]
    total = 0
    for name, qty in items:
        total += MENU[name] * qty
    return total

bill([("chai", 2), ("samosa", 1)])       # 55

This is fine. For a tiny problem, simple code is the right design. Don't add classes until something hurts.

Day 2: "Students get 10% off. Happy hour (4–6 pm) is 20% off."

The quick, obvious change:

def bill(items, customer_type, hour):
    total = sum(MENU[name] * qty for name, qty in items)
    if customer_type == "student":
        total = total * 0.9
    if 16 <= hour < 18:
        total = total * 0.8
    return total
Feel the pain
  • Every new offer ("Diwali 30% off", "buy 2 get 1") means editing this function again. Each edit risks breaking the offers that already work.
  • The function now has two jobs: adding up items and deciding discounts (Rule 1 broken).
  • Can a student also get happy hour? The rule is buried in ifs. Nobody can see it.

Fix with Rule 3: discounts are the part that keeps changing, so give them a socket.

from typing import Protocol

class Discount(Protocol):                      # the socket: "anything that can shrink a price"
    def apply(self, amount: float) -> float: ...

class NoDiscount:
    def apply(self, amount): return amount

class StudentDiscount:
    def apply(self, amount): return amount * 0.9

class HappyHourDiscount:
    def apply(self, amount): return amount * 0.8

def bill(items, discount: Discount):
    total = sum(MENU[name] * qty for name, qty in items)
    return discount.apply(total)               # bill() no longer knows ANY offer details

bill([("chai", 2)], StudentDiscount())         # 36.0
# Diwali offer? Write class DiwaliDiscount. bill() is never edited again.
🎉 You just learned the Strategy pattern "Strategy" = several interchangeable ways to do one job, behind one socket. Discounts, payment methods, pricing rules, routing algorithms: whenever you hear "different ways to…", it's Strategy. That's the whole pattern.

Day 3: "We accept cash, UPI, and card."

Your instinct now should be: "different ways to pay… that's a changing part → socket!" It's the same move as Day 2.

class PaymentMethod(Protocol):
    def pay(self, amount: float) -> bool: ...

class Cash:
    def pay(self, amount): print(f"Collect ₹{amount:.0f} cash"); return True

class UPI:
    def __init__(self, upi_id: str): self.upi_id = upi_id
    def pay(self, amount): print(f"UPI request ₹{amount:.0f} → {self.upi_id}"); return True

Notice UPI needs extra data (the UPI ID) and Cash doesn't. That's fine: each plug keeps its own details. The socket only promises pay(amount).

Time to make classes: the code is growing

Our "items as a list of tuples" is getting awkward. Let's find the things. Here's the trick: write the requirements as sentences and underline the nouns.

A customer places an order with several items from the menu. The order has a total. A discount may apply. The customer pays with a payment method.

NounClass?Why
menu item✅ MenuItemHas a name and a price
order✅ OrderHas items, a status, a total
item in the order✅ OrderLine"2 × chai": a menu item plus a quantity
discount, payment method✅ already socketsChanging parts
total❌ just a numberIt's a method of Order (it's calculated), not a thing
customer✅ small CustomerWe'll need their phone number on Day 5

Verbs become methods: "places an order", "adds an item", "pays". Rule 2 tells you where each one goes.

from dataclasses import dataclass, field

@dataclass(frozen=True)          # frozen = can't be changed after creation (a price tag shouldn't change mid-order)
class MenuItem:
    name: str
    price: float

@dataclass
class OrderLine:
    item: MenuItem
    qty: int
    def cost(self) -> float:                  # Rule 2: the line knows its item and qty, so it calculates its own cost
        return self.item.price * self.qty

@dataclass
class Customer:
    name: str
    phone: str

@dataclass
class Order:
    customer: Customer
    lines: list[OrderLine] = field(default_factory=list)   # "an order HAS lines"

    def add(self, item: MenuItem, qty: int = 1) -> None:
        self.lines.append(OrderLine(item, qty))

    def total(self, discount: Discount) -> float:          # Rule 2: the order has the lines, so it adds them up
        return discount.apply(sum(line.cost() for line in self.lines))
Look at what each class does MenuItem knows a price. OrderLine knows how much one line costs. Order knows the overall total. Nobody does someone else's job. Each class passes the test "one sentence, no 'and'".

Day 4: "Orders go PLACED → PREPARING → READY → PICKED UP. You can cancel only before we start preparing."

The obvious approach would be if checks scattered everywhere: "if status is PLACED and action is cancel then…". Instead, write the rules down once, as a table:

   from \ to     PREPARING   READY   PICKED_UP   CANCELLED
   PLACED            ✅                              ✅
   PREPARING                   ✅
   READY                                 ✅
   (PICKED_UP and CANCELLED are final: nothing allowed)

Code is just that table:

from enum import Enum

class Status(Enum):              # Enum = a fixed list of allowed values (no typos like "preparng")
    PLACED = "placed"
    PREPARING = "preparing"
    READY = "ready"
    PICKED_UP = "picked_up"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"

ALLOWED_MOVES = {
    Status.PLACED:    {Status.PREPARING, Status.CANCELLED},
    Status.PREPARING: {Status.READY},
    Status.READY:     {Status.PICKED_UP},
}

class InvalidMove(Exception):
    pass

# inside class Order:
#     status: Status = Status.PLACED
    def move_to(self, new: Status) -> None:
        if new not in ALLOWED_MOVES.get(self.status, set()):
            raise InvalidMove(f"cannot go from {self.status.name} to {new.name}")
        self.status = new
🎉 You just learned the State pattern (light version) "State" = an object behaves differently depending on its current stage, and the allowed moves between stages are explicit. Vending machines, elevators, orders, ATMs, traffic lights. A transition table like this is the easiest version. The "full" version (one class per state) is in ch.29. Use it when each state has lots of different behavior, not just different allowed moves.

Day 5: "When an order is READY, SMS the customer and show the number on the TV screen."

The tempting code: inside move_to, write send_sms(...) and tv.show(...). Then next week the owner asks for "also WhatsApp", and then "also update the loyalty app". Your Order class becomes an SMS, TV, WhatsApp, and loyalty class. That breaks Rule 1 badly.

Think of a YouTube subscription. The channel doesn't know who you are. It just says "new video!", and everyone who subscribed gets told. The order should work the same way: it announces "my status changed", and anyone who subscribed reacts.

class StatusListener(Protocol):                 # socket: "anyone who wants to hear about status changes"
    def on_status_change(self, order: "Order") -> None: ...

class SMSNotifier:
    def on_status_change(self, order):
        if order.status is Status.READY:
            print(f"SMS to {order.customer.phone}: your order #{order.id} is ready!")

class TVScreen:
    def on_status_change(self, order):
        if order.status is Status.READY:
            print(f"[TV] Now serving #{order.id}")

# inside class Order:
#     listeners: list[StatusListener] = field(default_factory=list)
    def subscribe(self, listener: StatusListener) -> None:
        self.listeners.append(listener)

    def move_to(self, new: Status) -> None:
        if new not in ALLOWED_MOVES.get(self.status, set()):
            raise InvalidMove(f"cannot go from {self.status.name} to {new.name}")
        self.status = new
        for listener in self.listeners:       # "new video!": tell every subscriber
            listener.on_status_change(self)    # Order has no idea what SMS or a TV is
🎉 You just learned the Observer pattern "Observer" = when something happens, notify everyone who subscribed, without knowing who they are. Notifications, live dashboards, event systems. WhatsApp support later? Write class WhatsAppNotifier and subscribe it. Order is never touched.

The finished design: everything together

One last small piece: a ChaiShop class that coordinates the steps (create order → total → pay), like a manager. It doesn't do the work itself. It asks the right objects to do it.

from __future__ import annotations
import itertools
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from enum import Enum
from typing import Protocol

# ---------- the menu ----------
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class MenuItem:
    name: str
    price: float

# ---------- sockets (the parts that change) ----------
class Discount(Protocol):
    def apply(self, amount: float) -> float: ...

class PaymentMethod(Protocol):
    def pay(self, amount: float) -> bool: ...

class StatusListener(Protocol):
    def on_status_change(self, order: Order) -> None: ...

# ---------- plugs ----------
class NoDiscount:
    def apply(self, amount): return amount

class StudentDiscount:
    def apply(self, amount): return round(amount * 0.9, 2)

class Cash:
    def pay(self, amount):
        print(f"  Collect ₹{amount} cash")
        return True

class UPI:
    def __init__(self, upi_id: str): self.upi_id = upi_id
    def pay(self, amount):
        print(f"  UPI request ₹{amount} → {self.upi_id}")
        return True

class SMSNotifier:
    def on_status_change(self, order):
        if order.status is Status.READY:
            print(f"  SMS {order.customer.phone}: order #{order.id} is ready")

class TVScreen:
    def on_status_change(self, order):
        print(f"  [TV] #{order.id} → {order.status.name}")

# ---------- order lifecycle ----------
class Status(Enum):
    PLACED = "placed"
    PREPARING = "preparing"
    READY = "ready"
    PICKED_UP = "picked_up"
    CANCELLED = "cancelled"

ALLOWED_MOVES = {
    Status.PLACED: {Status.PREPARING, Status.CANCELLED},
    Status.PREPARING: {Status.READY},
    Status.READY: {Status.PICKED_UP},
}

class InvalidMove(Exception): ...
class PaymentFailed(Exception): ...

# ---------- core things ----------
@dataclass
class Customer:
    name: str
    phone: str

@dataclass
class OrderLine:
    item: MenuItem
    qty: int
    def cost(self) -> float:
        return self.item.price * self.qty

_ids = itertools.count(1)

@dataclass
class Order:
    customer: Customer
    id: int = field(default_factory=lambda: next(_ids))
    lines: list[OrderLine] = field(default_factory=list)
    status: Status = Status.PLACED
    listeners: list[StatusListener] = field(default_factory=list)

    def add(self, item: MenuItem, qty: int = 1) -> None:
        self.lines.append(OrderLine(item, qty))

    def total(self, discount: Discount) -> float:
        return discount.apply(sum(line.cost() for line in self.lines))

    def subscribe(self, listener: StatusListener) -> None:
        self.listeners.append(listener)

    def move_to(self, new: Status) -> None:
        if new not in ALLOWED_MOVES.get(self.status, set()):
            raise InvalidMove(f"cannot go from {self.status.name} to {new.name}")
        self.status = new
        for listener in self.listeners:
            listener.on_status_change(self)

# ---------- the coordinator ----------
class ChaiShop:
    def __init__(self, menu: dict[str, MenuItem], listeners: list[StatusListener]):
        self.menu = menu
        self.listeners = listeners          # every new order gets these subscribers

    def place_order(self, customer: Customer, items: dict[str, int],
                    discount: Discount, payment: PaymentMethod) -> Order:
        order = Order(customer)
        for listener in self.listeners:
            order.subscribe(listener)
        for name, qty in items.items():
            order.add(self.menu[name], qty)
        amount = order.total(discount)
        if not payment.pay(amount):
            raise PaymentFailed(order.id)
        return order

# ---------- try it ----------
if __name__ == "__main__":
    menu = {"chai": MenuItem("chai", 20), "samosa": MenuItem("samosa", 15)}
    shop = ChaiShop(menu, listeners=[SMSNotifier(), TVScreen()])

    order = shop.place_order(Customer("Asha", "98xxxx"), {"chai": 2, "samosa": 1},
                             StudentDiscount(), UPI("asha@upi"))
    order.move_to(Status.PREPARING)
    order.move_to(Status.READY)
    try:
        order.move_to(Status.CANCELLED)       # too late to cancel!
    except InvalidMove as e:
        print("  Error:", e)
Output:
  UPI request ₹49.5 → asha@upi
  [TV] #1 → PREPARING
  SMS 98xxxx: order #1 is ready
  [TV] #1 → READY
  Error: cannot go from READY to CANCELLED

Picture of what we built

                    ChaiShop  (coordinator: asks others to do the work)
                       │ creates
                       ▼
 Customer ◄─has─ Order ─has─► OrderLine ─has─► MenuItem
                   │  │
         status: Status (enum + ALLOWED_MOVES table)      ← State pattern
                   │
                   └─tells─► StatusListener socket ◄── SMSNotifier, TVScreen    ← Observer pattern

 Order.total() ─uses─► Discount socket ◄── NoDiscount, StudentDiscount         ← Strategy pattern
 ChaiShop      ─uses─► PaymentMethod socket ◄── Cash, UPI                       ← Strategy pattern

What you actually did (this is the interview process)

What we didInterview name
Listened to what the shop owner wantedRequirements gathering
Underlined nouns and turned them into classesIdentifying entities
Turned verbs into methods, using "who has the data?"Assigning responsibilities
"Order has lines", "line has an item"Relationships / class diagram
Sockets for discounts, payments, and notificationsInterfaces, Open/Closed Principle, Strategy, Observer
Status tableState machine / State pattern
Adding new features as new classes, not edits"Extensible design": what interviewers grade most
Notice We never started with "which pattern should I use?" We started with "what hurts?" and applied the 3 rules. The patterns showed up on their own. This is the secret most courses skip.

Check yourself

The owner asks: "Add a Diwali discount." Which files or classes change?
Only one new class: class DiwaliDiscount with apply(). Nothing existing changes. That's the Open/Closed Principle: open for extension (new classes), closed for modification (old code is untouched).
"Add a loyalty app that gives points when an order is picked up." Where does that code go?
A new listener class, LoyaltyPoints, whose on_status_change checks if order.status is Status.PICKED_UP, plus one line subscribing it to the shop. Order doesn't change.
"Allow cancel during PREPARING too." What changes?
One entry in the table: Status.PREPARING: {Status.READY, Status.CANCELLED}. Because the rules live in one place, the change is one line.
Why does total() live in Order and not in ChaiShop?
Rule 2: the Order has the lines. If ChaiShop calculated it, ChaiShop would need to reach into the order's internals, which spreads knowledge around. ChaiShop only coordinates.
Two cashiers place orders at the same moment. Is anything shared that could break?
The ID counter itertools.count is shared. In CPython, next() on it is effectively atomic, but if you add shared stock (say, "only 10 samosas left"), check-and-decrement must be protected with a threading.Lock. Mentioning shared state like this in an interview earns extra points.

More interview questions

Design "Design a coffee-shop ordering system where drinks have optional add-ons (extra shot, oat milk, syrup), each adding cost."
This is the chai shop plus the Decorator pattern for add-ons, because add-ons stack: oat milk + extra shot + syrup. 
from typing import Protocol

class Beverage(Protocol):
    def cost(self) -> float: ...
    def describe(self) -> str: ...

class Espresso:
    def cost(self): return 120.0
    def describe(self): return "Espresso"

class AddOn:                                   # wraps a beverage and has the same interface
    price, label = 0.0, ""
    def __init__(self, inner: Beverage): self.inner = inner
    def cost(self): return self.inner.cost() + self.price
    def describe(self): return f"{self.inner.describe()} + {self.label}"

class ExtraShot(AddOn): price, label = 40.0, "extra shot"
class OatMilk(AddOn):   price, label = 30.0, "oat milk"

drink = OatMilk(ExtraShot(Espresso()))
assert drink.cost() == 190.0 and drink.describe() == "Espresso + extra shot + oat milk"
Alternative many interviewers accept: Drink(base, addons: list[AddOn]) with cost = base + sum(addon.price). It's simpler and easy to serialize. Mention both and pick one.
Design "Add combo meals: a combo contains items and maybe other combos, at a discounted price."
The Composite pattern: a combo and a single item share one interface, price(). Combo(items: list[Orderable], discount) computes price = sum(i.price() for i in items) * (1 - discount). Nested combos then work automatically through recursion, and Order holds Orderables without caring which kind each is.
Deep-dive Payment succeeds, but the kitchen system is down and the order can't be created. What do you do?
This is a consistency problem across two steps. Options: (1) Reorder the steps: create the order as PENDING_PAYMENT first, then pay, then confirm. (2) Compensate: if a later step fails, refund (a saga). (3) An outbox/queue: record "order paid" durably and let a retrying worker deliver it to the kitchen. Payment calls need idempotency keys, so a retry never charges twice. In the LLD, add a PAYMENT_FAILED / REFUNDED state to the state table.
Follow-up How would you unit-test ChaiShop.place_order?
Inject fakes for every socket: a FakePayment that records amounts and can be told to fail, a RecordingListener that stores the status changes it saw, and a fixed menu. Test: the total with each discount, that a failed payment raises PaymentFailed (and creates no order, if that's the rule), the allowed and forbidden transitions (parametrize over the table), and that listeners are notified in order. No mocks of internals and no real SMS.
Design Add inventory to the chai shop: an order fails if the ingredients run out, and the owner gets an alert when stock is low.
  • Inventory class: stock: dict[str, int] plus recipes: dict[menu_item, dict[ingredient, qty]]. Rule 2: the inventory knows the stock, so it does the checking.
  • reserve(order) checks all ingredients first, then deducts them. This must be all-or-nothing and done under a lock, because two cashiers can sell the last samosa at once.
  • release(order) on cancel. A LowStockListener (Observer) fires when an item drops below its threshold, and the owner's SMS or app subscribes.
  • ChaiShop.place_order: reserve stock, then pay. If the payment fails, release the stock (compensation).
  • Out-of-stock items can be hidden from the menu (the menu asks the inventory).
Design The chai shop opens 20 branches, each with a different menu and pricing. What changes in the design?
Add a Branch entity (id, address, its own Menu, Inventory, pricing strategy, and listeners). ChaiShop becomes per-branch, or a ChainService routes orders by branch_id. Orders record their branch. Shared things stay shared: the customer, loyalty, and payment providers. The core classes (Order, OrderLine, the Status table) don't change, which is the payoff of the earlier design. Pricing per region is just another Strategy plug.

26 LLD from Zero III: Your Recipe + First Solo Design

20 min · a fill-in-the-blanks recipe, then you design a Library yourself with hints

The recipe (use it for every LLD question)

StepAsk yourselfWrite down
1. Clarify"What must it do? What can I skip?"4–6 short use-case sentences: "A member borrows a book."
2. Nouns → things"Which nouns have their own data?"A list of classes. Fixed lists of values (status, size, type) become an Enum
3. Verbs → actions"Who has the data to do this?" (Rule 2)Methods on the right class
4. Connections"Who has whom?" (has a / is a kind of / uses)A small box-and-arrow sketch
5. Changing parts"What will the client change or add later?"Sockets (Protocol) + plugs (classes)
6. Stages"Does anything move through stages?"An Enum plus an allowed-moves table
7. Who must be told?"When X happens, who needs to know?"Listeners (Observer)
8. Coordinator"What's the entry point?"One service class: Library, ParkingLot, ChaiShop
9. Break it"What can go wrong? What if two people act at the same time?"Custom exceptions, and a lock around shared things
Magic phrases to say out loud in the interview
  • "Let me first list the main use cases so we agree on scope."
  • "The nouns here are… I'll make these my core classes."
  • "Payment types will grow, so I'll put them behind an interface. Adding one means adding a class, not editing code."
  • "The order moves through stages, so I'll model the allowed transitions explicitly."
  • "Two users could grab the same seat at once, so this check-and-book step needs to be atomic. I'll use a lock."

Your turn: design a Library system

The requirements: "Members can borrow books and return them. A member can have at most 3 books. A loan lasts 14 days. Late returns pay a fine of ₹10 per day, though we might change the fine rules later. If a book is borrowed, a member can reserve it, and they should be notified when it comes back."

Try each step yourself before opening the hint. Struggling a little is what makes it stick.

Step 2: What are the nouns, and which become classes?
Nouns: member, book, loan, fine, reservation, library.
  • Member: name, active loans.
  • Book: title, ISBN. Subtle point: a library owns several copies of the same book, so add BookCopy (copy ID + book + status). Interviewers love it when you spot this.
  • Loan: which copy, which member, borrowed date, due date, returned date.
  • Library: the coordinator.
  • Fine isn't a thing, it's a calculation that may change, so it becomes a socket.
  • Copy status (AVAILABLE, BORROWED, RESERVED) becomes an Enum.
Step 3: Where do borrow, return_book, is_overdue, and can_borrow go?
  • is_overdue() and days_late() go on Loan, because it has the dates.
  • can_borrow() goes on Member, because it knows its active loans (fewer than 3).
  • borrow() and return_book() go on Library. They coordinate several things: check the member, find a copy, create a loan, notify reservers.
Step 5–7: What are the changing parts, stages, and notifications?
  • Changing part: fine rules. Make a FinePolicy socket with PerDayFine(10) as the first plug. Later there might be NoFineForStudents or CappedFine.
  • Stages: a copy goes AVAILABLE → BORROWED → AVAILABLE (or RESERVED when someone is waiting).
  • Notify: when a reserved book comes back, tell the member who reserved it. That's a listener / Notifier socket (email, SMS, and so on).
Show me a full answer (compare it with yours)
from __future__ import annotations
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import date, timedelta
from enum import Enum
from typing import Protocol
import threading

class CopyStatus(Enum):
    AVAILABLE = "available"
    BORROWED = "borrowed"

class LibraryError(Exception): ...

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Book:
    isbn: str
    title: str

@dataclass(eq=False)
class BookCopy:
    copy_id: str
    book: Book
    status: CopyStatus = CopyStatus.AVAILABLE

@dataclass(eq=False)
class Member:
    member_id: str
    name: str
    loans: list[Loan] = field(default_factory=list)
    MAX_BOOKS = 3                                  # class constant (not a dataclass field: no annotation)
    def can_borrow(self) -> bool:                  # Rule 2: the member knows its own loans
        return len(self.loans) < self.MAX_BOOKS

@dataclass(eq=False)
class Loan:
    copy: BookCopy
    member: Member
    borrowed_on: date
    due_on: date
    returned_on: date | None = None
    def days_late(self, today: date) -> int:       # Rule 2: the loan knows its dates
        end = self.returned_on or today
        return max(0, (end - self.due_on).days)

# --- sockets ---
class FinePolicy(Protocol):
    def fine(self, loan: Loan, today: date) -> int: ...

class PerDayFine:
    def __init__(self, per_day: int = 10): self.per_day = per_day
    def fine(self, loan, today): return loan.days_late(today) * self.per_day

class Notifier(Protocol):
    def notify(self, member: Member, message: str) -> None: ...

class PrintNotifier:
    def notify(self, member, message): print(f"to {member.name}: {message}")

# --- coordinator ---
class Library:
    LOAN_DAYS = 14

    def __init__(self, fine_policy: FinePolicy, notifier: Notifier):
        self.copies: dict[str, list[BookCopy]] = {}       # isbn → copies
        self.waitlist: dict[str, list[Member]] = {}       # isbn → members waiting
        self.fine_policy, self.notifier = fine_policy, notifier
        self._lock = threading.Lock()                     # two members could grab the last copy at once

    def add_copy(self, copy: BookCopy) -> None:
        self.copies.setdefault(copy.book.isbn, []).append(copy)

    def borrow(self, member: Member, isbn: str, today: date) -> Loan:
        with self._lock:
            if not member.can_borrow():
                raise LibraryError(f"{member.name} already has {Member.MAX_BOOKS} books")
            copy = next((c for c in self.copies.get(isbn, []) if c.status is CopyStatus.AVAILABLE), None)
            if copy is None:
                raise LibraryError("no copy available. Reserve it instead")
            copy.status = CopyStatus.BORROWED
            loan = Loan(copy, member, today, today + timedelta(days=self.LOAN_DAYS))
            member.loans.append(loan)
            return loan

    def reserve(self, member: Member, isbn: str) -> None:
        with self._lock:
            self.waitlist.setdefault(isbn, []).append(member)

    def return_book(self, loan: Loan, today: date) -> int:
        with self._lock:
            loan.returned_on = today
            loan.member.loans.remove(loan)
            loan.copy.status = CopyStatus.AVAILABLE
            waiting = self.waitlist.get(loan.copy.book.isbn, [])
            next_member = waiting.pop(0) if waiting else None
        if next_member:                                    # notify OUTSIDE the lock (it may be slow)
            self.notifier.notify(next_member, f"'{loan.copy.book.title}' is available")
        return self.fine_policy.fine(loan, today)

Grade yourself: Did you find Loan as its own class? (That's the most common miss.) Was fine a socket? Did is_overdue/days_late end up on Loan? Did you mention the lock for "last copy"? If you got 3 of 4, you're interview-ready for easy-to-medium LLD.

Confusions everyone has (answered plainly)

"How do I know if something should be a class or just a variable?"
If it has its own data AND its own behavior, or several pieces of data that belong together, make it a class (Loan: dates + days_late). If it's a single value, make it an attribute (a name, a price). If it's a fixed set of choices, make it an Enum (status, size). If it's a calculation that may change, make it a socket (fine rules, discounts).
"Do I need to memorize all 23 design patterns?"
No. In LLD interviews, Strategy, Observer, State, Factory, and Singleton cover about 90% of what comes up. You already know the first three from the chai shop. Learn the rest (ch.28–29) as "nice to recognize".
"When should I use inheritance?"
Rarely. Use it when something is truly "a kind of" the parent in every situation and shares real behavior (an ABC base with shared code). For "different ways to do X", use a socket (Protocol) plus separate classes. For "things that change over time" (role, status), use an attribute.
"How many classes is too many?"
For a 45-minute interview, about 6–12 classes is normal. If a class has no data and only forwards calls, merge it. If a class's description needs "and", split it. Don't create sockets for things that will never change (YAGNI: "you aren't gonna need it").
"I freeze in the interview. What do I do first?"
Say: "Let me list the use cases." Write 4–6 sentences, then underline the nouns. You now have classes, and the panic is gone. The recipe is designed so you always know the next step.
"What's the difference between a Protocol and an ABC? Which do I use?"
Both create a socket. A Protocol says "anything with these methods fits", so plugs don't even need to inherit from it. An ABC requires plugs to inherit (class Cash(PaymentMethod)) and can give them shared code. For an interview, either is fine. Pick one and say why.

Where to go next

For every design pattern explained from scratch, with exercises you can run, see the Design Patterns & SOLID course.

  1. Redo the chai shop from memory on paper, in 20 minutes. Seriously. This is the most valuable exercise in this guide.
  2. Read ch.27 (SOLID will now feel obvious, since you've used it), then ch.29 (Behavioral patterns: you already know three).
  3. Walk through the Parking Lot (ch.30). Map each class to a recipe step.
  4. Practice one problem a day from the table in ch.31, using the recipe. Time yourself: 45 minutes.

More interview questions

Design "Design a hotel booking system." Give a 5-minute outline using the recipe.
  • Use cases: search available rooms for dates, book, cancel, check in, check out, pay.
  • Entities: Hotel, Room (number, RoomType enum, rate), Guest, Booking (room, guest, DateRange, status), Payment. DateRange is a value object with overlaps().
  • Rule 2: DateRange.overlaps(other), Room.is_free(range, bookings), Booking.cancel().
  • Sockets: PricingStrategy (weekend or seasonal rates), CancellationPolicy (full refund until 48 h before), PaymentMethod.
  • States: RESERVED → CHECKED_IN → CHECKED_OUT, or RESERVED → CANCELLED.
  • Concurrency: two guests booking the same room for overlapping dates. Make the overlap check plus insert atomic, either with a lock per room or with a DB exclusion constraint.
Design "Design Stack Overflow (questions, answers, comments, votes, reputation)."
  • Entities: User (reputation), Question, Answer, Comment, Tag, Vote (user, target, ±1).
  • Question and Answer share behavior (votes, comments, an author), so give them a common base Post (inheritance fits: they really are kinds of posts) or a Votable mixin.
  • Rules: one vote per user per post (a dict keyed by (user, post)), and you can't vote on your own post. Reputation changes on vote and accept: the Observer pattern, where a ReputationService subscribes to vote events.
  • Sockets: SearchStrategy (by tag, keyword, or votes) and ReputationRules.
  • States: a question goes OPEN → CLOSED or ANSWERED (accepted). Moderation adds a FLAGGED state.
Design "Design an Amazon-style parcel locker."
  • Entities: LockerStation, Locker (a Size enum, state), Package (size), Pickup (code, expiry).
  • Flow: assign the smallest free locker that fits (the same allocation strategy as the parking lot), generate a one-time code, notify the customer (Observer), open on a valid code, and when the code expires return the package to the courier.
  • States: a locker goes AVAILABLE → RESERVED → OCCUPIED → AVAILABLE.
  • Edge cases: wrong code attempts (rate-limit them), expired codes, no locker that fits, and concurrent assignments (lock the station).
Notice how much is reused from the parking lot. Interviewers like it when you spot that it's "the same shape".
Design Design Snake & Ladder (N players, dice, board with snakes and ladders).
  • Board(size, jumps: dict[int, int]): snakes and ladders are just jumps from one square to another (a snake goes down, a ladder goes up), so one dict covers both.
  • Dice socket: RandomDice(n), plus a FixedDice([…]) for deterministic tests.
  • Player(name, position). Game(board, players, dice) with a turn queue (deque.rotate) and play_turn() → roll, move (don't go past the end), apply the jump, check for a winner.
  • GameStatus enum: NOT_STARTED / IN_PROGRESS / FINISHED.
  • Extensions: rolling a 6 gives another turn, several dice, observers for a UI.
Design Design Chess. How do you handle each piece's moves?
  • Piece ABC (color, position) with an abstract candidate_moves(board), and subclasses King, Queen, Rook, Bishop, Knight, Pawn. This is polymorphism: the game asks any piece for its moves without type checks. Inheritance fits here, because these really are kinds of pieces.
  • Share sliding logic: Rook, Bishop, and Queen use a helper slide(directions) (or a mixin).
  • Board (an 8×8 grid, piece_at, move), Move (from, to, piece, captured: a Command, so undo is possible), Game (turn, status, history).
  • Rules engine: a move is legal only if your own king isn't in check afterwards. Simulate it with the Move command, then undo.
  • Special moves (castling, en passant, promotion) are separate Move subclasses or flags. Player is a Strategy (human or AI).
Design Design the LLD of a ride-hailing app (riders, drivers, matching, trip lifecycle, fares).
  • Entities: Rider, Driver (location, status AVAILABLE/ON_TRIP/OFFLINE), Vehicle, Trip, Location (a value object).
  • Trip states: REQUESTED → DRIVER_ASSIGNED → ARRIVED → IN_PROGRESS → COMPLETED, with CANCELLED possible from early states (a transition table).
  • Sockets: MatchingStrategy (nearest, highest rated, batched), PricingStrategy (base + per km + per minute, times surge), PaymentMethod.
  • Observer: trip events notify the rider app, driver app, and receipts.
  • Concurrency: the same driver offered to two riders. Atomically claim the driver (AVAILABLE → ON_TRIP) with a lock or compare-and-set, and if that fails, try the next candidate.

27 LLD Foundations: Method, Relationships & SOLID

25 min · how LLD rounds are scored, a 45-minute framework, UML relationships in Python, SOLID with code

A Low-Level Design (LLD) round tests whether you can turn vague requirements into classes, responsibilities, and interfaces that are correct, extensible, testable, and thread-safe, and then write working code in about 45 minutes.

What interviewers score

SignalWhat they look for
Requirement clarityYou ask questions, then fix the scope: core features vs nice-to-haves
ModelingCorrect entities, sensible responsibilities, no "god class"
AbstractionsInterfaces at the points that will change (pricing, strategy, storage, notifications)
PatternsUsed where they help, and named, not forced in
Code qualityRunnable, typed, readable, with custom exceptions for edge cases
ConcurrencyYou notice shared state (seats, spots, inventory) and protect it
Extensibility"Add a new vehicle type or payment method" requires a new class, not edits everywhere

The 45-minute framework

 0–5   REQUIREMENTS   Functional (what it does) + non-functional (concurrency, scale, extensibility).
                      Write 4–6 bullet use cases. State assumptions out loud.
 5–10  ENTITIES       Underline the nouns in the use cases → candidate classes. Verbs → methods.
                      Separate: entities (have identity) vs value objects vs enums vs services.
10–15  RELATIONSHIPS  Draw the class diagram: has-a / is-a / uses, with multiplicities (1, *, 0..1).
15–20  INTERFACES     Find the axes of change → ABC/Protocol + strategy. Define the public API.
20–40  CODE           Enums → models → strategies → core service → a demo main(). Happy path first.
40–45  EXTEND + TEST  Concurrency, edge cases, how you'd test, "what if we add X?"
Practice Say what you are doing as you go: "Pricing will change, so I'm putting it behind a PricingStrategy protocol." Interviewers grade reasoning more than syntax.

Class relationships (UML) in Python

RelationshipUMLMeaningPython shape
Inheritance (generalization)Car ──▷ Vehicleis-aclass Car(Vehicle)
RealizationStripe ┄┄▷ PaymentGatewayimplements an interfacesubclass an ABC, or match a Protocol
CompositionOrder ◆── OrderLinestrong has-a: the part dies with the wholecreated inside: self.lines = [OrderLine(...)]
AggregationTeam ◇── Playerweak has-a: the part lives independentlypassed in: Team(players)
AssociationDriver ─── Carknows-aboutan attribute that references another entity
DependencyReport ┄┄> Formatteruses temporarilya method parameter or local variable
class Engine: ...
class Car:
    def __init__(self, driver: "Driver"):
        self.engine = Engine()       # composition: Car creates and owns the Engine
        self.driver = driver         # association / aggregation: Driver exists independently
    def render(self, fmt: "Formatter") -> str:
        return fmt.format(self)      # dependency: used only inside this call
Interview check · 1 question
Composition vs aggregation: give a Python example.
Composition: House creates its Rooms in __init__, and rooms make no sense without the house. Aggregation: Department(employees) receives existing employees, who outlive the department. In code the difference is ownership and lifecycle (who creates and who destroys), not syntax.

Modeling toolkit in Python

ConceptPython tool
Entity (has identity, mutable)A class or @dataclass(eq=False) with an id. Equality by identity
Value object (no identity, immutable)@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True): Money, Address, Coordinates
Fixed set of states or typesEnum / IntEnum (ordered) / StrEnum
InterfaceProtocol (structural) or ABC (nominal, with shared code)
Error casesA custom exception hierarchy (class ParkingError(Exception))
Swappable behaviorA strategy object, or simply a callable
Persistence boundaryA Repository protocol with an in-memory implementation for the interview
Time and IDsInject a clock and an ID generator so tests are deterministic
Shared mutable statethreading.Lock per aggregate, or per resource for finer granularity
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from decimal import Decimal
from enum import Enum, auto
import itertools, uuid

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Money:                                   # value object: compared by value, immutable
    amount: Decimal
    currency: str = "INR"
    def __add__(self, other: "Money") -> "Money":
        if other.currency != self.currency: raise ValueError("currency mismatch")
        return Money(self.amount + other.amount, self.currency)

class OrderStatus(Enum):
    CREATED = auto(); PAID = auto(); SHIPPED = auto(); CANCELLED = auto()

@dataclass(eq=False)                           # entity: identity matters, not field values
class Order:
    id: str = field(default_factory=lambda: uuid.uuid4().hex)
    status: OrderStatus = OrderStatus.CREATED
Interview check · 1 question
Entity vs value object?
An entity has an identity that persists while its attributes change (User, Order), and equality is by ID. A value object is defined by its attributes, is immutable, and is interchangeable (Money, DateRange). Value objects are safe to share and hash, and they make invalid states unrepresentable.

SOLID with code

S: Single Responsibility

# ❌ One class validates, computes, saves, and emails: four reasons to change
class InvoiceService:
    def process(self, inv): self.validate(inv); self.total(inv); self.save(inv); self.email(inv)

# ✅ Each collaborator has one job; the service only orchestrates them
class InvoiceService:
    def __init__(self, calc: "TaxCalculator", repo: "InvoiceRepo", notifier: "Notifier"):
        self.calc, self.repo, self.notifier = calc, repo, notifier
    def process(self, inv):
        inv.total = self.calc.total(inv)
        self.repo.save(inv)
        self.notifier.send(inv.customer, f"Invoice {inv.id} ready")

O: Open/Closed

# ❌ Every new discount means editing this function
def discount(kind, amount):
    if kind == "festive": return amount * 0.8
    elif kind == "member": return amount * 0.9

# ✅ Open for extension (new classes), closed for modification
class Discount(Protocol):
    def apply(self, amount: Decimal) -> Decimal: ...
class Festive:
    def apply(self, amount): return amount * Decimal("0.8")
class Member:
    def apply(self, amount): return amount * Decimal("0.9")
def checkout(amount, discounts: list[Discount]):
    for d in discounts: amount = d.apply(amount)
    return amount

L: Liskov Substitution

# ❌ Penguin breaks the contract of Bird.fly: callers that hold a Bird get surprised
class Bird:
    def fly(self): ...
class Penguin(Bird):
    def fly(self): raise NotImplementedError

# ✅ Model capabilities separately, so every subtype honors its parent's contract
class Bird: ...
class FlyingBird(Bird):
    def fly(self): ...
class Penguin(Bird): ...
# Rules: don't strengthen preconditions, don't weaken postconditions, don't raise new exception types.

I: Interface Segregation

# ❌ class Machine(ABC): print(), scan(), fax()  → a SimplePrinter is forced to stub out fax()
# ✅ Small, role-based protocols; a class implements only what it actually supports
class Printer(Protocol):
    def print(self, doc) -> None: ...
class Scanner(Protocol):
    def scan(self) -> bytes: ...
class Office:                                  # depends only on what it uses
    def __init__(self, printer: Printer): self.printer = printer

D: Dependency Inversion

# ❌ High-level policy hard-codes a low-level detail
class Signup:
    def __init__(self): self.db = PostgresClient("prod-url")

# ✅ Both sides depend on an abstraction; the concrete class is injected (the composition root)
class UserRepo(Protocol):
    def add(self, user) -> None: ...
    def get(self, user_id) -> "User | None": ...
class Signup:
    def __init__(self, repo: UserRepo): self.repo = repo
class InMemoryUserRepo:                         # used in interviews and tests
    def __init__(self): self._d = {}
    def add(self, u): self._d[u.id] = u
    def get(self, uid): return self._d.get(uid)
signup = Signup(InMemoryUserRepo())             # production: Signup(PostgresUserRepo(pool))
Interview check · 4 questions
Is SOLID still relevant in a dynamic language like Python?
Yes, though the mechanics are lighter. Protocols and duck typing make ISP and DIP cheap, first-class functions often replace strategy classes, and modules give natural single responsibility. The goals are the same: change isolation, testability, and substitutability.
Concept "Give me a real example of violating SOLID from your experience, and how you fixed it."
Answer in STAR form with a concrete before and after. Example: "Our ReportService had a 400-line generate() with if fmt == 'pdf' / 'csv' / 'xlsx' branches (it broke OCP and SRP). Every new format touched it and broke the others. I extracted a ReportRenderer protocol with one class per format and a registry. New formats became new files, review got easier, and we added two formats with zero changes to the core." Interviewers want real stakes, a trade-off, and a measurable result.
Coding Refactor this to follow SRP and OCP.
def export(data, fmt): if fmt == "csv": ...write csv... elif fmt == "json": ...json.dumps... send_email(...)
import csv, io, json
from typing import Protocol

class Exporter(Protocol):
    def export(self, rows: list[dict]) -> str: ...

class CSVExporter:
    def export(self, rows):
        buf = io.StringIO()
        w = csv.DictWriter(buf, fieldnames=rows[0].keys()); w.writeheader(); w.writerows(rows)
        return buf.getvalue()

class JSONExporter:
    def export(self, rows): return json.dumps(rows)

EXPORTERS: dict[str, Exporter] = {"csv": CSVExporter(), "json": JSONExporter()}   # add formats here only

class ReportService:                     # orchestration only; exporting and sending are injected
    def __init__(self, exporters: dict[str, Exporter], send):
        self.exporters, self.send = exporters, send
    def run(self, rows, fmt, to):
        self.send(to, self.exporters[fmt].export(rows))

sent = []
ReportService(EXPORTERS, lambda to, body: sent.append((to, body))).run([{"a": 1}], "json", "x@y")
assert sent == [("x@y", '[{"a": 1}]')]
SRP: formatting, sending, and orchestration are separate. OCP: XML is a new class plus a registry entry. And the service is easy to test, because sending is injected.
Concept Dependency Inversion vs Dependency Injection vs Inversion of Control: how do they differ?
Dependency Inversion (a principle, the D in SOLID): high-level code depends on abstractions, not on concrete details. Dependency Injection (a technique): dependencies are passed in (constructor or parameters) instead of created inside. That's how you achieve inversion. Inversion of Control (a broader idea): a framework calls your code instead of your code calling it ("don't call us, we'll call you"): pytest fixtures, FastAPI Depends, Django signals. A DI container is one form of IoC.

Other principles interviewers mention

  • Composition over inheritance: inject behavior (strategy) instead of building subclass explosions like RedLargeCheckedShirt.
  • Program to an interface: type parameters and attributes as Protocols.
  • Law of Demeter: order.customer.wallet.debit() becomes order.charge(). Talk to your neighbours, not strangers.
  • Tell, don't ask: account.withdraw(x) enforces its own invariants, instead of the caller checking the balance and then setting it.
  • DRY, KISS, YAGNI: don't build a plugin framework for one implementation. Add the seam where change is likely.
  • High cohesion, low coupling: things that change together live together. Modules talk through narrow APIs.
  • Fail fast: validate in constructors or __post_init__ so invalid objects can't exist.
Interview check · 3 questions
How do you decide where to put an interface?
At an axis of change or an external boundary: algorithms that vary (pricing, allocation, scheduling), infrastructure (DB, payment, notification), and anything you'll want to fake in tests. Don't add interfaces for stable internal details (YAGNI).
Concept What are coupling and cohesion, and how do you spot problems in a code review?
Cohesion: how related the things inside one module are (high is good). Coupling: how much modules depend on each other's internals (low is good). Warning signs in review: a change to feature X touches 8 files ("shotgun surgery"), classes reaching through chains (a.b.c.d()), utility "god modules", circular imports, tests that need huge setups, and boolean flag parameters that switch behavior.
Follow-up Does too much abstraction hurt? How do you know when to stop?
Yes: indirection costs readability and makes code harder to navigate, and premature interfaces guess wrong about the future. Heuristics: add an abstraction when there is a second implementation or a real test need (the rule of three for duplication), at external boundaries, and around parts that are known to change. If an interface has one implementation and no tests use a fake, inline it. "Duplication is far cheaper than the wrong abstraction" (Sandi Metz).

More interview questions

Coding Design an in-memory file system with mkdir(path), ls(path), write(path, content) (appends), and read(path).
class Node:
    def __init__(self, is_file=False):
        self.is_file = is_file
        self.children: dict[str, "Node"] = {}     # Composite: a directory holds nodes
        self.content: list[str] = []

class FileSystem:
    def __init__(self): self.root = Node()

    def _walk(self, path: str, create: bool = False) -> Node:
        node = self.root
        for part in filter(None, path.split("/")):
            if part not in node.children:
                if not create: raise FileNotFoundError(path)
                node.children[part] = Node()
            node = node.children[part]
        return node

    def mkdir(self, path: str) -> None:
        self._walk(path, create=True)

    def ls(self, path: str) -> list[str]:
        node = self._walk(path)
        return [path.rsplit("/", 1)[-1]] if node.is_file else sorted(node.children)

    def write(self, path: str, content: str) -> None:
        node = self._walk(path, create=True)
        node.is_file = True
        node.content.append(content)              # list + join avoids O(n²) string concatenation

    def read(self, path: str) -> str:
        return "".join(self._walk(path).content)

fs = FileSystem(); fs.mkdir("/a/b"); fs.write("/a/b/f.txt", "hi"); fs.write("/a/b/f.txt", "!")
assert fs.ls("/a/b") == ["f.txt"] and fs.read("/a/b/f.txt") == "hi!" and fs.ls("/a/b/f.txt") == ["f.txt"]
Extensions: permissions, rm, size by recursion (Composite), and a lock per node for concurrent access.

28 Design Patterns I: Creational & Structural

25 min · each pattern: intent, Python implementation, when to use it

For each pattern, know three things: the problem it solves, a short implementation, and the Pythonic shortcut. Interviewers love "isn't that just a function in Python?"

Creational patterns

Singleton: one instance, thread-safe

import threading

class ConfigManager:
    _instance = None
    _lock = threading.Lock()
    def __new__(cls, *args, **kwargs):
        if cls._instance is None:                  # fast path, no lock
            with cls._lock:                        # double-checked locking
                if cls._instance is None:
                    cls._instance = super().__new__(cls)
                    cls._instance._init_once()
        return cls._instance
    def _init_once(self): self.settings = {}       # __init__ would re-run on every ConfigManager() call

# Pythonic shortcut: a module-level instance   →  config = ConfigManager()  in config.py
# Downsides: hidden global state, harder tests. Prefer injecting the instance.

Factory Method / Simple Factory: decouple creation from use

class Notifier(Protocol):
    def send(self, to: str, msg: str) -> None: ...
class EmailNotifier:
    def send(self, to, msg): print(f"email {to}: {msg}")
class SMSNotifier:
    def send(self, to, msg): print(f"sms {to}: {msg}")

class NotifierFactory:
    _registry: dict[str, type[Notifier]] = {"email": EmailNotifier, "sms": SMSNotifier}
    @classmethod
    def register(cls, kind: str):                 # new types plug in without editing the factory (OCP)
        def deco(klass): cls._registry[kind] = klass; return klass
        return deco
    @classmethod
    def create(cls, kind: str) -> Notifier:
        try: return cls._registry[kind]()
        except KeyError: raise ValueError(f"unknown notifier {kind!r}") from None

@NotifierFactory.register("push")
class PushNotifier:
    def send(self, to, msg): ...

Abstract Factory: families of related objects

class UIFactory(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def button(self) -> "Button": ...
    @abstractmethod
    def checkbox(self) -> "Checkbox": ...
class DarkFactory(UIFactory):
    def button(self): return DarkButton()
    def checkbox(self): return DarkCheckbox()
# The client receives one factory and gets consistent widgets from it, never mixing Dark and Light.

Builder: step-by-step construction of complex objects

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HttpRequest:
    method: str; url: str; headers: dict; body: bytes | None; timeout: float

class RequestBuilder:
    def __init__(self, url: str):
        self._url, self._method, self._headers, self._body, self._timeout = url, "GET", {}, None, 10.0
    def method(self, m: str) -> "RequestBuilder": self._method = m; return self    # fluent interface
    def header(self, k: str, v: str) -> "RequestBuilder": self._headers[k] = v; return self
    def json(self, data) -> "RequestBuilder":
        self._body = json.dumps(data).encode(); return self.header("Content-Type", "application/json")
    def build(self) -> HttpRequest:
