        if self._method == "GET" and self._body: raise ValueError("GET cannot have a body")
        return HttpRequest(self._method, self._url, dict(self._headers), self._body, self._timeout)

req = RequestBuilder("https://api.x.com").method("POST").json({"a": 1}).build()
# Pythonic shortcut: keyword arguments with defaults usually remove the need for a builder.
# Use a builder when there's validation across fields, staged construction, or several representations.

Prototype and Object Pool

import copy
base_doc = Document(template="invoice", styles={...})
doc = copy.deepcopy(base_doc)                     # Prototype: clone a configured object instead of rebuilding it

class ConnectionPool:                             # Object Pool: reuse expensive objects (DB connections, threads)
    def __init__(self, factory, size: int):
        self._q = queue.Queue(maxsize=size)
        for _ in range(size): self._q.put(factory())
    @contextmanager
    def connection(self, timeout: float = 5):
        conn = self._q.get(timeout=timeout)       # blocks when the pool is exhausted → backpressure
        try: yield conn
        finally: self._q.put(conn)                # always returned, even if the body raises
Interview check · 5 questions
Factory vs Builder?
A factory decides which class to instantiate and returns it in one call. A builder decides how one complex object is assembled step by step, with validation at build(). They combine well: a factory can return the right builder.
Why is Singleton often called an anti-pattern?
It's global mutable state with hidden dependencies. It makes tests order-dependent and hard to fake, and it's tricky under concurrency and multiprocessing (each process gets its own "singleton"). Prefer creating one instance at the composition root and injecting it.
Coding Your service uses a DBClient singleton everywhere. How do you test it, and how would you refactor?
Short term: patch the accessor in tests (monkeypatch.setattr(db_module, "get_client", lambda: fake)), or give the singleton a _reset() hook for tests only. Long term: create the client once at the composition root (main.py or the FastAPI lifespan) and inject it (constructor parameters, FastAPI Depends). Code then depends on a DBClient protocol, tests pass a fake, and there's no global state.
Deep-dive Implement the Builder pattern or just use kwargs? Defend your choice in Python.
Python has keyword arguments, default values, and dataclasses.replace(), so the classic Java builder (which exists to avoid telescoping constructors) is usually unnecessary. A builder is justified when: (1) construction happens in steps over time (a query builder adding clauses), (2) validation spans several fields and must run once at build(), (3) the same steps produce different representations (SQL vs a Mongo query), or (4) a fluent DSL improves readability. Otherwise use a frozen dataclass with kwargs.
Concept Factory Method vs Abstract Factory, with one example each.
Factory Method: creates one product, and the concrete type is decided by a method, subclass, or registry. NotifierFactory.create("sms") returns an SMSNotifier. Abstract Factory: creates a family of related products that must match. AWSFactory creates S3Storage + SQSQueue + SESMailer, and GCPFactory creates the GCP equivalents, so you never mix S3 with Pub/Sub by accident. If the question involves "families", "themes", or "providers", it's an Abstract Factory.

Structural patterns

Adapter: make an incompatible interface fit

class PaymentGateway(Protocol):
    def pay(self, amount: Decimal, ref: str) -> str: ...

class LegacyBankAPI:                              # third-party code you can't change
    def make_transfer(self, paise: int, note: str) -> dict: return {"txn": "T1"}

class LegacyBankAdapter:                          # implements our interface by wrapping theirs
    def __init__(self, api: LegacyBankAPI): self.api = api
    def pay(self, amount, ref):
        return self.api.make_transfer(int(amount * 100), ref)["txn"]

Decorator (structural): add behavior by wrapping an object with the same interface

class DataSource(Protocol):
    def read(self) -> bytes: ...
    def write(self, data: bytes) -> None: ...

class FileSource:
    def __init__(self, path): self.path = path
    def read(self): return Path(self.path).read_bytes()
    def write(self, d): Path(self.path).write_bytes(d)

class CompressionDecorator:
    def __init__(self, inner: DataSource): self.inner = inner
    def read(self): return zlib.decompress(self.inner.read())
    def write(self, d): self.inner.write(zlib.compress(d))

class EncryptionDecorator:
    def __init__(self, inner: DataSource, key): self.inner, self.f = inner, Fernet(key)
    def read(self): return self.f.decrypt(self.inner.read())
    def write(self, d): self.inner.write(self.f.encrypt(d))

src = EncryptionDecorator(CompressionDecorator(FileSource("x.bin")), key)   # stacked at runtime
# Contrast: Python's @decorator syntax wraps FUNCTIONS; this pattern wraps OBJECTS. Same idea.

Proxy: control access to an object (lazy, caching, auth, remote)

class CachingImageProxy:
    def __init__(self, loader: "ImageLoader"): self._loader, self._cache = loader, {}
    def load(self, url):
        if url not in self._cache: self._cache[url] = self._loader.load(url)   # lazy + cached
        return self._cache[url]

class AuthProxy:
    def __init__(self, target, user): self._t, self._u = target, user
    def __getattr__(self, name):                  # generic delegation via __getattr__
        if not self._u.can(name): raise PermissionError(name)
        return getattr(self._t, name)

Facade: one simple entry point over a complex subsystem

class CheckoutFacade:
    def __init__(self, inventory, payments, shipping, notifier):
        self.inv, self.pay, self.ship, self.notify = inventory, payments, shipping, notifier
    def place_order(self, cart, user) -> str:
        self.inv.reserve(cart.items)
        try:
            txn = self.pay.charge(user, cart.total)
        except PaymentError:
            self.inv.release(cart.items); raise   # the facade owns the compensation logic
        tracking = self.ship.schedule(cart, user.address)
        self.notify.send(user.email, f"Order placed, tracking {tracking}")
        return tracking

Composite: treat a tree of objects uniformly

class Node(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def size(self) -> int: ...

class File(Node):
    def __init__(self, name, nbytes): self.name, self.nbytes = name, nbytes
    def size(self): return self.nbytes

class Directory(Node):
    def __init__(self, name): self.name, self.children: list[Node] = name, []
    def add(self, n: Node) -> "Directory": self.children.append(n); return self
    def size(self): return sum(c.size() for c in self.children)   # recursion over the tree
# Used for file systems, UI widget trees, org charts, menus, expression trees.

Bridge and Flyweight

  • Bridge: separate an abstraction from its implementation so both can vary. Notification(channel: Channel): Urgent/Regular notifications × Email/SMS channels gives 2 + 2 classes instead of 4 subclasses.
  • Flyweight: share the intrinsic state of many fine-grained objects. A text editor shares one Glyph('a', font) per character style. In Python: an interning cache (functools.cache on a factory) plus __slots__.
Interview check · 3 questions
Adapter vs Decorator vs Proxy vs Facade?
All four wrap something. Adapter changes the interface to one the client expects. Decorator keeps the same interface and adds behavior, and can be stacked. Proxy keeps the same interface and controls access (lazy loading, caching, auth, remote). Facade offers a new, simpler interface over many objects.
Coding Two payment SDKs have different APIs. Write adapters so your checkout code uses one interface.
from typing import Protocol
from decimal import Decimal

class PaymentGateway(Protocol):
    def charge(self, amount: Decimal, token: str) -> str: ...     # returns a transaction id

class StripeLikeSDK:                                  # third-party: cents and kwargs, returns a dict
    def create_charge(self, *, amount_cents: int, source: str) -> dict: return {"id": "ch_1"}
class RazorLikeSDK:                                   # third-party: paise, positional args, returns an object
    class _R:
        txn_id = "rz_9"
    def pay(self, paise: int, card_token: str): return self._R()

class StripeAdapter:
    def __init__(self, sdk: StripeLikeSDK): self.sdk = sdk
    def charge(self, amount, token):
        return self.sdk.create_charge(amount_cents=int(amount * 100), source=token)["id"]

class RazorAdapter:
    def __init__(self, sdk: RazorLikeSDK): self.sdk = sdk
    def charge(self, amount, token):
        return self.sdk.pay(int(amount * 100), token).txn_id

def checkout(gw: PaymentGateway, total: Decimal) -> str:
    return gw.charge(total, "tok_test")

assert checkout(StripeAdapter(StripeLikeSDK()), Decimal("9.99")) == "ch_1"
assert checkout(RazorAdapter(RazorLikeSDK()), Decimal("9.99")) == "rz_9"
Unit conversion (rupees to paise) and response translation live in the adapter. Checkout code never imports the SDKs.
Deep-dive Your Singleton cache works in dev, but each Gunicorn worker has different data. Why?
A singleton is per process. Gunicorn (and multiprocessing, Celery prefork) runs several processes, each with its own memory and therefore its own "singleton". With fork, they start as copies, then diverge. Shared state must live outside the processes: Redis, memcached, a DB, or shared memory. Only use in-process singletons for things that are fine to duplicate (config, connection pools per worker).

More interview questions

Design "Design a logging framework like Python's logging." Which patterns would you use?
  • Logger hierarchy by dotted name (a.b is a child of a), with propagation to the parents (Chain of Responsibility).
  • Registry/Singleton: getLogger(name) always returns the same logger (Flyweight-like caching).
  • Handlers (console, file, HTTP) attached to loggers. One record fans out to several handlers (Observer).
  • Formatter per handler (Strategy). Filters (Chain).
  • Non-functional: thread safety (a lock per handler), async or queued handlers so a slow sink can't block callers, and lazy message formatting.

29 Design Patterns II: Behavioral

25 min · Strategy, Observer, Command, State, Chain of Responsibility, Template, Mediator, Memento, Visitor, Iterator

Strategy: swap an algorithm at runtime

class PricingStrategy(Protocol):
    def price(self, base: Decimal) -> Decimal: ...

class Regular:
    def price(self, base): return base
class Surge:
    def __init__(self, multiplier: Decimal): self.m = multiplier
    def price(self, base): return base * self.m

class RideService:
    def __init__(self, pricing: PricingStrategy): self.pricing = pricing
    def fare(self, km: float) -> Decimal: return self.pricing.price(Decimal(km) * 12)

svc = RideService(Surge(Decimal("1.5")))
# Pythonic shortcut: a plain callable also works → RideService(pricing=lambda b: b * 2)

Observer / Pub-Sub: notify dependents of a change

class EventBus:
    def __init__(self):
        self._subs: dict[str, list[Callable]] = defaultdict(list)
        self._lock = threading.Lock()
    def subscribe(self, topic: str, handler: Callable) -> Callable[[], None]:
        with self._lock: self._subs[topic].append(handler)
        return lambda: self._unsubscribe(topic, handler)       # returns an unsubscribe handle
    def _unsubscribe(self, topic, handler):
        with self._lock: self._subs[topic].remove(handler)
    def publish(self, topic: str, event) -> None:
        with self._lock: handlers = list(self._subs[topic])    # snapshot; never call user code while holding the lock
        for h in handlers:
            try: h(event)
            except Exception: log.exception("handler failed")  # one bad subscriber must not break the others

bus = EventBus()
unsub = bus.subscribe("order.paid", lambda e: print("ship", e))
bus.publish("order.paid", {"id": 1})
# Leak risk: subscribers keep their owners alive. Use weakref.WeakMethod or always unsubscribe.
Interview check · 2 questions
How do you make an Observer implementation thread-safe and leak-free?
Guard the subscriber list with a lock, but copy it before notifying and call handlers outside the lock, which prevents deadlocks and re-entrancy problems. Isolate each handler's exceptions. Return unsubscribe handles or use weak references. For slow handlers, dispatch asynchronously through a queue or thread pool.
Deep-dive Observer in-process vs a message broker (Kafka, RabbitMQ). When do you switch?
In-process observers are synchronous, simple, and transactional with the caller, but a slow or failing subscriber hurts the publisher, events are lost on a crash, and they only work within one process. Switch to a broker when subscribers live in other services, when you need durability or replay, or when independent scaling or backpressure matters. Then design for at-least-once delivery: idempotent consumers, the outbox pattern for publishing atomically with the DB write, and ordering per key.

Command: an action as an object (undo/redo, queues, macros)

class Command(Protocol):
    def execute(self) -> None: ...
    def undo(self) -> None: ...

@dataclass
class InsertText:
    doc: "Document"; pos: int; text: str
    def execute(self): self.doc.content = self.doc.content[:self.pos] + self.text + self.doc.content[self.pos:]
    def undo(self):    self.doc.content = self.doc.content[:self.pos] + self.doc.content[self.pos + len(self.text):]

class Editor:
    def __init__(self, doc): self.doc, self._undo, self._redo = doc, [], []
    def run(self, cmd: Command):
        cmd.execute(); self._undo.append(cmd); self._redo.clear()   # a new action invalidates the redo history
    def undo(self):
        if self._undo: cmd = self._undo.pop(); cmd.undo(); self._redo.append(cmd)
    def redo(self):
        if self._redo: cmd = self._redo.pop(); cmd.execute(); self._undo.append(cmd)
Interview check · 2 questions
How would you implement undo/redo?
The Command pattern with two stacks. Each command implements execute/undo. A new command clears the redo stack. Alternatives: Memento snapshots (simpler, more memory) or event sourcing. Mention merging small commands (typing) and capping the history size with a deque(maxlen=N).
Coding Command pattern with macros: group several commands so they undo as one.
class SetValue:
    def __init__(self, store, key, value): self.store, self.key, self.value = store, key, value
    def execute(self):
        self.old = self.store.get(self.key); self.store[self.key] = self.value
    def undo(self):
        if self.old is None: self.store.pop(self.key, None)
        else: self.store[self.key] = self.old

class Macro:                                     # Composite + Command: a command made of commands
    def __init__(self, *cmds): self.cmds = cmds
    def execute(self):
        for c in self.cmds: c.execute()
    def undo(self):
        for c in reversed(self.cmds): c.undo()   # undo in REVERSE order

store = {"theme": "light"}
m = Macro(SetValue(store, "theme", "dark"), SetValue(store, "font", 14))
m.execute(); assert store == {"theme": "dark", "font": 14}
m.undo();    assert store == {"theme": "light"}

State: behavior changes with internal state (no giant if/elif)

class OrderState(ABC):
    def pay(self, o: "Order"):    raise InvalidTransition(f"cannot pay in {type(self).__name__}")
    def ship(self, o: "Order"):   raise InvalidTransition(f"cannot ship in {type(self).__name__}")
    def cancel(self, o: "Order"): raise InvalidTransition(f"cannot cancel in {type(self).__name__}")

class Created(OrderState):
    def pay(self, o):    o.state = Paid()
    def cancel(self, o): o.state = Cancelled()
class Paid(OrderState):
    def ship(self, o):   o.state = Shipped()
    def cancel(self, o): o.refund(); o.state = Cancelled()
class Shipped(OrderState): pass
class Cancelled(OrderState): pass

class Order:
    def __init__(self): self.state: OrderState = Created()
    def pay(self): self.state.pay(self)          # delegate to the current state
    def ship(self): self.state.ship(self)
    def cancel(self): self.state.cancel(self)
    def refund(self): ...
# Lighter alternative: an Enum plus a transition table  {(State.CREATED, "pay"): State.PAID, ...}
Interview check · 2 questions
Strategy vs State: they look identical, so what's the difference?
Both delegate to an interchangeable object. With Strategy, the client chooses the algorithm and strategies don't know about each other. With State, the object itself switches state as a result of operations, and state classes know their transitions. The intent differs: "how to do X" vs "what I can do now".
Coding Model a traffic light as a state machine, where each state knows its duration and its next state.
from enum import Enum

class Light(Enum):
    RED = ("GREEN", 30)
    GREEN = ("YELLOW", 25)
    YELLOW = ("RED", 5)
    def __init__(self, nxt, seconds):
        self._next_name, self.seconds = nxt, seconds
    @property
    def next(self) -> "Light": return Light[self._next_name]

class TrafficLight:
    def __init__(self, start=Light.RED): self.state, self.elapsed = start, 0
    def tick(self, seconds=1):
        self.elapsed += seconds
        while self.elapsed >= self.state.seconds:           # handles big ticks across several states
            self.elapsed -= self.state.seconds
            self.state = self.state.next

t = TrafficLight(); t.tick(30); assert t.state is Light.GREEN
t.tick(30); assert t.state is Light.RED and t.elapsed == 0     # 25 green + 5 yellow
Enum members carry the data (next state and duration), so the transitions live in one place. Add a pedestrian button with a new state, or an override that shortens the green.

Chain of Responsibility: pass a request along handlers

class Handler(ABC):
    def __init__(self, nxt: "Handler | None" = None): self.nxt = nxt
    def handle(self, req):
        if self.nxt: return self.nxt.handle(req)
        return req

class Auth(Handler):
    def handle(self, req):
        if not req.get("token"): raise PermissionError("no token")
        return super().handle(req)
class RateLimit(Handler):
    def handle(self, req):
        if not limiter.allow(req["user"]): raise TooManyRequests()
        return super().handle(req)
class Validate(Handler):
    def handle(self, req): ...; return super().handle(req)

pipeline = Auth(RateLimit(Validate()))         # the same idea as web middleware and logging handlers
pipeline.handle({"token": "t", "user": "u"})
Interview check · 1 question
Coding Implement a middleware pipeline where each middleware can run code before and after the next one (like Django/ASGI).
from typing import Callable
Handler = Callable[[dict], dict]
Middleware = Callable[[dict, Handler], dict]

def build(handler: Handler, middlewares: list[Middleware]) -> Handler:
    for mw in reversed(middlewares):                          # the first middleware becomes the outermost
        handler = (lambda mw, nxt: lambda req: mw(req, nxt))(mw, handler)   # bind now: avoids late binding
    return handler

def timing(req, nxt):
    req.setdefault("log", []).append("timing:start")
    resp = nxt(req)
    req["log"].append("timing:end"); return resp

def auth(req, nxt):
    if not req.get("user"): return {"status": 401}
    return nxt(req)

app = build(lambda req: {"status": 200}, [timing, auth])
r = {"user": "u"}; assert app(r)["status"] == 200 and r["log"] == ["timing:start", "timing:end"]
assert app({})["status"] == 401
This is the Chain of Responsibility. Note the closure late-binding trap in the loop (ch.05), handled by binding through a factory lambda.

Template Method: a fixed skeleton with overridable steps

class DataExporter(ABC):
    def export(self, query) -> bytes:          # the template: the order of steps is fixed
        rows = self.fetch(query)
        rows = self.transform(rows)
        return self.serialize(rows)
    @abstractmethod
    def fetch(self, query): ...
    def transform(self, rows): return rows    # a hook with a default
    @abstractmethod
    def serialize(self, rows) -> bytes: ...

class CSVExporter(DataExporter):
    def fetch(self, q): return db.run(q)
    def serialize(self, rows): ...
Interview check · 1 question
Concept Template Method vs Strategy: both vary steps of an algorithm. How do you choose?
Template Method uses inheritance: the base class fixes the skeleton, and subclasses override hook steps. The choice is made when the class is written, one per subclass. Strategy uses composition: the algorithm is passed in as an object or function, and can be swapped at runtime or combined independently. Prefer Strategy when the steps vary independently (a format × a transport would otherwise need 4 subclasses) or must change at runtime. Template Method is fine when there's one fixed skeleton with a few hooks, as in frameworks (unittest.TestCase.setUp).

Mediator, Memento, Visitor, Iterator

# Mediator: objects talk through a hub instead of to each other (chat room, air-traffic control, UI dialog)
class ChatRoom:
    def __init__(self): self.users = {}
    def join(self, u): self.users[u.name] = u; u.room = self
    def send(self, sender, to, msg): self.users[to].receive(sender.name, msg)

# Memento: snapshot and restore state without exposing internals
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class EditorMemento: content: str; cursor: int
class TextEditor:
    def save(self) -> EditorMemento: return EditorMemento(self.content, self.cursor)
    def restore(self, m: EditorMemento): self.content, self.cursor = m.content, m.cursor

# Visitor: add operations over a class hierarchy without editing it. In Python, singledispatch does this:
@functools.singledispatch
def area(shape): raise TypeError(type(shape))
@area.register
def _(s: Circle): return math.pi * s.r ** 2
@area.register
def _(s: Rect): return s.w * s.h

# Iterator: built into the language (__iter__/__next__, generators); see ch.07

Pattern selection cheat sheet

When you hear…Reach for
"different algorithms or rules, chosen at runtime"Strategy
"when X happens, notify A, B, C"Observer / event bus
"undo, redo, queue, retry, audit log of actions"Command
"lifecycle: created → paid → shipped…", "machine modes"State
"series of checks or filters, middleware"Chain of Responsibility
"same steps, different details"Template Method
"create one of many types from config or input"Factory (+ registry)
"complex object with many optional parts"Builder
"integrate a third-party or legacy API"Adapter
"add logging, caching, or retries without touching the class"Decorator / Proxy
"tree or hierarchy treated uniformly"Composite
"simplify a messy subsystem"Facade

More interview questions

Design "Design a notification service: email, SMS, and push, with user preferences, templates, and retries."
  • Channel protocol, with EmailChannel, SMSChannel, PushChannel (Strategy) chosen by a ChannelFactory from the user's preferences.
  • TemplateRenderer (per channel and locale) separates content from delivery.
  • A NotificationService.notify(user, event) facade: look up preferences, render, then enqueue one job per channel.
  • Workers send with retry plus exponential backoff, using idempotency keys to avoid duplicates. Failures go to a dead-letter queue.
  • A provider Adapter (Twilio, SES) behind each channel, so providers can be swapped or failed over.
  • Rate limits per user (quiet hours) through a chain of filters: preferences, then rate limit, then dedupe.
Follow-up Implement Tic-Tac-Toe (n×n) with O(1) win detection per move.
class TicTacToe:
    def __init__(self, n: int):
        self.n = n
        self.rows, self.cols = [0] * n, [0] * n
        self.diag = self.anti = 0
    def move(self, r: int, c: int, player: int) -> int:
        """Returns the winner (1 or 2), or 0. Player 1 counts +1, player 2 counts -1."""
        d = 1 if player == 1 else -1
        self.rows[r] += d; self.cols[c] += d
        if r == c: self.diag += d
        if r + c == self.n - 1: self.anti += d
        if self.n in (abs(self.rows[r]), abs(self.cols[c]), abs(self.diag), abs(self.anti)):
            return player
        return 0

g = TicTacToe(3)
assert [g.move(0,0,1), g.move(0,2,2), g.move(2,2,1), g.move(1,1,2), g.move(2,0,1), g.move(1,0,2), g.move(2,1,1)][-1] == 1
For the full LLD version, add Board, Player (a Strategy: human or AI), a GameState enum, move validation, and a Command for undo.

30 LLD Case Study: Parking Lot (Full Walkthrough)

25 min · the most-asked LLD problem, done step by step with the framework from ch.27

Step 1: Requirements (state them)

  • Multiple floors. Spots come in sizes SMALL, MEDIUM, and LARGE.
  • Vehicles: motorcycle (needs SMALL or larger), car (MEDIUM or larger), truck (LARGE).
  • Park: assign the nearest suitable free spot and issue a ticket. Unpark: compute the fee, free the spot, return a receipt.
  • Pricing varies (hourly, flat, per vehicle type), and so does spot allocation (nearest, by floor), so both get strategies.
  • Non-functional: concurrent entry and exit gates, O(log n) spot finding, testable (inject the clock).
  • Out of scope (say it): payments integration, reservations, a UI.

Step 2–3: Entities and relationships

ParkingLot ◆──* ParkingFloor ◆──* ParkingSpot ───0..1 Vehicle
    │                                    ▲
    ├── uses ──▷ SpotAllocationStrategy   │ referenced by
    ├── uses ──▷ PricingStrategy          │
    └──* Ticket ─────────────────────────┘     Ticket ──▷ Receipt (on exit)
Enums: VehicleType, SpotSize          Errors: ParkingFullError, InvalidTicketError

Step 4–5: Code

from __future__ import annotations
import heapq, itertools, math, threading
from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from decimal import Decimal
from enum import Enum, IntEnum
from typing import Callable, Protocol

# ---------- enums and errors ----------
class SpotSize(IntEnum):            # IntEnum, so sizes can be compared: SMALL < MEDIUM < LARGE
    SMALL = 1
    MEDIUM = 2
    LARGE = 3

class VehicleType(Enum):
    MOTORCYCLE = SpotSize.SMALL
    CAR = SpotSize.MEDIUM
    TRUCK = SpotSize.LARGE
    @property
    def min_spot(self) -> SpotSize: return self.value

class ParkingError(Exception): ...
class ParkingFullError(ParkingError): ...
class InvalidTicketError(ParkingError): ...

# ---------- models ----------
@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Vehicle:
    plate: str
    type: VehicleType

@dataclass(eq=False)
class ParkingSpot:
    floor: int
    number: int
    size: SpotSize
    vehicle: Vehicle | None = None
    @property
    def free(self) -> bool: return self.vehicle is None

class ParkingFloor:
    def __init__(self, level: int, layout: dict[SpotSize, int]):
        self.level = level
        self._free: dict[SpotSize, list[tuple[int, ParkingSpot]]] = {s: [] for s in SpotSize}
        n = itertools.count(1)
        for size, count in layout.items():
            for _ in range(count):
                spot = ParkingSpot(level, next(n), size)
                self._free[size].append((spot.number, spot))
        for heap in self._free.values(): heapq.heapify(heap)   # min-heap by spot number = nearest first

    def take(self, size: SpotSize) -> ParkingSpot | None:
        heap = self._free[size]
        return heapq.heappop(heap)[1] if heap else None        # O(log n)

    def release(self, spot: ParkingSpot) -> None:
        heapq.heappush(self._free[spot.size], (spot.number, spot))

    def free_count(self, size: SpotSize) -> int: return len(self._free[size])

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Ticket:
    id: int
    vehicle: Vehicle
    spot: ParkingSpot
    entry: datetime

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Receipt:
    ticket: Ticket
    exit: datetime
    fee: Decimal

# ---------- strategies (the axes of change) ----------
class PricingStrategy(Protocol):
    def fee(self, ticket: Ticket, exit_time: datetime) -> Decimal: ...

class HourlyPricing:
    RATES = {SpotSize.SMALL: Decimal(20), SpotSize.MEDIUM: Decimal(40), SpotSize.LARGE: Decimal(80)}
    def fee(self, ticket, exit_time):
        hours = max(1, math.ceil((exit_time - ticket.entry).total_seconds() / 3600))   # bill partial hours as full ones
        return self.RATES[ticket.spot.size] * hours

class SpotAllocationStrategy(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def allocate(self, floors: list[ParkingFloor], vehicle: Vehicle) -> ParkingSpot | None: ...

class NearestFloorFirst(SpotAllocationStrategy):
    def allocate(self, floors, vehicle):
        for floor in floors:                                   # lowest floor first
            for size in SpotSize:                              # smallest spot that fits (saves big spots)
                if size >= vehicle.type.min_spot and (spot := floor.take(size)):
                    return spot
        return None

# ---------- the core service ----------
class ParkingLot:
    def __init__(self, floors: list[ParkingFloor], pricing: PricingStrategy,
                 allocator: SpotAllocationStrategy | None = None,
                 clock: Callable[[], datetime] = lambda: datetime.now(timezone.utc)):
        self._floors = {f.level: f for f in floors}
        self._ordered = sorted(floors, key=lambda f: f.level)
        self._pricing = pricing
        self._allocator = allocator or NearestFloorFirst()
        self._clock = clock
        self._active: dict[int, Ticket] = {}
        self._by_plate: dict[str, int] = {}
        self._ids = itertools.count(1)
        self._lock = threading.Lock()           # several gates call park/unpark concurrently

    def park(self, vehicle: Vehicle) -> Ticket:
        with self._lock:                        # find + take + record must be atomic (otherwise two cars get one spot)
            if vehicle.plate in self._by_plate:
                raise ParkingError(f"{vehicle.plate} is already parked")
            spot = self._allocator.allocate(self._ordered, vehicle)
            if spot is None:
                raise ParkingFullError(f"no spot for {vehicle.type.name}")
            spot.vehicle = vehicle
            ticket = Ticket(next(self._ids), vehicle, spot, self._clock())
            self._active[ticket.id] = ticket
            self._by_plate[vehicle.plate] = ticket.id
            return ticket

    def unpark(self, ticket_id: int) -> Receipt:
        with self._lock:
            ticket = self._active.pop(ticket_id, None)
            if ticket is None:
                raise InvalidTicketError(ticket_id)
            del self._by_plate[ticket.vehicle.plate]
            ticket.spot.vehicle = None
            self._floors[ticket.spot.floor].release(ticket.spot)
        now = self._clock()                     # pricing runs outside the lock (pure, possibly slow)
        return Receipt(ticket, now, self._pricing.fee(ticket, now))

    def availability(self) -> dict[int, dict[str, int]]:
        with self._lock:
            return {f.level: {s.name: f.free_count(s) for s in SpotSize} for f in self._ordered}

# ---------- demo ----------
if __name__ == "__main__":
    lot = ParkingLot([ParkingFloor(0, {SpotSize.SMALL: 2, SpotSize.MEDIUM: 2, SpotSize.LARGE: 1}),
                      ParkingFloor(1, {SpotSize.MEDIUM: 5})], HourlyPricing())
    t = lot.park(Vehicle("KA01AB1234", VehicleType.CAR))
    print(t.spot.floor, t.spot.number, lot.availability())
    print(lot.unpark(t.id).fee)
Interview check · 2 questions
Design "Extend the parking lot: users can reserve a spot for a time slot via an app, and prices rise when the lot is 80% full."
  • Reservation entity (user, spot size, slot as a DateRange, status). The allocator now has to skip spots held by reservations whose slot overlaps now. Keep an interval index per spot, or a set of held spots refreshed from active reservations.
  • Reservation states: BOOKED → ACTIVE (arrived) → COMPLETED, or BOOKED → NO_SHOW (after a grace period, with a fee) or CANCELLED.
  • Dynamic pricing: a SurgePricing(base, occupancy_provider) strategy that wraps (decorates) HourlyPricing. Occupancy comes from availability(). Decide whether the price is locked at entry or computed at exit, and state it.
  • The ParkingLot core changes only to accept the reservation-aware allocator, which shows the earlier design was extensible.
Coding Extend the parking lot with EV charging spots without modifying ParkingLot.
# 1. data: spots gain a feature flag (added field with a default → existing code unaffected)
#    @dataclass(eq=False) class ParkingSpot: ...; ev_charger: bool = False
# 2. a NEW allocation strategy, injected into the lot
class EVFirstAllocator(SpotAllocationStrategy):
    def __init__(self, fallback: SpotAllocationStrategy): self.fallback = fallback
    def allocate(self, floors, vehicle):
        if getattr(vehicle, "is_ev", False):
            for floor in floors:
                if spot := floor.take_matching(lambda s: s.ev_charger and s.size >= vehicle.type.min_spot):
                    return spot
        return self.fallback.allocate(floors, vehicle)       # Decorator-style: wrap the old strategy
# 3. a NEW pricing strategy that adds a charging fee for EV spots
class EVPricing:
    def __init__(self, base: PricingStrategy, per_hour=Decimal(50)): self.base, self.per_hour = base, per_hour
    def fee(self, ticket, exit_time):
        extra = self.per_hour * math.ceil((exit_time - ticket.entry).total_seconds() / 3600) if ticket.spot.ev_charger else 0
        return self.base.fee(ticket, exit_time) + extra
# wiring:  ParkingLot(floors, EVPricing(HourlyPricing()), EVFirstAllocator(NearestFloorFirst()))
The only change to existing classes is an additive one: a field with a default, plus a take_matching helper on the floor. That's open/closed in practice.

Step 6: Discuss extensions (this is where seniors score)

Follow-upAnswer with the design
Add an electric vehicle with charging spotsAdd SpotFeature.EV on spots and a new allocation strategy that filters on it. No changes to ParkingLot (OCP).
Weekend pricingA new PricingStrategy, or a CompositePricing that picks a strategy by date. Injected.
One global lock is a bottleneckUse a lock per floor: allocation tries each floor under that floor's lock. Plate/ticket maps get their own small lock, or a dict with careful single operations. Or shard by entry gate.
Multiple servers or gatesState moves to a DB. Claim a spot atomically: UPDATE spots SET vehicle=? WHERE id=? AND vehicle IS NULL (optimistic), or Redis SETNX per spot.
Display boards per floorObserver: the lot publishes spot.changed events, and boards subscribe.
PaymentsPaymentProcessor protocol (Strategy plus an Adapter per provider). Unpark becomes: compute fee, pay, then release.
TestingInject a fake clock (clock=lambda: fixed_time), an in-memory lot, and parametrized tests per vehicle type. Use threads to stress-test that no spot is assigned twice.
Common mistakes A ParkingLot god class with pricing if/elifs inside it. Linear scans over every spot. No locking around check-then-take. Car(Vehicle) subclasses that differ only in data (an Enum is enough). Using float for money. Calling datetime.now() deep in the logic, which makes it untestable.
Interview check · 3 questions
Deep-dive The lot has 10 entry gates and the single global lock shows contention. Redesign the locking.
Option 1: a lock per floor. A gate tries floors in order, acquiring each floor's lock only while popping from its heap, and the ticket registry gets its own small lock. Option 2: assign gates to floors or zones (sharding), so most operations never contend. Option 3: an optimistic, lock-free claim, where free spots go in a queue.Queue per (floor, size) and get_nowait() is the atomic claim. Always acquire multiple locks in a fixed order (floor number) to avoid deadlock. Measure the contention first, because a parking lot's throughput rarely needs this.
Follow-up What unit tests would you write for the parking lot?
Parametrized tests for vehicle type vs spot size (a motorcycle can take a SMALL spot, then MEDIUM when SMALL is full; a truck only takes LARGE). Full lot raises ParkingFullError. The same plate twice is rejected. An invalid or reused ticket raises. Fees at the boundaries with a fixed clock (0 minutes is 1 hour, 61 minutes is 2 hours). availability() before and after. A concurrency test: N threads park into M spots, and exactly M succeed with unique spots. A released spot is reused, nearest first.
Design Payment happens at the exit gate, and sometimes it fails. How do you make sure the car isn't stuck and money isn't lost?
Separate fee calculation, payment, and releasing the spot. Flow: unpark → compute the fee → create a Payment (PENDING, with an idempotency key = the ticket ID) → call the gateway with a timeout. On success, mark the payment PAID, release the spot, and open the gate. On failure or timeout, keep the ticket ACTIVE and let the driver retry or use another method. Before retrying, ask the gateway for the status of that idempotency key, so a slow success isn't charged twice. Add a manual override by an attendant, and an audit log. Ticket states: ACTIVE → PAYMENT_PENDING → PAID → EXITED.

31 LLD Case Studies: Rate Limiter, Vending Machine, Elevator, Splitwise, Booking

35 min · five frequently asked problems, each with its key insight, core code, and follow-ups

1. Rate limiter (Strategy + thread safety)

Requirements: limit N requests per client per window, with a pluggable algorithm, thread-safe, using O(1) memory per client where possible.

import threading, time
from collections import deque, defaultdict

class RateLimitAlgorithm(Protocol):
    def allow(self) -> bool: ...

class TokenBucket:
    """Allows bursts of up to `capacity`; refills at `rate` tokens/sec. O(1) memory."""
    def __init__(self, capacity: int, rate: float, clock=time.monotonic):
        self.capacity, self.rate, self.clock = capacity, rate, clock
        self.tokens, self.last = float(capacity), clock()
        self.lock = threading.Lock()
    def allow(self) -> bool:
        with self.lock:
            now = self.clock()
            self.tokens = min(self.capacity, self.tokens + (now - self.last) * self.rate)  # lazy refill
            self.last = now
            if self.tokens >= 1:
                self.tokens -= 1
                return True
            return False

class SlidingWindowLog:
    """Exact: at most `limit` requests in any `window` seconds. O(limit) memory."""
    def __init__(self, limit: int, window: float, clock=time.monotonic):
        self.limit, self.window, self.clock = limit, window, clock
        self.log: deque[float] = deque()
        self.lock = threading.Lock()
    def allow(self) -> bool:
        with self.lock:
            now = self.clock()
            while self.log and self.log[0] <= now - self.window:   # evict expired timestamps
                self.log.popleft()
            if len(self.log) < self.limit:
                self.log.append(now)
                return True
            return False

class RateLimiter:
    def __init__(self, factory: Callable[[], RateLimitAlgorithm]):
        self._factory = factory
        self._clients: dict[str, RateLimitAlgorithm] = {}
        self._lock = threading.Lock()
    def allow(self, client_id: str) -> bool:
        algo = self._clients.get(client_id)
        if algo is None:
            with self._lock:                                  # create one per client, exactly once
                algo = self._clients.setdefault(client_id, self._factory())
        return algo.allow()

limiter = RateLimiter(lambda: TokenBucket(capacity=10, rate=5))
AlgorithmProsCons
Fixed window counterSimplest, O(1)A burst of 2× the limit at window edges
Sliding window logExactMemory grows with the limit
Sliding window counterApproximates smoothly, O(1)Approximate
Token bucketAllows bursts, O(1), the industry defaultTwo parameters to tune
Leaky bucketSmooths output to a constant rateAdds queueing latency

Follow-ups: distributed limits (Redis with a Lua script, or INCR + EXPIRE, atomically), evicting idle clients (TTL or an LRU), per-endpoint rules (Chain of Responsibility), returning Retry-After headers.

Interview check · 2 questions
Token bucket vs sliding window: which would you choose?
A token bucket for APIs: O(1) state, it tolerates short bursts up to capacity, and it enforces an average rate. A sliding window log when you need an exact "no more than N in any window" guarantee and N is small. A sliding window counter as an O(1) approximation for a distributed setup with Redis.
Coding Design a hit counter: hit(t), and count(t) returning the hits in the past 300 seconds, with constant memory.
class HitCounter:
    WINDOW = 300
    def __init__(self):
        self.times = [0] * self.WINDOW          # a ring buffer of size 300 (one bucket per second)
        self.hits = [0] * self.WINDOW
    def hit(self, t: int) -> None:
        i = t % self.WINDOW
        if self.times[i] != t:                  # the bucket holds an old second → reset it
            self.times[i], self.hits[i] = t, 0
        self.hits[i] += 1
    def count(self, t: int) -> int:
        return sum(h for ts, h in zip(self.times, self.hits) if t - ts < self.WINDOW)

hc = HitCounter(); hc.hit(1); hc.hit(2); hc.hit(300)
assert hc.count(300) == 3 and hc.count(301) == 2
O(1) hit, O(window) count, and memory that doesn't grow with traffic. Under concurrency, add a lock, or shard counters per thread and sum them.

2. Vending machine (State pattern)

class VendingState(ABC):
    def insert(self, m: "VendingMachine", amount: int): raise VendingError("cannot insert now")
    def select(self, m: "VendingMachine", code: str):   raise VendingError("insert money first")
    def cancel(self, m: "VendingMachine") -> int:       return 0

class Idle(VendingState):
    def insert(self, m, amount):
        m.balance += amount; m.state = HasMoney()

class HasMoney(VendingState):
    def insert(self, m, amount): m.balance += amount
    def select(self, m, code):
        item = m.inventory.get(code)
        if item is None or item.qty == 0: raise VendingError("sold out")
        if m.balance < item.price: raise VendingError(f"need {item.price - m.balance} more")
        item.qty -= 1
        change, m.balance = m.balance - item.price, 0
        m.state = Idle()
        return item.name, change
    def cancel(self, m):
        refund, m.balance = m.balance, 0
        m.state = Idle()
        return refund

@dataclass
class Item: name: str; price: int; qty: int

class VendingMachine:
    def __init__(self, inventory: dict[str, Item]):
        self.inventory, self.balance, self.state = inventory, 0, Idle()
        self._lock = threading.Lock()
    def insert(self, amount: int):
        with self._lock: self.state.insert(self, amount)
    def select(self, code: str):
        with self._lock: return self.state.select(self, code)
    def cancel(self) -> int:
        with self._lock: return self.state.cancel(self)

Follow-ups: a coin/change inventory (greedy change-making, which fails for some coin systems, so then use DP), a Maintenance state, payment strategies (cash, card, UPI), an Observer to alert on low stock.

3. Elevator system (Strategy for dispatching, the LOOK algorithm)

class Direction(Enum): UP = 1; DOWN = -1; IDLE = 0

class Elevator:
    def __init__(self, eid: int, floor: int = 0):
        self.id, self.floor, self.direction = eid, floor, Direction.IDLE
        self.up: list[int] = []        # min-heap of floors above
        self.down: list[int] = []      # max-heap (negated) of floors below
    def add_stop(self, f: int):
        if f > self.floor and f not in self.up: heapq.heappush(self.up, f)
        elif f < self.floor and -f not in self.down: heapq.heappush(self.down, -f)
    def step(self) -> None:
        """LOOK: keep going in the current direction while it has stops, then reverse."""
        if self.direction in (Direction.UP, Direction.IDLE) and self.up:
            self.direction = Direction.UP
            self.floor += 1
            if self.up[0] == self.floor: heapq.heappop(self.up)       # arrived: open doors
        elif self.down:
            self.direction = Direction.DOWN
            self.floor -= 1
            if -self.down[0] == self.floor: heapq.heappop(self.down)
        elif self.up:
            self.direction = Direction.UP
        else:
            self.direction = Direction.IDLE

class DispatchStrategy(Protocol):
    def choose(self, elevators: list[Elevator], floor: int, d: Direction) -> Elevator: ...

class NearestSuitable:
    def choose(self, elevators, floor, d):
        def cost(e: Elevator):
            moving_toward = (e.direction == Direction.UP and d == Direction.UP and e.floor <= floor) or \
                            (e.direction == Direction.DOWN and d == Direction.DOWN and e.floor >= floor)
            if e.direction == Direction.IDLE or moving_toward: return abs(e.floor - floor)
            return abs(e.floor - floor) + 100        # penalty: it would have to turn around first
        return min(elevators, key=cost)

class ElevatorController:
    def __init__(self, n: int, strategy: DispatchStrategy):
        self.elevators, self.strategy = [Elevator(i) for i in range(n)], strategy
    def request(self, floor: int, d: Direction) -> int:          # a hall call
        e = self.strategy.choose(self.elevators, floor, d); e.add_stop(floor); return e.id
    def press(self, eid: int, floor: int): self.elevators[eid].add_stop(floor)   # a car call
    def tick(self):
        for e in self.elevators: e.step()

Discuss: FCFS vs SCAN vs LOOK (minimizes travel and avoids starvation), the State pattern for doors (Open, Closed, Moving, Maintenance), capacity and weight limits, emergency mode (Command/priority), and running each elevator in its own thread with a request queue.

4. Splitwise (Strategy for splits + greedy debt simplification)

class SplitStrategy(Protocol):
    def shares(self, amount: Decimal, users: list[str], meta: dict) -> dict[str, Decimal]: ...

from decimal import Decimal, ROUND_DOWN

class EqualSplit:
    def shares(self, amount, users, meta):
        base = (amount / len(users)).quantize(Decimal("0.01"), rounding=ROUND_DOWN)
        out = {u: base for u in users}
        out[users[0]] += amount - base * len(users)       # the first user absorbs the rounding remainder
        return out

class ExactSplit:
    def shares(self, amount, users, meta):
        if sum(meta["amounts"].values()) != amount: raise ValueError("exact amounts must sum to total")
        return dict(meta["amounts"])

class PercentSplit:
    def shares(self, amount, users, meta):
        if sum(meta["percents"].values()) != 100: raise ValueError("percents must sum to 100")
        return {u: amount * Decimal(p) / 100 for u, p in meta["percents"].items()}

class Ledger:
    def __init__(self): self.net: dict[str, Decimal] = defaultdict(Decimal)   # +ve: is owed, -ve: owes
    def add_expense(self, payer: str, amount: Decimal, users: list[str], split: SplitStrategy, **meta):
        for user, share in split.shares(amount, users, meta).items():
            self.net[user] -= share
        self.net[payer] += amount

    def simplify(self) -> list[tuple[str, str, Decimal]]:
        """Greedy: match the biggest debtor with the biggest creditor. At most n-1 transfers."""
        creditors = [(-v, u) for u, v in self.net.items() if v > 0]   # max-heaps via negation
        debtors   = [(v, u) for u, v in self.net.items() if v < 0]
        heapq.heapify(creditors); heapq.heapify(debtors)
        txns = []
        while creditors and debtors:
            c_amt, c = heapq.heappop(creditors); d_amt, d = heapq.heappop(debtors)
            pay = min(-c_amt, -d_amt)
            txns.append((d, c, pay))
            if (rest := -c_amt - pay) > 0: heapq.heappush(creditors, (-rest, c))
            if (rest := -d_amt - pay) > 0: heapq.heappush(debtors, (-rest, d))
        return txns

Discuss: groups, Decimal for money with a remainder policy, the fact that minimizing the number of transactions exactly is NP-hard (greedy is the standard answer), keeping an expense history as immutable events (supports audit and undo), and notifications through an Observer.

5. Movie or seat booking (concurrency: temporary seat locks)

Key insight: two users must not book the same seat. Use a temporary hold with a TTL during payment, then confirm. Booking is all-or-nothing across the selected seats.

class SeatUnavailable(Exception): ...

class SeatLockService:
    def __init__(self, ttl: float = 300, clock=time.monotonic):
        self.ttl, self.clock = ttl, clock
        self._holds: dict[tuple[str, str], tuple[str, float]] = {}   # (show, seat) → (user, expires_at)
        self._booked: set[tuple[str, str]] = set()
        self._lock = threading.Lock()

    def _held_by_other(self, key, user, now) -> bool:
        h = self._holds.get(key)
        return h is not None and h[0] != user and h[1] > now          # expired holds count as free

    def hold(self, show: str, seats: list[str], user: str) -> None:
        with self._lock:
            now = self.clock()
            keys = [(show, s) for s in seats]
            bad = [k[1] for k in keys if k in self._booked or self._held_by_other(k, user, now)]
            if bad: raise SeatUnavailable(bad)                       # check ALL seats first, then take all
            for k in keys: self._holds[k] = (user, now + self.ttl)

    def confirm(self, show: str, seats: list[str], user: str) -> None:   # called after payment succeeds
        with self._lock:
            now = self.clock()
            keys = [(show, s) for s in seats]
            for k in keys:
                h = self._holds.get(k)
                if h is None or h[0] != user or h[1] <= now:
                    raise SeatUnavailable(f"hold expired for {k[1]}")
            for k in keys:
                del self._holds[k]; self._booked.add(k)

    def release(self, show: str, seats: list[str], user: str) -> None:
        with self._lock:
            for s in seats:
                if self._holds.get((show, s), ("",))[0] == user: del self._holds[(show, s)]

Discuss: in production this lives in a DB or Redis: SET seat:{show}:{id} user NX EX 300 per seat (a Lua script makes the multi-seat hold atomic), or DB row locks with SELECT … FOR UPDATE, or optimistic locking with a version column. Payment is idempotent (an idempotency key), and expired holds are released by TTL. Entities: Movie, Theatre, Screen, Show, Seat, Booking, Payment.

Interview check · 1 question
How do you prevent double booking under concurrency?
Make "check availability + claim" a single atomic operation. In one process, hold a lock across both steps. Across servers, use an atomic conditional write: UPDATE … WHERE status='FREE' and check the affected row count, a DB unique constraint on (show, seat), SELECT … FOR UPDATE, or Redis SET NX EX. Add TTL holds for the payment window and idempotency keys for retries.

Interview check · 6 questions
Coding Implement an LRU cache with O(1) get and put, without OrderedDict.
class _Node:
    __slots__ = ("key", "val", "prev", "next")
    def __init__(self, key=None, val=None):
        self.key, self.val, self.prev, self.next = key, val, None, None

class LRUCache:
    def __init__(self, capacity: int):
        self.cap, self.map = capacity, {}
        self.head, self.tail = _Node(), _Node()          # sentinels: no None checks needed
        self.head.next, self.tail.prev = self.tail, self.head

    def _unlink(self, n):
        n.prev.next, n.next.prev = n.next, n.prev
    def _push_front(self, n):
        n.prev, n.next = self.head, self.head.next
        self.head.next.prev = n
        self.head.next = n

    def get(self, key):
        n = self.map.get(key)
        if n is None: return -1
        self._unlink(n); self._push_front(n)             # mark as most recently used
        return n.val

    def put(self, key, val):
        if (n := self.map.get(key)) is not None:
            n.val = val; self._unlink(n); self._push_front(n); return
        if len(self.map) == self.cap:
            lru = self.tail.prev                         # least recently used
            self._unlink(lru); del self.map[lru.key]
        n = _Node(key, val); self.map[key] = n; self._push_front(n)

c = LRUCache(2); c.put(1, 1); c.put(2, 2); c.get(1); c.put(3, 3)
assert (c.get(2), c.get(1), c.get(3)) == (-1, 1, 3)
Follow-ups: thread safety (one lock around get and put), TTL, LFU (frequency buckets each holding a doubly linked list).
Coding An in-memory key-value store with set/get/delete plus nested transactions (begin, commit, rollback).
_MISSING = object()

class KVStore:
    def __init__(self):
        self.data = {}
        self.tx: list[dict] = []                  # a stack of undo logs, one per open transaction

    def get(self, k, default=None): return self.data.get(k, default)

    def _record(self, k):
        if self.tx and k not in self.tx[-1]:
            self.tx[-1][k] = self.data.get(k, _MISSING)   # remember the original value once per transaction

    def set(self, k, v): self._record(k); self.data[k] = v
    def delete(self, k): self._record(k); self.data.pop(k, None)

    def begin(self): self.tx.append({})
    def rollback(self):
        if not self.tx: raise RuntimeError("no transaction")
        for k, old in self.tx.pop().items():
            if old is _MISSING: self.data.pop(k, None)
            else: self.data[k] = old
    def commit(self):
        if not self.tx: raise RuntimeError("no transaction")
        undo = self.tx.pop()
        if self.tx:                                       # nested: merge into the parent's undo log
            for k, old in undo.items(): self.tx[-1].setdefault(k, old)

s = KVStore(); s.set("a", 1); s.begin(); s.set("a", 2); s.begin(); s.delete("a"); s.rollback()
assert s.get("a") == 2; s.rollback(); assert s.get("a") == 1
An undo log costs memory proportional to the changes, not to the size of the store. Add a TTL with a heap of (expiry, key) and lazy eviction on get.
Coding A delayed task scheduler: schedule(fn, delay) runs fn after the delay, on a background thread.
import heapq, itertools, threading, time

class Scheduler:
    def __init__(self):
        self._heap, self._seq = [], itertools.count()
        self._cv = threading.Condition()
        self._running = True
        threading.Thread(target=self._loop, daemon=True).start()

    def schedule(self, fn, delay: float) -> None:
        with self._cv:
            heapq.heappush(self._heap, (time.monotonic() + delay, next(self._seq), fn))
            self._cv.notify()                 # wake the loop: the new task may be the earliest

    def _loop(self):
        while True:
            with self._cv:
                while self._running and (not self._heap or self._heap[0][0] > time.monotonic()):
                    timeout = self._heap[0][0] - time.monotonic() if self._heap else None
                    self._cv.wait(timeout)
                if not self._running: return
                _, _, fn = heapq.heappop(self._heap)
            try: fn()                         # run OUTSIDE the lock
            except Exception as e: print("task failed:", e)

    def shutdown(self):
        with self._cv: self._running = False; self._cv.notify()

out = []; s = Scheduler()
s.schedule(lambda: out.append("b"), 0.05); s.schedule(lambda: out.append("a"), 0.01)
time.sleep(0.15); s.shutdown(); assert out == ["a", "b"]
Discuss: a worker pool for long tasks, cancellation via task IDs, recurring jobs (re-push after running), and persistence for durability.
Coding LFU cache with O(1) get and put (evict the least frequently used; ties go to the least recently used).
from collections import defaultdict, OrderedDict

class LFUCache:
    def __init__(self, capacity: int):
        self.cap = capacity
        self.vals: dict = {}                                    # key → value
        self.freq: dict = {}                                    # key → use count
        self.buckets = defaultdict(OrderedDict)                 # count → keys in LRU order
        self.min_freq = 0

    def _touch(self, key):
        f = self.freq[key]
        del self.buckets[f][key]
        if not self.buckets[f]:
            del self.buckets[f]
            if self.min_freq == f: self.min_freq = f + 1
        self.freq[key] = f + 1
        self.buckets[f + 1][key] = None

    def get(self, key):
        if key not in self.vals: return -1
        self._touch(key)
        return self.vals[key]

    def put(self, key, value):
        if self.cap == 0: return
        if key in self.vals:
            self.vals[key] = value; self._touch(key); return
        if len(self.vals) == self.cap:
            old, _ = self.buckets[self.min_freq].popitem(last=False)   # LRU among the least frequent
            if not self.buckets[self.min_freq]: del self.buckets[self.min_freq]
            del self.vals[old], self.freq[old]
        self.vals[key], self.freq[key] = value, 1
        self.buckets[1][key] = None
        self.min_freq = 1                                       # a new key always has the lowest frequency

c = LFUCache(2); c.put(1, 1); c.put(2, 2); c.get(1); c.put(3, 3)   # evicts 2 (freq 1)
assert (c.get(2), c.get(3)) == (-1, 3)
c.put(4, 4)                                                    # 1 and 3 both have freq 2 → evict LRU = 1
assert (c.get(1), c.get(3), c.get(4)) == (-1, 3, 4)
Coding Autocomplete: insert words with frequencies, and return the top 3 suggestions for a prefix.
import heapq

class TrieNode:
    __slots__ = ("children", "count")
    def __init__(self): self.children, self.count = {}, 0

class Autocomplete:
    def __init__(self): self.root = TrieNode()
    def add(self, word: str, times: int = 1):
        node = self.root
        for ch in word: node = node.children.setdefault(ch, TrieNode())
        node.count += times
    def suggest(self, prefix: str, k: int = 3) -> list[str]:
        node = self.root
        for ch in prefix:
            if ch not in node.children: return []
            node = node.children[ch]
        found, stack = [], [(node, prefix)]
        while stack:                                   # iterative DFS: no recursion limit
            n, word = stack.pop()
            if n.count: found.append((n.count, word))
            stack.extend((child, word + c) for c, child in n.children.items())
        return [w for _, w in heapq.nsmallest(k, found, key=lambda t: (-t[0], t[1]))]

ac = Autocomplete()
for w, n in [("python", 5), ("pytest", 3), ("pandas", 4), ("pyspark", 3), ("py", 1)]: ac.add(w, n)
assert ac.suggest("py") == ["python", "pyspark", "pytest"] and ac.suggest("x") == []
Optimization for scale: store the top-k list at every node (updated on insert), so a query is O(len(prefix)).
Design Design a meeting-room booking system for an office.
  • Entities: Room (capacity, floor, equipment set), Employee, Booking (room, organizer, attendees, a TimeSlot value object), BookingStatus enum.
  • TimeSlot.overlaps(other): a.start < b.end and b.start < a.end.
  • RoomFinder strategy: filter by capacity and equipment, then rank by "smallest fitting room" or "nearest floor".
  • Booking under concurrency: a lock per room. Inside it, check for overlaps against that room's bookings (kept sorted, so bisect finds neighbors in O(log n)), then insert.
  • Extensions: recurring meetings (expand into instances, all-or-nothing), cancellation notifications (Observer), auto-release if nobody checks in within 10 minutes (a scheduler).

More interview questions

How would you test an LLD solution?
Inject the clock and ID generator for deterministic tests. Unit-test each strategy separately. Use parametrized tests for vehicle, split, or state combinations. Assert every invalid transition raises. Write concurrency tests where N threads race to grab the last resource and exactly one wins. Use in-memory repositories instead of mocks.
How do you scale your single-process LLD to multiple machines?
Move the state from memory into a shared store (SQL or Redis), and replace locks with atomic DB operations or distributed locks with fencing tokens. Make operations idempotent, publish domain events to a queue (Kafka, SQS) instead of calling observers in-process, and keep the domain classes unchanged behind repository interfaces (DIP pays off here).

Structural pattern matching (3.10)

def handle(event):
    match event:
        case {"type": "click", "pos": (x, y)}:        # mapping + sequence patterns, with capture
            return f"click at {x},{y}"
        case {"type": "key", "key": str(k)} if k.isalpha():   # class pattern + guard
            return f"key {k}"
        case Point(x=0, y=0):                          # class pattern by attributes
            return "origin"
        case [first, *rest]:                           # sequence pattern (not str/bytes)
            return f"list starting {first}"
        case "quit" | "exit":                          # OR pattern of literals
            return "bye"
        case _:
            return "unknown"
Trap case RED: is a capture pattern. It matches anything and binds it to the name RED. To match a constant, use a dotted name: case Color.RED:. Class patterns use isinstance plus __match_args__ for positional matching.

More interview questions

Concept "Which recent Python features have you actually used in production, and why?"
Pick 3–4 real ones with a reason each. For example: match for parsing event payloads (it replaced nested if/isinstance), TaskGroup + asyncio.timeout (structured concurrency removed orphan-task leaks), ExceptionGroup handling, PEP 695 generics for a typed repository layer, tomllib for config, and the speedups from 3.11+ (we measured about 20% lower CPU after upgrading). Specific, measured experience beats reciting a list.
Deep-dive When is match better than if/elif, and when is it worse?
It's better for destructuring structured data, meaning dict shapes, sequences, or class attributes, with captures and guards in one readable statement (parsers, command dispatch, AST walking, event routing). It's worse, or just overkill, for simple value comparisons, which a dict dispatch handles. Watch out: a bare name is a capture pattern (it matches anything). Patterns are checked in order. Class patterns rely on __match_args__. It's not faster than if/elif.
Design Plan the upgrade of a large service from Python 3.9 to 3.13.
  1. Inventory: dependencies with 3.13 wheels, removed modules (PEP 594 dead batteries, distutils), and deprecated APIs.
  2. Run the tests with -W error::DeprecationWarning on the current version, and fix the warnings first.
  3. Add a CI matrix (3.9 and 3.13), upgrade dependencies, and pin them with a lockfile.
  4. Use pyupgrade or ruff's UP rules for syntax modernization (optional).
  5. Canary deploy, comparing latency, CPU, memory, and error rates.
  6. Roll out progressively, then drop old-version support.
Mention behavior changes to check, such as locals() semantics and asyncio API changes.
Concept When is the walrus operator := good style, and when is it bad?
Good: avoiding a duplicate call or computation in a condition: while chunk := f.read(8192):, if m := pattern.match(s):, and comprehensions that filter on a computed value [y for x in xs if (y := f(x))]. Bad: cramming logic into one line, nesting it inside complex expressions, or using it where a plain assignment on the previous line is clearer. Remember that it binds in the enclosing scope, even inside a comprehension.
Concept What is a PEP? Name the ones every senior should know.
A Python Enhancement Proposal is a design document for changes to the language, stdlib, or process. The key ones: PEP 8 (style guide), PEP 20 (the Zen of Python, import this), PEP 257 (docstrings), PEP 484 (type hints), PEP 517/518/621 (packaging and pyproject.toml), PEP 572 (walrus), PEP 634 (pattern matching), PEP 703 (removing the GIL), PEP 695 (generics syntax). Knowing why a feature was added (the PEP's motivation) is a strong senior signal.

33 Interview Gotchas & Rapid-Fire Review

20 min · "what does this print?", plus 40 quick-answer questions. Use this for revision.

What does this print?

def f(x, l=[]): l.append(x); return l → f(1); print(f(2))
[1, 2]. The default list is created once, when def runs, and is shared across calls. (ch.05)
[lambda: i for i in range(3)] → call each
2 2 2. Closures look up i when called, after the loop has finished. Fix with lambda i=i: i. (ch.05)
a = [[0]*2]*2; a[0][0] = 1; print(a)
[[1, 0], [1, 0]]. Both rows are the same list object. (ch.02)
print(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3, round(2.5), -7 // 2)
False 2 -4. Binary floats, banker's rounding, and floor division toward −∞. (ch.03)
d = {1: "a", 1.0: "b", True: "c"}; print(d)
{1: 'c'}. All three keys are equal and have the same hash. The first key object is kept and the last value wins. (ch.02)
t = (1, [2]); t[1] += [3] → result?
It raises TypeError, but t becomes (1, [2, 3]). The in-place extend succeeded before the tuple item assignment failed. (ch.02)
try: return "try" finally: return "finally"
"finally". The finally block's return overrides the try's return and swallows exceptions. 3.14 warns about it. (ch.15)
x = 10; def f(): print(x); x = 1 → f()
UnboundLocalError. The assignment makes x local for the whole function. (ch.05)
class A: items = []; a, b = A(), A(); a.items.append(1); print(b.items)
[1]. A mutable class attribute is shared by every instance. (ch.11)
print(type(type), type(object), isinstance(type, object))
<class 'type'> <class 'type'> True. type is its own metaclass, and every class is an object. (ch.13)
g = (x for x in range(3)); print(list(g), list(g))
[0, 1, 2] []. Generators can be consumed only once. (ch.07)
print(bool("False"), bool([0]), bool(0.0))
True True False. A non-empty string or list is truthy. (ch.02)
for i in range(3): pass then print(i); [j for j in range(3)] then print(j)
2, then NameError. For-loop variables leak into the enclosing scope. Comprehension variables don't. (ch.05)
a = 1000; b = 1000; a is b in the REPL vs a function?
Implementation dependent. Typically False when the lines are entered separately in the REPL, and True inside one code object where the constants are shared. Never use is for numbers. (ch.02)
"abc" * 0, [1] * -1, "a" in "", "" in "a"
'' [] False True. The empty string is a substring of every string.

Rapid-fire (answer in one breath)

QuestionAnswer
Mutable vs immutable built-ins?Immutable: int, float, str, bytes, tuple, frozenset, None. Mutable: list, dict, set, bytearray
What can be a dict key?Any hashable object (stable __hash__ plus __eq__)
*args, **kwargs?Collect extra positional arguments into a tuple and keyword arguments into a dict. At a call site, the same syntax unpacks
is vs ==?Identity vs equality (__eq__)
Shallow vs deep copy?New container with shared items vs a recursive copy with a memo dict
Iterator protocol?__iter__ returns self, __next__ raises StopIteration at the end
Generator?A function with yield that returns a lazy iterator by suspending its frame
Decorator?A callable that takes a function and returns a replacement: f = deco(f)
Closure?A function plus cells capturing variables from an enclosing scope
LEGB?Local, Enclosing, Global, Built-in name resolution
GIL?One thread runs bytecode at a time. Protects refcounts. Optional in 3.13t/3.14t
Threads for CPU work?No speedup with the GIL. Use processes or C extensions
asyncio's core?A single-threaded event loop with coroutines suspended at await, using a selector for I/O
Memory management?Refcounting, a cyclic GC, and pymalloc arenas/pools/blocks
__new__ vs __init__?Create the instance vs initialize it
MRO?C3 linearization. Inspect it with Cls.__mro__
super()?Next class in the MRO of type(self), not "the parent"
Descriptor?A class attribute with __get__/__set__/__delete__. Behind methods and property
__slots__?Fixed attributes with no __dict__. Less memory
Metaclass?The class of a class. type by default
classmethod vs staticmethod?Receives cls vs receives nothing implicitly
__str__ vs __repr__?For users vs unambiguous, for developers
Context manager?__enter__/__exit__. A truthy __exit__ suppresses the exception
else on a loop?Runs if the loop finished without break
List comprehension vs generator expression?Eager list vs a lazy iterator with O(1) memory
dict ordered?Insertion order is guaranteed since 3.7
dict lookup complexity?O(1) average, O(n) worst. Open addressing
list.sort algorithm?Timsort/Powersort: stable, O(n log n), adaptive
Monkey patching?Replacing attributes at runtime. Used in tests
Duck typing?Behavior matters, not type. EAFP style
__name__ == "__main__"?True when the module is run as a script rather than imported
.pyc?Cached bytecode in __pycache__, validated by mtime or hash
Pickle risk?Unpickling can execute arbitrary code
Walrus?:= assigns inside an expression
Type hints enforced?No. Checked by static tools, or at runtime by libraries such as pydantic
Protocol?A structural interface
@dataclass(frozen=True)?Immutable and hashable; generates init/repr/eq/hash
functools.wraps?Copies the wrapped function's metadata onto the wrapper and sets __wrapped__
Patch target?Where the name is looked up, not where it is defined
Fork + threads?Deadlock risk. Use spawn or forkserver
SOLID?Single responsibility, Open/closed, Liskov substitution, Interface segregation, Dependency inversion
Composition vs aggregation?The whole owns the part's lifecycle vs the part lives independently
Strategy vs State?The client picks the algorithm vs the object switches its own state
Adapter vs Decorator vs Proxy vs Facade?Change the interface / add behavior / control access / simplify a subsystem
Factory vs Builder?Which class to create vs how to assemble one complex object
Undo/redo?Command pattern with undo and redo stacks
Prevent double booking?Atomic check-and-claim: a lock, a conditional UPDATE, or SET NX with a TTL hold
Rate limiter default?Token bucket: O(1) state, allows bursts
Entity vs value object?Has identity and is mutable vs defined by its values and immutable (frozen dataclass)

Live-coding warm-ups to practice

  • A decorator: retry with backoff, timing, or memoize. Also the async variant.
  • An LRU cache (OrderedDict, or a dict plus a doubly linked list).
  • A context manager in both class and @contextmanager form.
  • A generator pipeline: read a large file lazily, filter, aggregate.
  • Thread-safe producer/consumer with queue.Queue, and the asyncio version with asyncio.Queue.
  • A rate limiter (token bucket) and a concurrent fetcher with a Semaphore.
  • A descriptor-based validated field, and a class registry with __init_subclass__.
  • Flatten nested lists (recursive and iterative), group-by with defaultdict, top-k with heapq.
  • LLD in 45 minutes: Parking Lot, Rate Limiter, Vending Machine, Elevator, Splitwise, BookMyShow, LRU/LFU cache, Logger, Snake & Ladder, ATM. Use the recipe in ch.26, the framework in ch.27, and the practice table in ch.31.
How seniors answer State the concept in one line, explain the mechanism (what CPython does), name the trade-offs or edge cases, and finish with how you've applied it in production. For example: "We hit the GIL on a CPU-bound ETL step and moved it to a ProcessPoolExecutor with chunked inputs, which cut runtime 6×."

More interview questions

Behavioral "Tell me about the hardest production bug you've debugged in Python."
Use STAR: Situation (the system and the stakes), Task (your role), Action (hypotheses, the tools you used, such as py-spy, tracemalloc, and logs, and how you narrowed it down), Result (the metric impact plus a prevention step: a test, an alert, a lint rule). Strong stories show methodical debugging and a technical root cause, for example "a fork after threads deadlocked the logging lock" or "an unbounded lru_cache keyed on request objects". Practice 2–3 stories that link to chapters in this guide.
Behavioral "How do you review a junior's Python pull request?"
First correctness and design: does it solve the problem, are there edge cases, are errors handled, is it tested? Then readability: naming, function size, clear data flow. Then Python specifics: mutable defaults, broad except, missing with, and O(n²) patterns. Automate style with ruff, mypy, and pre-commit so reviews focus on substance. Write comments as questions with reasons, label nits as nits, and praise good choices. A senior multiplies the team, not just the code.
Behavioral "You disagree with a staff engineer's design. What do you do?"
Understand their reasoning fully first (ask about the constraints). Make your case with data: a prototype, a benchmark, or a list of risks. Write down the trade-offs. Escalate to a decision-maker only if it's still unresolved, and once a decision is made, commit to it fully ("disagree and commit"). Then set criteria for revisiting it. Interviewers look for ownership, humility, and decision-making based on evidence.
Behavioral "Tell me about a time you significantly improved performance."
Structure: baseline ("the nightly ETL took 4 h and blocked reports"), measurement ("profiled with py-spy: 70% was in row-by-row pandas apply plus N+1 DB lookups"), change ("vectorized the transforms, batched lookups into one query with an IN clause, cached reference data"), result ("down to 25 min, compute cost −60%"), safety ("compared outputs on a week of data before switching, added a perf regression test"). Interviewers check that you measured first and validated correctness.
Behavioral "Describe a design decision you made that turned out wrong. What did you learn?"
Pick a real, moderate mistake (not a catastrophe, not a fake "I work too hard"). Example: "I introduced a generic plugin framework for two exporters, and it added indirection nobody needed. We later inlined it. I learned to wait for the third use case before abstracting, and to write ADRs (architecture decision records) so trade-offs get reviewed." Show ownership, what you changed in your process, and how you fixed it.
Behavioral "How do you mentor junior developers?"
Concrete practices: pairing on real tickets, review comments that explain the why and link to resources, gradually bigger ownership (bug fix → feature → design doc), regular 1:1s with goals, writing internal guides (the team's Python style, testing patterns), and giving credit publicly. Mention a measurable outcome: "two juniors became on-call capable in 4 months". Seniors are judged on how much they improve the team.
